## AWS compute for web/services — Lambda (Node/Python), ECS Fargate, App Runner; when each; cold starts, packaging, env/secrets

### identity

- **name**: AWS compute for web/services — Lambda (Node/Python), ECS Fargate, App Runner; when each; cold starts, packaging, env/secrets
- **item_id**: aws-compute-serverless
- **cluster**: aws
- **short_definition**: AWS offers three managed compute tiers relevant to a TS/Next + Python-services stack: Lambda (event-driven, per-invocation billing, cold-start-sensitive, best for APIs behind API Gateway, queue consumers, and cron-style jobs), ECS on Fargate (long-running or always-warm containers, full control over concurrency/networking, best for Python worker services, WebSocket servers, or anything needing >15 min execution or steady load), and App Runner (the simplest path from a container image or source repo to a running HTTPS service, sitting between Lambda and Fargate on control/complexity). Next.js itself typically deploys to Vercel, not raw AWS compute; this item covers AWS-hosted Python services, background workers, and any Node/TS Lambda functions the stack needs alongside Supabase.
- **scope_in**: Lambda (Node.js and Python runtimes, packaging, layers, cold starts, concurrency), ECS Fargate (task definitions, services, scaling), App Runner (source- and image-based deploys), choosing between the three, environment variables and secrets injection into each, container image packaging basics for Fargate/App Runner/Lambda container images.
- **scope_out**: IAM roles/OIDC for these compute types are in aws-identity-iam-oidc; API Gateway/ALB routing in front of compute and VPC networking for Fargate are in aws-edge-network-cost; S3/RDS/DynamoDB/Secrets Manager as data dependencies are in aws-data-storage; Vercel's own serverless/edge runtime for the Next.js frontend is in the vercel cluster; Cloudflare Workers/Fly/Railway alternatives are in the platforms cluster.
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Default new AWS Lambda functions to the Node.js 22.x or Python 3.13 managed runtime (latest LTS-equivalent at build time), ARM64/Graviton architecture unless a native dependency forces x86, and a memory setting tuned by AWS Lambda Power Tuning rather than left at the 128 MB default.
- Use Lambda for: HTTP APIs behind API Gateway with sub-second-to-a-few-second handlers, SQS/EventBridge/S3-event consumers, and scheduled (cron-style) jobs under 15 minutes.
- Use ECS on Fargate for: Python background workers or long-running services (job runners, WebSocket servers, anything needing more than 15 minutes, steady/predictable load where per-request Lambda billing is wasteful, or workloads needing more control over networking/sidecars).
- Use App Runner for: the fastest path to a public HTTPS service from a container image or a supported source repo when you don't need Fargate's fine-grained task/service/networking control — good for a small internal API or admin tool, not for a system needing VPC-heavy integration.
- Package Lambda functions as .zip deployments for typical Node/Python handlers; use Lambda container images only when dependencies exceed the .zip size/layer limits or you need a custom runtime.
- Keep Lambda handlers stateless and idempotent; use provisioned concurrency only for latency-critical, low-traffic-variance endpoints where cold starts are unacceptable, not as a default.
- Inject configuration via environment variables for non-secret config and via Secrets Manager/Parameter Store references (resolved at cold start, cached in-process) for secrets — never bake secrets into the deployment package or container image.
- For Python services with native dependencies (numpy, pandas, ML libraries), prefer Fargate or Lambda container images over .zip layers, since layer size limits make large native wheels painful to manage.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Choose Lambda over Fargate when traffic is spiky/low-volume and cost should scale to near-zero at idle; choose Fargate when traffic is steady enough that per-request Lambda pricing exceeds a small always-on Fargate task, or when execution regularly exceeds 15 minutes.
- Choose Fargate over Lambda when the workload needs a persistent process (WebSocket server, long-lived DB connection pool, in-memory cache warmed once), or when you need >10GB memory, GPU, or more than 6 vCPU-equivalent compute per unit of work.
- Choose App Runner over Fargate when you want AWS to manage the ECS/ALB/scaling wiring for you and don't need custom VPC topology, multiple containers per task, or fine-grained autoscaling policies — App Runner trades control for simplicity.
- Prefer Supabase Edge Functions (Deno) over AWS Lambda for functions that only need to talk to Supabase Postgres/Auth/Storage and don't need AWS-specific services — avoids running two clouds for the same job.
- Prefer Vercel serverless/edge functions over AWS Lambda for anything colocated with the Next.js app (API routes, server actions) unless a hard requirement (VPC-only resource, AWS-specific SDK, long-running job) forces it onto AWS compute.
- Escalate from Lambda to Fargate/App Runner once cold-start latency becomes user-visible for Node/Python APIs and provisioned concurrency cost approaches or exceeds a small Fargate task's steady-state cost.
- Use ECS Fargate Spot for interruption-tolerant batch/worker workloads (up to ~70% cheaper); never for latency-sensitive request-serving tasks without a non-Spot fallback.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do initialize SDK clients, DB connection pools, and Secrets Manager fetches outside the Lambda handler function body so they're reused across warm invocations.
- Do set Lambda memory based on actual profiling (AWS Lambda Power Tuning or manual load testing) — memory also scales CPU/network bandwidth, so under-provisioning often costs more in duration than the memory saved.
- Do use Lambda SnapStart (Java) or provisioned concurrency (Node/Python) only after confirming cold starts are actually a measured problem for the specific function, not preemptively for every function.
- Do set explicit CPU/memory reservations and health checks on every ECS Fargate task definition and service.
- Do use `.dockerignore`/build stages to keep Lambda container images and Fargate images minimal — image size directly affects cold start and pull time.
- Don't run a Lambda function with 15-minute timeout and default 128 MB memory 'just in case' — this masks real performance problems and wastes budget on unnecessarily long executions.
- Don't put a relational DB connection pool (e.g. Postgres via SQLAlchemy/pg) directly in a high-concurrency Lambda without RDS Proxy or a pooler — Lambda's per-invocation scaling can exhaust DB connections in seconds.
- Don't use ECS Fargate for a function whose real workload pattern is a handful of requests per hour — you'll pay for idle vCPU/memory continuously versus near-zero Lambda cost.
- Don't hardcode secrets or connection strings into a Lambda's environment variables in plaintext when Secrets Manager/Parameter Store SecureString is available — use the reference and resolve at cold start.
- Don't deploy Fargate tasks without a defined `stopTimeout` and graceful shutdown handling for SIGTERM — abrupt kills during deploys drop in-flight requests.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- Lambda (Node.js/TS) via SAM/CDK-style config, placeholders only ---
# template.yaml (AWS SAM)
Resources:
  ApiFunction:
    Type: AWS::Serverless::Function
    Properties:
      FunctionName: my-app-api
      Runtime: nodejs22.x
      Architectures: [arm64]
      Handler: dist/handler.main
      MemorySize: 512   # tune with Lambda Power Tuning, not left at default
      Timeout: 10
      Environment:
        Variables:
          NODE_ENV: production
          DB_SECRET_ARN: arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-app/db-XXXXXX
      Policies:
        - AWSSecretsManagerGetSecretValuePolicy:
            SecretArn: arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-app/db-XXXXXX

# --- ECS Fargate task definition (excerpt, placeholders only) ---
# task-def.json
{
  "family": "my-app-worker",
  "cpu": "512",
  "memory": "1024",
  "networkMode": "awsvpc",
  "requiresCompatibilities": ["FARGATE"],
  "executionRoleArn": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/my-app-ecs-execution",
  "taskRoleArn": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/my-app-worker-task",
  "containerDefinitions": [
    {
      "name": "worker",
      "image": "123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/my-app-worker:latest",
      "essential": true,
      "stopTimeout": 30,
      "secrets": [
        {"name": "DATABASE_URL", "valueFrom": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789012:secret:my-app/db-XXXXXX"}
      ],
      "logConfiguration": {
        "logDriver": "awslogs",
        "options": {
          "awslogs-group": "/ecs/my-app-worker",
          "awslogs-region": "us-east-1",
          "awslogs-stream-prefix": "worker"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}

# --- App Runner (apprunner.yaml, source-based deploy) ---
version: 1.0
runtime: python313
build:
  commands:
    build:
      - pip install -r requirements.txt
run:
  runtime-version: 3.13
  command: uvicorn app.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8080
  network:
    port: 8080
  env:
    - name: ENVIRONMENT
      value: production
  # Secrets referenced via App Runner service config -> Secrets Manager ARN, not inline values.

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- Lambda cold starts (especially Python with large dependency trees, or Node with a heavy bundle) causing p99 latency spikes on low-traffic APIs — mitigated by trimming dependencies, bundling with esbuild/tree-shaking, or provisioned concurrency for the specific function.
- Lambda-to-RDS/Aurora connection exhaustion under burst concurrency because each concurrent invocation opens its own DB connection — requires RDS Proxy or a serverless-aware pooler.
- ECS Fargate task stuck in a crash-loop due to a missing/incorrect health check or a container failing to bind the expected port, silently cycling and burning cost without serving traffic.
- App Runner auto-deploy from source triggering an unreviewed production deployment on every push to the tracked branch if branch protection/approval gates aren't configured separately.
- Deployment package or container image exceeding Lambda's size limits (50MB zipped via API, 250MB unzipped including layers; 10GB for container images) discovered only at deploy time.
- Fargate task definitions without `stopTimeout`/SIGTERM handling causing dropped requests and incomplete work during every rolling deploy.
- Lambda function timeout set equal to or greater than a downstream API Gateway integration timeout (29s max for REST APIs), causing silent 504s that look like Lambda failures but are actually gateway-side.
- Provisioned concurrency left enabled on a function after traffic patterns change, quietly billing for reserved capacity that's no longer needed.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Generating a Lambda function with default 128 MB memory and 3-second timeout for a real production API handler without any profiling-based justification.
- Embedding `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/secrets as plaintext Lambda environment variables or baked into a container image layer instead of a Secrets Manager/Parameter Store reference.
- Opening a new database connection (or connection pool) inside the Lambda handler body on every invocation instead of at module/global scope.
- Using Lambda for a workload that needs to run longer than 15 minutes by chaining Step Functions retries as a workaround instead of moving the workload to Fargate.
- Deploying ECS services without a task role separate from the execution role (over-broad task role reused for both pulling images and running application logic).
- Choosing App Runner for a workload that needs private VPC-only resource access without first confirming App Runner's VPC connector adds acceptable latency/complexity versus just using Fargate.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- AWS SAM CLI or AWS CDK — Lambda packaging, local testing (`sam local invoke`), and IaC deploy.
- AWS Lambda Power Tuning (open-source, AWS Labs) — automated memory/cost/duration tuning.
- Amazon ECR — container registry for Fargate/App Runner/Lambda-container-image builds.
- AWS Copilot CLI — higher-level ECS Fargate deploy/ops tool for teams that don't want raw task-definition JSON.
- App Runner console/CLI (`aws apprunner`) with `apprunner.yaml` for source-based builds.
- esbuild/webpack (Node) and pip/uv with `--platform` targeting (Python) for trimming Lambda deployment package size.
- Alternative: Serverless Framework — still widely used for Lambda, though AWS SAM/CDK are the more AWS-native path for a greenfield 2026 stack.
**versions_as_of** _Version and runtime pins as of research date (labeled as_of)_:

- Lambda supported runtimes as of 2026-08-29: Node.js 22.x and Python 3.13 are current-generation managed runtimes; verify against the live supported-runtimes list before pinning, as AWS deprecates older minor versions on a rolling schedule. [uncertain: exact current default/latest minor at time of read]
- ECS Fargate platform version LATEST tracks AWS's rolling Fargate platform updates; pin to a specific platform version only if you need reproducibility guarantees beyond LATEST.
- Lambda now also offers 'Lambda Managed Instances' and 'Durable Functions' as newer 2026-era additions (long-lived execution / durable workflow orchestration) alongside classic Lambda Functions — evaluate before defaulting to classic Lambda + Step Functions for durable workflow needs [uncertain: maturity/GA status and whether these fit a greenfield 2026 build].

- item: Lambda function memory range | value: 128 MB – 10,240 MB, 1 MB increments; 1,769 MB ≈ 1 vCPU | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/gettingstarted-limits.html

- item: ECS Fargate pricing (Linux/x86, us-east-1) | value: $0.0404/vCPU-hour, $0.00444/GB-hour; ARM/Graviton ~20% cheaper ($0.0324/vCPU-hour, $0.00356/GB-hour); Fargate Spot up to 70% discount | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/pricing/

- item: Lambda ephemeral /tmp storage | value: 512 MB – 10,240 MB configurable | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/gettingstarted-limits.html
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Older Lambda runtime minor versions (e.g. Node.js 16/18, Python 3.9/3.10) are on AWS's runtime deprecation schedule — do not pin new greenfield functions to anything but the current or previous LTS-equivalent runtime; check the live deprecation dates before build. [uncertain: exact 2026 deprecation cutoffs — verify at build time]
- Lambda's classic zip-based Node.js runtime remains fully supported; container-image Lambda is not being deprecated but adds cold-start overhead versus zip for small functions — don't default to container images without a reason.
- AWS App Mesh (relevant if considering service mesh alongside Fargate) is on an end-of-support track per AWS's own announcements — avoid greenfield adoption; prefer ECS Service Connect or a simpler alternative.

### security

- **identity_model**: Every compute unit runs under its own IAM role, never shared static credentials: a Lambda function has one execution role (assumed automatically by the Lambda service) scoped to exactly the AWS APIs and secrets it needs; an ECS Fargate task has both an execution role (used by the ECS agent to pull images and write logs) and a separate task role (used by application code inside the container to call AWS APIs) — these must not be the same role; an App Runner service has an instance role for the same purpose. None of these ever receive `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` — the SDK's default credential provider chain resolves credentials automatically from the execution environment. CI deployment of any of these (updating function code, pushing images, updating task definitions/services) uses the OIDC-federated GitHub Actions role from aws-identity-iam-oidc, scoped to only the specific `lambda:UpdateFunctionCode`, `ecs:UpdateService`, or `apprunner:StartDeployment`-class actions needed.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Over-privileged Lambda execution role or ECS task role granting far more than the function/service actually calls (common when copy-pasting a broad managed policy to 'get it working').
- Secrets leaking through Lambda environment variables visible in the console/CloudFormation drift or through container image layers if a secret was ever baked in during a bad build.
- SSRF or dependency-confusion attacks against a Lambda/Fargate workload pivoting to internal AWS metadata endpoints if IMDSv1 or overly permissive network egress is left enabled.
- Unbounded Lambda concurrency during a traffic spike or retry storm exhausting downstream database connections or account-level concurrency, causing cascading failures across unrelated functions in the same account/region.
- App Runner's auto-deploy-on-push convenience becoming an unreviewed production deploy path if branch protections aren't separately enforced.
- Container images pulled from a public/untrusted registry instead of a private ECR repo with image scanning enabled.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Write one narrowly scoped IAM policy per function/service rather than reusing a single 'app-role' across many Lambda functions or ECS services.
- Use ECR image scanning (on push) and block deploys of images with critical/high vulnerabilities via CI gate.
- Set reserved/account concurrency limits on high-risk Lambda functions to prevent one function's spike from starving others in the same account.
- Use RDS Proxy (or equivalent pooling) for any Lambda function talking to RDS/Aurora at meaningful concurrency.
- Disable IMDSv1 and enforce IMDSv2 on any EC2-backed infrastructure adjacent to these services; for Fargate, use `awsvpc` networking mode with security groups scoped to only required ports.
- Require CI/CD-only deploys (no console-driven manual updates) for production Lambda/ECS/App Runner, gated by the same reviewed pipeline that manages IAM.
- Set CloudWatch alarms on Lambda throttles, error rate, and duration approaching timeout; on ECS, alarm on task restart count and unhealthy-target count.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Must generate a memory/timeout value with a stated rationale (e.g. based on expected payload/DB round-trip) rather than leaving Lambda defaults unexamined.
- Must resolve secrets via Secrets Manager/Parameter Store SDK calls or ECS task-definition `secrets` blocks — never plaintext values in `environment`/`Environment.Variables`.
- Must create a separate ECS execution role and task role, never reuse one role for both.
- Must include a health check (Lambda: none needed but log-based alarms; ECS/App Runner: explicit HTTP health check path and interval) in every generated task/service definition.
- Must not generate Lambda functions with `Timeout` >= the known API Gateway integration timeout ceiling without flagging the mismatch.
- Should default new Lambda functions to ARM64/Graviton unless a stated dependency requires x86.
- Must include `stopTimeout` and graceful-shutdown guidance in generated ECS task definitions.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- Build step that fails if the Lambda deployment package (zipped) exceeds a set size budget well below the 50MB hard limit, catching bloat before it becomes a hard failure.
- ECR image scan gate blocking deploy on critical/high CVEs.
- Staging deploy + smoke test (basic health-check curl, or a synthetic invoke) required before promoting to production for all three compute types.
- Terraform/CDK plan review specifically for any IAM role/policy change attached to compute resources.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- CloudWatch Logs for all three compute types, structured JSON logging recommended for easier querying; consider shipping to an OTel collector for a unified trace view across Lambda, Fargate, and the Next.js/Vercel frontend.
- AWS X-Ray (or OTel with the AWS Distro) for distributed tracing across Lambda → API Gateway → downstream services.
- Key alerts: Lambda error rate and throttle count, Lambda duration approaching timeout, ECS/App Runner unhealthy-target count and task restart rate, cold-start duration (Lambda `Init Duration` metric) trending upward.
- CloudWatch dashboards per environment (staging/production) rather than relying on default per-resource views.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- First surprise: Lambda provisioned concurrency left on after a traffic pattern changes, billing continuously for reserved capacity nobody is using — review provisioned concurrency settings monthly.
- Second surprise: an ECS Fargate service sized for peak load running 24/7 at that size instead of using Service Auto Scaling to scale down during low-traffic hours — Fargate has no built-in scale-to-zero, unlike Lambda.
- Third surprise: App Runner's 'Provisioned' billing for memory continues even when the container is idle (only vCPU is billed only while 'active'), which can surprise teams expecting Lambda-style full scale-to-zero economics.
- Set AWS Budgets with a service-level budget for Lambda, ECS, and App Runner separately so a runaway function/service is caught in days, not at month-end.
- For batch/worker Fargate workloads, default to Fargate Spot with a small non-Spot baseline rather than 100% on-demand Fargate.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Positive: Lambda function config specifying `MemorySize`/`Timeout` with a comment or documented rationale, and `arm64` architecture by default.
- Positive: ECS task definitions with distinct `executionRoleArn` and `taskRoleArn`, and a `secrets` block referencing Secrets Manager/SSM ARNs.
- Negative (flag): Lambda `Environment.Variables` containing values matching secret-shaped patterns (long base64/hex strings, `postgres://user:pass@` connection strings) instead of a Secrets Manager ARN reference.
- Negative (flag): ECS task definition using the same role ARN for both `executionRoleArn` and `taskRoleArn`.
- Negative (flag): Lambda function with `Timeout: 900` (max) and default `MemorySize: 128` together — signals unexamined defaults rather than tuning.
- Negative (flag): container image `FROM` a public unpinned tag (e.g. `python:latest`) rather than a pinned digest/version for production Fargate/App Runner builds.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- Lambda for spiky/event-driven work under 15 minutes; Fargate for steady/long-running Python services; App Runner for the simplest container-to-HTTPS path.
- Tune Lambda memory with profiling, not defaults; default ARM64 unless a dependency forces x86.
- Separate ECS execution role from task role; never plaintext secrets in env vars — use Secrets Manager/SSM references.
- Use RDS Proxy for any Lambda-to-RDS/Aurora path at real concurrency.
- Fargate has no built-in scale-to-zero — pair with Service Auto Scaling or accept 24/7 baseline cost; use Fargate Spot for interruption-tolerant workers.
- Watch Lambda provisioned concurrency for stale reservations; watch App Runner's always-billed memory even when 'idle'.
- New Lambda 2026-era features (Managed Instances, Durable Functions) exist alongside classic Lambda — evaluate maturity before adopting for a greenfield build.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Compute choice (Lambda/Fargate/App Runner) documented with the decision rationale, not defaulted by habit.
- Lambda memory/timeout tuned and justified; ARM64 used unless blocked.
- ECS execution role and task role are distinct, least-privilege.
- No plaintext secrets in any environment variable across all three compute types.
- Health checks and graceful shutdown (`stopTimeout`/SIGTERM handling) configured for Fargate/App Runner.
- RDS Proxy or equivalent pooling in place for any Lambda-to-relational-DB path.
- Budgets/alarms set per compute type before first production deploy.
- ECR image scanning enabled and gating CI for any container-based deploy.

### Uncertain fields

## AWS data layer — S3 (buckets, presigned, lifecycle), RDS/Aurora Postgres vs Supabase, DynamoDB fit, Secrets Manager/Parameter Store, SQS/EventBridge

### identity

- **name**: AWS data layer — S3 (buckets, presigned, lifecycle), RDS/Aurora Postgres vs Supabase, DynamoDB fit, Secrets Manager/Parameter Store, SQS/EventBridge
- **item_id**: aws-data-storage
- **cluster**: aws
- **short_definition**: The AWS data layer covers object storage (S3), relational databases (RDS/Aurora PostgreSQL), NoSQL (DynamoDB), secrets/config storage (Secrets Manager, SSM Parameter Store), and async messaging (SQS, EventBridge). In a TS/Next + Python-services + Supabase stack, Supabase Postgres is the default relational store and its own Storage covers most object-storage needs for user-facing assets — AWS's data layer is brought in deliberately: S3 for large/AWS-native object storage (backups, data-lake inputs, ML artifacts), DynamoDB for high-throughput key-value workloads Postgres isn't suited for, Secrets Manager/Parameter Store when AWS compute needs credentials Supabase's own env-var model doesn't cover, and SQS/EventBridge for decoupling AWS-hosted services.
- **scope_in**: S3 bucket configuration (block-public-access, versioning, lifecycle, presigned URLs), RDS/Aurora PostgreSQL provisioning and Serverless v2 vs Supabase decision, DynamoDB fit and on-demand vs provisioned capacity, Secrets Manager vs SSM Parameter Store choice and rotation, SQS (standard/FIFO) and EventBridge (event bus, rules, scheduler) for async/event-driven glue.
- **scope_out**: IAM roles/OIDC granting access to these resources are in aws-identity-iam-oidc; compute reading/writing this data (Lambda/Fargate/App Runner) is in aws-compute-serverless; CloudFront in front of S3, VPC networking for RDS/Aurora, and NAT egress costs are in aws-edge-network-cost; Supabase's own Postgres/Storage/Auth internals are out of scope for this AWS-cluster item.
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Default to Supabase Postgres (with its built-in Storage) as the primary datastore for a greenfield app; only stand up RDS/Aurora when a specific AWS-native workload (e.g. a Python service that must live in the same VPC as other AWS resources, or a compliance/data-residency requirement) justifies a second database.
- When RDS/Aurora is justified, default to Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible with Serverless v2 for variable workloads, or provisioned Graviton (`db.r7g`/`db.t4g` families) instances for steady, predictable load — never the legacy Aurora MySQL-compatible or non-Graviton instance families for a new build.
- Default every S3 bucket to Block Public Access enabled account-wide and per-bucket, versioning on for anything holding user data or backups, and a lifecycle rule transitioning to Infrequent Access/Glacier for objects older than a defined window.
- Use presigned URLs (short expiry, scoped to one object/action) for any client-direct upload/download to S3 — never expose long-lived credentials or a public bucket to achieve the same result.
- Default to Secrets Manager for anything that needs automatic rotation (DB credentials, API keys with a rotation Lambda) and SSM Parameter Store (SecureString, Standard tier) for static configuration/secrets that don't need rotation — Parameter Store Standard tier is free and sufficient for most greenfield config needs.
- Reach for DynamoDB only for access patterns that are genuinely key-value/single-table at high throughput (e.g. session state, feature flags at scale, event deduplication) — don't use it as a general relational substitute for Postgres.
- Default to EventBridge for AWS-service-to-AWS-service event routing (S3 events, scheduled jobs via EventBridge Scheduler) and SQS for point-to-point work-queue decoupling (task queues consumed by Fargate workers or Lambda) — use both together rather than picking one for everything.
- Enable S3 Server-Side Encryption (SSE-S3 or SSE-KMS) by default on every bucket; use SSE-KMS with a customer-managed key only when you need audit-level control over key usage.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Choose Supabase Postgres over RDS/Aurora whenever the workload doesn't have a hard AWS-native requirement — Supabase bundles Postgres + Auth + Storage + Realtime + connection pooling (PgBouncer) with less operational surface than self-managing RDS/Aurora, and avoids running two separate Postgres instances for one app.
- Choose RDS/Aurora over Supabase when: the workload must run inside a private VPC alongside other AWS compute with no public internet hop, when you need Aurora-specific features (Aurora Serverless v2's instant scaling, cross-region read replicas, Aurora I/O-Optimized for very high I/O workloads), or when contractual/compliance requirements mandate AWS as the data residency boundary.
- Choose DynamoDB over Postgres/Supabase only for access patterns with single-digit-millisecond latency at very high request volume on simple key lookups — if you find yourself needing joins, ad-hoc queries, or complex filtering, that's a signal to stay on Postgres.
- Choose Secrets Manager over Parameter Store when the secret needs automatic rotation (RDS credentials, third-party API keys with a rotation Lambda) or cross-account/cross-region replication; choose Parameter Store when the value is static config or a secret that's rotated manually/infrequently, since Parameter Store Standard tier is free versus Secrets Manager's per-secret monthly charge.
- Choose EventBridge over SQS when you need content-based routing, multiple independent consumers of the same event, or a scheduled/cron trigger (EventBridge Scheduler); choose SQS when you need a simple durable work queue with one logical consumer group, visibility timeouts, and dead-letter-queue semantics.
- Use S3 directly (not through Supabase Storage) when files are large (multi-GB), need S3-specific features (Transfer Acceleration, Requester Pays, Glacier archival), or are consumed by AWS-native services (Athena, data pipelines, ML training).
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do enable S3 Block Public Access at the account level (`aws s3control put-public-access-block`) as a first step in any new account, then only relax it per-bucket with explicit justification (e.g. a CloudFront-fronted static site using OAC, not direct bucket public access).
- Do set presigned URL expiry as short as the use case allows (minutes, not days) and scope to a single object key and single HTTP method.
- Do enable automatic rotation on Secrets Manager secrets that back RDS/Aurora credentials using AWS's provided rotation Lambda templates.
- Do use RDS Proxy in front of Aurora/RDS for any Lambda-based access pattern to avoid connection exhaustion.
- Do set a DynamoDB table to On-Demand capacity mode by default for unpredictable/new workloads; switch to Provisioned + auto-scaling only once traffic is well understood and steady enough to make the switch worthwhile.
- Do configure SQS dead-letter queues on every queue that has a consumer, with a maxReceiveCount tuned to the workload's expected transient-failure rate.
- Don't create an S3 bucket without versioning for anything holding data you can't regenerate (user uploads, backups, generated reports).
- Don't grant `s3:*` on `*` to a Lambda/ECS task role 'to make uploads work' — scope to specific bucket ARNs and specific actions (`s3:GetObject`, `s3:PutObject`).
- Don't store secrets as plaintext SSM Standard-type parameters — always use `SecureString` type even in Parameter Store.
- Don't run both Supabase Postgres and RDS/Aurora for the same logical data without a clear reason — this doubles operational and cost surface for no benefit in most greenfield apps.
- Don't use SQS FIFO queues by default 'to be safe' — they cost more and cap throughput; use Standard queues unless strict ordering/exactly-once semantics are a genuine requirement.
- Don't leave DynamoDB tables on Provisioned capacity with a manually-set high RCU/WCU 'just in case' — this bills continuously regardless of actual usage; use auto-scaling or On-Demand instead.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- S3 bucket (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "artifacts" {
  bucket = "my-app-artifacts-123456789012"
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "artifacts" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.artifacts.id
  block_public_acls       = true
  block_public_policy     = true
  ignore_public_acls      = true
  restrict_public_buckets = true
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "artifacts" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.artifacts.id
  versioning_configuration { status = "Enabled" }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration" "artifacts" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.artifacts.id
  rule {
    apply_server_side_encryption_by_default { sse_algorithm = "AES256" }
  }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_lifecycle_configuration" "artifacts" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.artifacts.id
  rule {
    id     = "transition-old-artifacts"
    status = "Enabled"
    transition {
      days          = 90
      storage_class = "STANDARD_IA"
    }
  }
}

# --- Presigned URL generation (Python, boto3) ---
# url = s3_client.generate_presigned_url(
#     ClientMethod="put_object",
#     Params={"Bucket": "my-app-artifacts-123456789012", "Key": "uploads/example-key"},
#     ExpiresIn=300,  # 5 minutes
# )

# --- Secrets Manager secret + rotation reference (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret" "db" {
  name = "my-app/db"
}

resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret_rotation" "db" {
  secret_id           = aws_secretsmanager_secret.db.id
  rotation_lambda_arn = "arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:123456789012:function:my-app-rotate-db-secret"
  rotation_rules { automatically_after_days = 30 }
}

# --- SQS queue with DLQ (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_sqs_queue" "dlq" {
  name = "my-app-jobs-dlq"
}

resource "aws_sqs_queue" "jobs" {
  name                       = "my-app-jobs"
  visibility_timeout_seconds = 60
  redrive_policy = jsonencode({
    deadLetterTargetArn = aws_sqs_queue.dlq.arn
    maxReceiveCount     = 5
  })
}

# --- EventBridge rule -> SQS target (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_cloudwatch_event_rule" "nightly" {
  name                = "my-app-nightly-job"
  schedule_expression = "cron(0 3 * * ? *)"
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_event_target" "nightly_to_sqs" {
  rule = aws_cloudwatch_event_rule.nightly.name
  arn  = aws_sqs_queue.jobs.arn
}

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- S3 bucket accidentally made public via a bucket policy or ACL change, discovered only after data exfiltration — mitigated by account-level Block Public Access as a backstop.
- Presigned URLs generated with excessive expiry (hours/days) or overly broad scope (whole-bucket instead of one key), effectively acting as long-lived public access.
- Aurora/RDS running out of max_connections under Lambda burst concurrency because RDS Proxy wasn't put in front of it.
- DynamoDB hot partition (a single high-traffic partition key) throttling requests even though aggregate table throughput looks fine on paper — requires partition key design review, not just capacity increases.
- Secrets Manager secret rotation Lambda failing silently, leaving credentials stale until the next manual check or an application outage surfaces it.
- SQS queue with no dead-letter queue configured, causing poison messages to retry indefinitely and starve the consumer of capacity for good messages.
- EventBridge rule pattern too broad, triggering downstream consumers on unrelated events and causing unexpected invocation volume/cost.
- Running Supabase Postgres and RDS/Aurora side by side without a clear data-ownership boundary, leading to data drift or duplicated migration/backup effort.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Generating an S3 bucket policy with `"Principal": "*"` and no condition, or leaving Block Public Access disabled without an explicit, documented reason (e.g. CloudFront OAC pattern that still doesn't need bucket-level public access anyway).
- Hardcoding database credentials or API keys as plaintext values instead of Secrets Manager/Parameter Store SecureString references.
- Provisioning a new RDS/Aurora instance as the default choice before checking whether Supabase already covers the need.
- Setting DynamoDB Provisioned capacity to a large fixed RCU/WCU value without auto-scaling or without first validating the access pattern actually needs DynamoDB over Postgres.
- Creating SQS FIFO queues by default for workloads with no ordering/exactly-once requirement.
- Skipping S3 bucket versioning on buckets holding irreplaceable data 'to save cost' before evaluating the lifecycle-based cost tradeoff.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- AWS CLI / boto3 (Python) / AWS SDK for JavaScript v3 (Node/TS) — direct S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager, SQS, EventBridge access.
- Terraform/CDK — declarative provisioning for all data-layer resources.
- AWS Database Migration Service (DMS) — if migrating data between Postgres instances (e.g. RDS to Supabase or vice versa).
- RDS Proxy — connection pooling in front of RDS/Aurora for Lambda-heavy access patterns.
- Supabase CLI / Supabase Studio — primary tooling for the default Postgres+Storage path, used alongside (not instead of) the AWS CLI when AWS resources are also in play.
- AWS Secrets Manager rotation Lambda templates (AWS-provided, per-engine) — starting point for RDS/Aurora secret rotation.
**versions_as_of** _Version and runtime pins as of research date (labeled as_of)_:

- Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible current major version tracks upstream PostgreSQL releases; verify the latest supported major/minor at provisioning time rather than pinning to a specific version here. [uncertain: exact latest Aurora PostgreSQL version as of 2026-08-29 — not confirmed via fetch]
- DynamoDB Standard and Standard-IA table classes are current; no major API-shape changes noted as of this research pass.
- boto3/AWS SDK v3 (JS) are the current-generation SDKs; avoid the legacy AWS SDK for JavaScript v2 in new Node/TS code.

**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Aurora MySQL-compatible and older non-Graviton (`db.r5`/`db.m5`) instance families remain supported but are not the greenfield default — prefer Graviton (`db.r7g`/`db.r6g`) for new provisioned Aurora PostgreSQL instances.
- AWS SDK for JavaScript v2 — superseded by v3 (modular, tree-shakeable); do not start new Node/TS projects on v2.
- DynamoDB Reserved Capacity purchasing model exists alongside On-Demand/Provisioned — evaluate only after traffic is well understood; not a greenfield default.

### security

- **identity_model**: Every data-layer resource is accessed via IAM role/policy, never embedded credentials for the AWS-native services: S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager, SQS, and EventBridge are all reached through the calling compute's execution/task role (see aws-compute-serverless) with resource-scoped policies (e.g. `s3:GetObject` on a specific bucket ARN, `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` on a specific secret ARN). RDS/Aurora supports IAM database authentication (short-lived auth tokens instead of static DB passwords) as the preferred connection method where the driver/pooler supports it; where IAM auth isn't practical, the DB password itself is stored in Secrets Manager and rotated automatically, never hardcoded. Supabase Postgres, by contrast, uses its own service-role/anon-key and connection-string model — those keys are Supabase secrets, not AWS IAM, and should be stored in the same secrets-management discipline (Secrets Manager or the CI platform's own secret store) rather than committed to source.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Publicly accessible S3 bucket or object due to a misconfigured bucket policy, ACL, or disabled Block Public Access.
- Overly broad IAM policy on a compute role granting `s3:*`/`dynamodb:*`/`secretsmanager:*` on `*` instead of specific resource ARNs.
- Secrets Manager/Parameter Store secrets readable by more principals than necessary due to an overly permissive resource policy or IAM policy.
- RDS/Aurora publicly accessible (`PubliclyAccessible: true`) when it should only be reachable from within the VPC.
- SQS queue policy allowing cross-account or public `sqs:SendMessage`/`sqs:ReceiveMessage` without a condition, enabling message injection or exfiltration.
- DynamoDB table without point-in-time recovery (PITR) enabled, risking unrecoverable data loss from an application bug or accidental delete.
- Data residency/compliance drift from running duplicate copies of the same data in both Supabase and RDS/Aurora with unclear source-of-truth ownership.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Account-level S3 Block Public Access plus per-bucket policy review in CI (policy-as-code linting) before any bucket policy change ships.
- Least-privilege IAM policies scoped to specific resource ARNs for every compute-to-data-layer access path, generated from actual usage rather than copied broad templates.
- RDS/Aurora provisioned with `PubliclyAccessible: false` and placed in private subnets, reachable only from application security groups within the VPC.
- Enable DynamoDB point-in-time recovery and S3 versioning on every table/bucket holding data that can't be regenerated.
- Automatic Secrets Manager rotation for all RDS/Aurora credentials; IAM database authentication where the client stack supports it, to eliminate long-lived DB passwords entirely.
- Explicit SQS queue policies scoping `sqs:SendMessage`/`sqs:ReceiveMessage` to specific principals (the known producer/consumer roles), not open to the account or public.
- A documented single source of truth per data domain (Supabase vs RDS/Aurora) to prevent silent duplication and drift.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Must never generate an S3 bucket policy or ACL granting public read/write without an explicit, stated CloudFront-OAC-style justification and confirmation Block Public Access is still enabled at the bucket/account level for anything not intentionally public.
- Must generate presigned URLs with a short, explicit `ExpiresIn`/expiry and scoped to one object key and one action.
- Must resolve database credentials and API keys via Secrets Manager/Parameter Store SDK calls, never plaintext literals in code or IaC.
- Must default new relational-storage requests to Supabase Postgres unless the task explicitly states an AWS-native/VPC-only requirement; must not silently provision RDS/Aurora as a default.
- Must configure a dead-letter queue whenever generating a new SQS queue with a consumer.
- Must enable DynamoDB point-in-time recovery and S3 versioning by default when generating new tables/buckets for durable data.
- Should flag (not silently resolve) any request to run parallel Postgres instances (Supabase + RDS/Aurora) for the same logical dataset.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- IaC policy scanning (checkov/tfsec-style) blocking merges that disable S3 Block Public Access, make an RDS instance publicly accessible, or open an SQS/SNS resource policy to `Principal: *`.
- Migration review gate for any schema change touching the primary Postgres store (Supabase or RDS/Aurora), separate from the general deploy pipeline.
- Secrets-scanning step blocking commits containing connection-string-shaped or API-key-shaped literals.
- A drift-detection job (`terraform plan` on a schedule) flagging any manual console change to data-layer resources.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- CloudWatch metrics for RDS/Aurora (CPU, connections, replica lag, storage), DynamoDB (throttled requests, consumed capacity), SQS (queue depth, age of oldest message, DLQ message count), and S3 (request metrics, replication lag if cross-region replication is used).
- Alert on: SQS DLQ message count > 0, DynamoDB throttled request rate above a small threshold, RDS/Aurora connection count approaching max_connections, S3 4xx/5xx error rate spikes.
- Secrets Manager rotation success/failure events surfaced as alerts, not just left in CloudTrail.
- Central log/metrics pipeline (OTel where possible) unifying AWS data-layer metrics with Supabase's own dashboard/logs for one operational view.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- First surprise: Aurora provisioned instances billed 24/7 regardless of load when Serverless v2 or right-sizing would have been cheaper for variable workloads — review instance utilization against Serverless v2 monthly.
- Second surprise: DynamoDB left on Provisioned capacity with auto-scaling misconfigured (min capacity set too high), billing for unused reserved throughput continuously — prefer On-Demand until traffic is well characterized.
- Third surprise: S3 storage class left entirely on Standard for old/rarely-accessed objects (backups, logs, historical exports) with no lifecycle rule, when Infrequent Access/Glacier would cut storage cost substantially.
- Fourth surprise: running both Supabase (with its own compute/storage billing) and RDS/Aurora in parallel 'just in case,' doubling database spend for a single logical dataset.
- Set AWS Budgets per data-layer service (RDS/Aurora, DynamoDB, S3, Secrets Manager) and a Cost Anomaly Detection monitor on the account, given the shortened ~6-month credit runway on new accounts post the 2025-07-15 Free Tier change.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Positive: S3 bucket IaC including `block_public_acls/block_public_policy/ignore_public_acls/restrict_public_buckets = true` and a versioning + lifecycle block.
- Positive: SQS queue IaC including a `redrive_policy` referencing a DLQ.
- Negative (flag): S3 bucket policy with `"Principal": "*"` and no `Condition` block.
- Negative (flag): connection-string-shaped literals (`postgres://`, `mysql://`) or `sk_live_`/`AKIA`-shaped tokens appearing directly in source, env files, or IaC variable defaults instead of a Secrets Manager/Parameter Store reference.
- Negative (flag): `aws_db_instance`/`aws_rds_cluster` resources with `publicly_accessible = true` outside a documented, deliberate exception.
- Negative (flag): DynamoDB table resource with a large fixed `read_capacity`/`write_capacity` and no `aws_appautoscaling_target` attached.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- Default to Supabase Postgres; bring in RDS/Aurora only for a stated AWS-native/VPC/compliance requirement.
- S3: Block Public Access + versioning + lifecycle + short-scoped presigned URLs as the default bucket posture.
- RDS Proxy in front of any Lambda-to-Postgres access path at real concurrency.
- DynamoDB only for genuine high-throughput key-value patterns, On-Demand by default until traffic is understood.
- Secrets Manager for anything needing rotation; Parameter Store SecureString (free Standard tier) for static config/secrets.
- SQS for point-to-point work queues (always with a DLQ); EventBridge for routed/scheduled events across AWS services.
- Never run Supabase and RDS/Aurora in parallel for the same logical dataset without a documented reason.
- Watch Aurora Serverless v2 as the default for variable-load AWS-native Postgres instead of always-on provisioned instances.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Data-layer choice (Supabase vs RDS/Aurora vs DynamoDB) documented with rationale for each new datastore.
- Every S3 bucket has Block Public Access, versioning, encryption, and a lifecycle rule.
- Every SQS queue with a consumer has a dead-letter queue configured.
- RDS/Aurora instances are not publicly accessible; IAM DB auth or Secrets-Manager-rotated credentials in use.
- No plaintext secrets/connection strings in code, env files, or IaC defaults.
- DynamoDB tables have point-in-time recovery enabled and capacity mode matches actual traffic maturity.
- Budgets/alerts configured per data-layer service before production traffic begins.
- No duplicate/parallel Postgres instances for the same logical dataset without documented justification.

## AWS edge, network, and cost — CloudFront, API Gateway vs ALB, VPC basics for Fargate/RDS, egress and NAT traps, budgets/alerts, free-tier cliffs

### identity

- **name**: AWS edge, network, and cost — CloudFront, API Gateway vs ALB, VPC basics for Fargate/RDS, egress and NAT traps, budgets/alerts, free-tier cliffs
- **item_id**: aws-edge-network-cost
- **cluster**: aws
- **short_definition**: This item covers the network and cost surface that sits around AWS compute and data services: CloudFront as the CDN/edge layer, API Gateway vs Application Load Balancer as the entry point into compute, the minimal VPC topology needed for Fargate/RDS, the well-known NAT Gateway and data-transfer cost traps, and the budgeting/alerting discipline required to avoid bill shock — especially now that AWS's Free Tier has moved to a short, credit-based model for new accounts.
- **scope_in**: CloudFront distributions and caching, API Gateway (REST/HTTP) vs ALB choice for routing into Lambda/Fargate/App Runner, minimal VPC design (public/private subnets, NAT Gateway vs NAT instance vs no-NAT patterns, VPC endpoints), egress/data-transfer cost mechanics, AWS Budgets and Cost Anomaly Detection, and the 2025 Free Tier restructuring.
- **scope_out**: IAM/OIDC for CI or workload identity is in aws-identity-iam-oidc; the compute resources themselves (Lambda/Fargate/App Runner configuration) are in aws-compute-serverless; S3/RDS/DynamoDB/Secrets Manager/SQS/EventBridge internals are in aws-data-storage; Vercel's own edge network and CDN are in the vercel cluster; Cloudflare's zero-egress R2/Workers alternative is in the platforms cluster.
- **priority**: P1

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Front any public API on AWS with either API Gateway (HTTP API, not REST API, for a new greenfield build) in front of Lambda, or an Application Load Balancer in front of Fargate/EC2 — don't run both in front of the same compute unless a specific requirement (e.g. WAF at ALB plus API Gateway's request validation) justifies it.
- Default to API Gateway's cheaper HTTP API type over the older REST API type for new Lambda-backed APIs unless a REST-API-only feature (request/response transformation, usage plans/API keys, private VPC endpoints for the API itself) is required.
- Put CloudFront in front of any public S3-hosted static assets or Next.js-adjacent AWS content, using Origin Access Control (OAC) so the S3 bucket itself stays fully private.
- Default new VPCs to a minimal two-AZ, public+private subnet layout; put Fargate tasks and RDS/Aurora instances in private subnets with no direct internet route.
- Avoid NAT Gateways entirely where possible: give private-subnet resources reachability to AWS services (S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager, ECR) via VPC Gateway/Interface Endpoints instead of routing through a NAT Gateway to the public internet — NAT Gateway data-processing charges are one of the most common unexpected AWS bills.
- When a NAT Gateway is genuinely required (e.g. outbound calls to third-party APIs from a private subnet), use exactly one NAT Gateway per AZ you need redundancy in, not one per subnet, and monitor its data-processing GB/month closely.
- Set up AWS Budgets (cost budget + usage budget) and Cost Anomaly Detection on day one of any new account, before the first resource is deployed — this is now more urgent given the shortened Free Tier credit runway.
- Enable CloudFront caching aggressively for static/cacheable content to reduce origin data-transfer cost, since CloudFront's own free tier (1TB/month) is larger and often cheaper than direct S3/EC2 data transfer out to the internet.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Choose API Gateway over ALB when the backend is Lambda and you want built-in request throttling, API keys/usage plans, or native Lambda-proxy integration without managing a load balancer.
- Choose ALB over API Gateway when the backend is Fargate/EC2, when you need WebSocket support beyond API Gateway's WebSocket API pricing model, or when you need path-based/host-based routing across many long-running services.
- Choose HTTP API over REST API (API Gateway) by default for cost (roughly a third of REST API's per-request price) unless a REST-API-exclusive feature is required.
- Choose to skip a NAT Gateway entirely when all outbound traffic from private subnets is to AWS services reachable via VPC Endpoints — only add a NAT Gateway when true public-internet egress from a private subnet is unavoidable.
- Choose CloudFront in front of API Gateway/ALB when you need edge caching, geographic distribution, or a WAF attachment point close to users; skip it for purely internal/private APIs with no public caching benefit.
- Escalate from a single NAT Gateway to one-per-AZ only once cross-AZ NAT data-processing charges or an availability requirement justifies the added ~$0.045/hour per additional NAT Gateway.
- Reach for CloudFront's newer flat-rate bundled plans instead of pure pay-as-you-go pricing when usage is predictable and the bundled allowance (e.g. free plan: 1M requests/100GB) covers a small app's real traffic — otherwise pay-as-you-go remains more flexible for variable/growing traffic.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do use VPC Gateway Endpoints (free) for S3 and DynamoDB traffic from private subnets, and Interface Endpoints (hourly + per-GB charge, but usually cheaper than NAT for steady traffic) for other AWS services like Secrets Manager and ECR.
- Do set CloudFront cache behaviors explicitly per path pattern rather than relying on default caching for API-backed content that shouldn't be cached.
- Do use AWS WAF (attached to CloudFront or ALB, not both redundantly) with at least the AWS Managed Rules baseline rule group for any public-facing API.
- Do tag every resource with a cost-allocation tag (project/environment) from day one so AWS Budgets and Cost Explorer can attribute spend accurately.
- Don't route Lambda/Fargate egress to other AWS services (S3, DynamoDB, ECR, Secrets Manager) through a NAT Gateway when a VPC Endpoint would eliminate the NAT data-processing charge entirely.
- Don't create a NAT Gateway per subnet 'for isolation' when one per AZ (shared across subnets in that AZ) achieves the same redundancy at a fraction of the cost.
- Don't leave a CloudFront distribution's default TTL at values that cache dynamic/user-specific API responses, causing stale or cross-user data leakage.
- Don't skip AWS Budgets 'until we have real traffic' — set at least a low-threshold alert immediately so a misconfiguration (e.g. an open NAT-routed loop or a runaway Lambda) is caught within hours, not at month-end.
- Don't assume the old 12-month/750-hour/1M-request free tier framing from older tutorials still applies unchanged to a brand-new account created after the 2025-07-15 change to a ~$200 6-month credit model.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- Minimal VPC with NAT-free private subnets using VPC Endpoints (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16"
}

resource "aws_subnet" "private_a" {
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = "10.0.1.0/24"
  availability_zone = "us-east-1a"
}

# Gateway endpoint: free, no NAT needed for S3/DynamoDB traffic from private subnets.
resource "aws_vpc_endpoint" "s3" {
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  service_name      = "com.amazonaws.us-east-1.s3"
  vpc_endpoint_type = "Gateway"
  route_table_ids   = [aws_route_table.private.id]
}

# Interface endpoint for Secrets Manager (hourly + per-GB, still usually cheaper than NAT).
resource "aws_vpc_endpoint" "secretsmanager" {
  vpc_id              = aws_vpc.main.id
  service_name        = "com.amazonaws.us-east-1.secretsmanager"
  vpc_endpoint_type   = "Interface"
  subnet_ids          = [aws_subnet.private_a.id]
  private_dns_enabled = true
}

# --- API Gateway HTTP API in front of Lambda (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_apigatewayv2_api" "http_api" {
  name          = "my-app-api"
  protocol_type = "HTTP"
}

resource "aws_apigatewayv2_integration" "lambda" {
  api_id                 = aws_apigatewayv2_api.http_api.id
  integration_type       = "AWS_PROXY"
  integration_uri        = "arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:123456789012:function:my-app-api"
  payload_format_version = "2.0"
}

# --- CloudFront distribution over a private S3 origin via OAC (Terraform excerpt) ---
resource "aws_cloudfront_origin_access_control" "oac" {
  name                              = "my-app-oac"
  origin_access_control_origin_type = "s3"
  signing_behavior                  = "always"
  signing_protocol                  = "sigv4"
}

# --- AWS Budgets: cost budget with alert (Terraform, placeholders only) ---
resource "aws_budgets_budget" "monthly" {
  name         = "my-app-monthly"
  budget_type  = "COST"
  limit_amount = "50"
  limit_unit   = "USD"
  time_unit    = "MONTHLY"

  notification {
    comparison_operator        = "GREATER_THAN"
    threshold                  = 80
    threshold_type             = "PERCENTAGE"
    notification_type          = "ACTUAL"
    subscriber_email_addresses = ["alerts@example.com"]
  }
}

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- NAT Gateway data-processing charges silently accumulating because private-subnet traffic to S3/DynamoDB/ECR is routed through NAT instead of a VPC Endpoint — one of the single most common 'surprise AWS bill' root causes reported across practitioner posts.
- CloudFront caching a dynamic, per-user API response due to a default cache behavior, causing one user to see another user's cached data.
- API Gateway REST API chosen by default (habit from older tutorials) when HTTP API would have cost roughly a third as much for the same traffic.
- A misconfigured route table sending all private-subnet egress through a single NAT Gateway in one AZ, creating a cross-AZ data-transfer charge and a single point of failure.
- Public S3 bucket used as a CloudFront origin without OAC, leaving the bucket directly accessible outside CloudFront despite the distribution being 'set up correctly'.
- AWS Budgets alert configured but pointed at an email address nobody monitors, or set at a threshold so high (e.g. 100% of a large limit) that it fires only after the damage is done.
- New account created after 2025-07-15 running out of its ~$200/6-month credit far faster than a team expects because architecture was designed assuming the old 12-month per-service free tier still applied.
- Cross-region or cross-AZ data transfer between compute and RDS/Aurora/S3 treated as 'free like same-region' when it in fact incurs standard inter-AZ/inter-region transfer charges.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Generating a VPC + NAT Gateway for every private subnet's outbound traffic to AWS services without first checking whether a VPC Endpoint eliminates the need entirely.
- Defaulting to API Gateway REST API for a new Lambda-backed HTTP API without considering HTTP API's lower cost.
- Creating a CloudFront distribution with an S3 origin that's still bucket-policy-public 'in case CloudFront has an issue', defeating the purpose of OAC.
- Skipping AWS Budgets/Cost Anomaly Detection setup in the initial IaC for a new account or environment.
- Setting CloudFront default TTL to a long value on a behavior that serves personalized/authenticated API responses.
- Assuming NAT Gateway hourly cost is the whole story and ignoring the per-GB data-processing charge, which usually dominates the bill for any real traffic volume.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- AWS Cost Explorer and AWS Budgets — cost visibility and alerting.
- AWS Cost Anomaly Detection — automatic anomaly alerts beyond static budget thresholds.
- AWS Trusted Advisor (cost-optimization checks, even on lower support tiers) — flags idle NAT Gateways, unattached EIPs, etc.
- VPC Reachability Analyzer — verify actual network paths (e.g. confirm a private subnet really has no internet route) before shipping.
- AWS WAF — attach to CloudFront or ALB for a managed rule baseline.
- Terraform/CDK — declarative VPC, CloudFront, API Gateway, ALB, and Budgets provisioning.
- Third-party: Infracost or similar — pre-merge cost estimation on Terraform plans, useful for catching a NAT Gateway or over-provisioned resource before it ships.
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- API Gateway REST API is not deprecated but is the legacy/costlier type versus HTTP API for new Lambda-proxy use cases — avoid defaulting to it without a REST-API-specific need.
- Classic Load Balancer (CLB) is long-superseded by ALB/NLB — never choose CLB for a greenfield build.
- NAT instances (self-managed EC2 acting as NAT) are largely superseded by managed NAT Gateway for anything beyond a cost-sensitive dev environment; don't default to NAT Gateway either when a VPC Endpoint removes the need for NAT entirely.
- The pre-2025-07-15 AWS Free Tier messaging (12 months of specific per-service allowances for new accounts) is stale for any account created after that date — treat older cost-planning blog posts and tutorials with this caveat in mind.

### security

- **identity_model**: Network- and edge-layer resources are largely reached without their own separate identity model — CloudFront, API Gateway, and ALB are public-facing entry points secured by resource policies, WAF rules, and (for API Gateway) IAM/Lambda-authorizer/JWT-authorizer configurations rather than by the caller assuming an AWS role. Where identity does matter here: CloudFront's Origin Access Control (OAC) uses a SigV4-signed request (effectively a CloudFront-service identity) to reach a private S3 origin, replacing the older Origin Access Identity (OAI) mechanism; VPC Interface Endpoints can be restricted via endpoint policies scoping which IAM principals may use them; and any CI/CD pipeline that provisions this network/edge layer (VPC, CloudFront, API Gateway, ALB, Budgets) does so via the same OIDC-federated, least-privilege GitHub Actions role established in aws-identity-iam-oidc — never a static-keyed pipeline identity.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- S3 origin behind CloudFront still directly public because OAC/bucket-policy wiring was left incomplete, letting attackers bypass CloudFront's caching/WAF entirely.
- Security groups on Fargate/RDS in the VPC left overly permissive (0.0.0.0/0 ingress on a database port) instead of scoped to specific application security groups.
- API Gateway or ALB exposed without any WAF or rate limiting, vulnerable to credential-stuffing or scraping traffic that also drives up per-request cost.
- A misconfigured route table accidentally giving a 'private' subnet a direct internet gateway route, defeating the intended network isolation for RDS/Fargate.
- Cost-based denial-of-wallet: an attacker or bot driving high request volume against a pay-per-request API Gateway/Lambda/CloudFront stack to inflate the victim's bill, not just to cause downtime.
- Budget/anomaly alerts routed to an unmonitored inbox, meaning a real cost incident goes unnoticed until the invoice.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Always pair a CloudFront-fronted S3 origin with OAC and a bucket policy that only allows the specific CloudFront distribution's service principal — verify with VPC/S3 access logs, not just the console checkmark.
- Scope every security group to the minimum required port/protocol and to specific source security groups (not CIDR ranges) wherever the source is another AWS resource in the same VPC.
- Attach AWS WAF with at least the AWS Managed Rules Core rule set and a rate-based rule to any public CloudFront distribution, API Gateway stage, or ALB.
- Use VPC Reachability Analyzer or a scripted check in CI to assert that 'private' subnets genuinely have no `0.0.0.0/0` route to an Internet Gateway.
- Set AWS Budgets with multiple thresholds (e.g. 50%, 80%, 100% of expected monthly spend) routed to a monitored alert channel (Slack/PagerDuty), not just email.
- Enable AWS Shield Standard (automatic, free) as baseline DDoS protection on CloudFront/ALB; evaluate Shield Advanced only if cost-of-attack risk is high enough to justify it.
- Rate-limit and cache aggressively at the CloudFront/API Gateway layer to blunt both security and cost impact of abusive traffic before it reaches compute/data layers.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Must default new Lambda-backed public APIs to API Gateway HTTP API, not REST API, unless a REST-API-only feature is explicitly required.
- Must wire any CloudFront-to-S3 origin through Origin Access Control with a matching bucket policy, and must not leave the origin bucket independently public.
- Must route private-subnet-to-AWS-service traffic through VPC Endpoints where available (S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager, ECR) instead of defaulting to a NAT Gateway.
- Must generate security group rules scoped to specific ports and specific source security groups/CIDRs, never `0.0.0.0/0` ingress on a database or internal-service port.
- Must include an AWS Budgets resource (with at least one alert threshold and a real notification target) in any IaC that provisions a new AWS account or environment.
- Should attach AWS WAF with the managed baseline rule group to any newly generated public CloudFront distribution, API Gateway stage, or ALB listener.
- Must flag (not silently proceed) if asked to generate a NAT Gateway without first checking whether the described traffic pattern could instead use a VPC Endpoint.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- IaC policy scanning blocking any security group with `0.0.0.0/0` ingress on a non-HTTP(S) port, or any S3-origin CloudFront distribution missing OAC.
- A cost-estimation step (e.g. Infracost) on Terraform plans flagging newly introduced NAT Gateways, oversized ALBs, or unbounded API Gateway usage plans before merge.
- A required check that every new environment's Terraform/CDK stack includes an `aws_budgets_budget` (or equivalent) resource.
- VPC Reachability Analyzer or an equivalent scripted assertion run in CI against any VPC/route-table change touching private subnets.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- VPC Flow Logs enabled on any VPC hosting RDS/Fargate, shipped to a log sink for anomaly review.
- CloudFront and API Gateway access logs (and ALB access logs) enabled and shipped to a central log pipeline, not left in default S3 buckets with no retention policy.
- AWS Cost Explorer dashboards segmented by cost-allocation tag (project/environment), reviewed weekly during early growth.
- Alert on: NAT Gateway data-processing GB/month trending upward unexpectedly, CloudFront/API Gateway 4xx/5xx rate spikes, WAF blocked-request rate spikes (possible attack), and any Budgets/Cost Anomaly Detection alert routed to a monitored channel with an on-call owner.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- First surprise (most common across practitioner reports): NAT Gateway data-processing charges from routing AWS-service traffic through NAT instead of a VPC Endpoint — this alone has produced multi-hundred-dollar unexpected line items on otherwise small accounts.
- Second surprise: choosing API Gateway REST API by habit when HTTP API would have cost roughly a third as much at the same request volume.
- Third surprise: the 2025-07-15 Free Tier restructuring means a new AWS account now has roughly a 6-month, ~$200-credit runway rather than the previously-assumed 12-month per-service free allowances that many architecture-planning blog posts and tutorials still describe — plan initial cost expectations against the credit model, not the old framing.
- Set AWS Budgets and Cost Anomaly Detection before deploying a single resource in any new account or environment; put NAT Gateway data-processing and CloudFront data-transfer-out as named line items in the budget review, since they are the two edge/network costs most likely to scale unexpectedly with real traffic.
- Prefer CloudFront's free 1TB/month + cached responses over direct S3/ALB data transfer out wherever the content is cacheable — this is usually the cheapest way to serve static/semi-static content at scale.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Positive: VPC IaC including `aws_vpc_endpoint` resources for S3/DynamoDB (Gateway type) alongside any private subnet route table.
- Positive: `aws_cloudfront_origin_access_control` paired with a bucket policy scoped to the specific distribution.
- Positive: `aws_budgets_budget` present in every environment's IaC stack.
- Negative (flag): `aws_nat_gateway` resource present without any accompanying VPC Endpoint resources in the same VPC — a strong signal of avoidable NAT cost.
- Negative (flag): security group `ingress` block with `cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]` on a database port (5432, 3306) or any non-web port.
- Negative (flag): `aws_s3_bucket_policy` granting public access on a bucket also used as a CloudFront origin.
- Negative (flag): new AWS account/environment IaC with no `aws_budgets_budget` or equivalent cost-alerting resource anywhere in the stack.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- HTTP API over REST API by default for new Lambda-backed public APIs — roughly a third of the per-request cost.
- VPC Endpoints (free for S3/DynamoDB) instead of NAT Gateway for private-subnet-to-AWS-service traffic — the single biggest avoidable AWS cost trap.
- CloudFront + OAC for any public S3 content; never leave the origin bucket independently public.
- Security groups scoped to specific ports and source security groups, never `0.0.0.0/0` on internal/database ports.
- AWS Budgets + Cost Anomaly Detection set up before the first resource deploys in any new account/environment.
- AWS Free Tier changed 2025-07-15 to a ~$200/6-month credit model for new accounts — don't plan against the old 12-month per-service framing.
- CloudFront's free 1TB/month + caching is usually cheaper than direct data transfer out for cacheable content.
- WAF managed baseline rule group on any public CloudFront/API Gateway/ALB entry point.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- API Gateway HTTP API chosen over REST API unless a specific REST-only feature is needed.
- No NAT Gateway exists without first confirming a VPC Endpoint couldn't cover the same traffic.
- Every CloudFront S3 origin uses OAC with a matching bucket policy; the origin bucket has no independent public access.
- All security groups scoped to specific ports and source security groups, no `0.0.0.0/0` on non-web ports.
- AWS Budgets and Cost Anomaly Detection configured for the account/environment before first deploy.
- WAF with managed baseline rules attached to any public CloudFront/API Gateway/ALB.
- VPC Flow Logs and CloudFront/API Gateway/ALB access logs enabled and shipped to a log sink.
- Cost-allocation tags applied to every network/edge resource for accurate Cost Explorer attribution.
- Cost assumptions for the account are built against the current 2025-07-15+ credit-based Free Tier model, not older 12-month tutorials.
