## AWS identity for apps and CI — IAM roles, OIDC from GitHub Actions, least privilege, SSO/Identity Center, no static keys

### identity

- **name**: AWS identity for apps and CI — IAM roles, OIDC from GitHub Actions, least privilege, SSO/Identity Center, no static keys
- **item_id**: aws-identity-iam-oidc
- **cluster**: aws
- **short_definition**: AWS identity is role-based: humans and workloads assume short-lived IAM roles rather than holding long-lived credentials. For a greenfield TS/Next + Python-services + Supabase stack, CI/CD (GitHub Actions) authenticates to AWS via an OIDC identity provider that trusts GitHub's token issuer and hands out temporary STS credentials scoped to one repo/branch/environment; humans get access through IAM Identity Center (SSO) with permission sets, never long-lived IAM user access keys; and service-to-service calls (Lambda/ECS to S3/DynamoDB/Secrets Manager) use the compute's attached execution/task role, never embedded credentials.
- **scope_in**: IAM roles and policies, OIDC identity provider setup for GitHub Actions, `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials`, trust-policy scoping (repo/branch/environment claims), least-privilege permission boundaries, IAM Identity Center (workforce SSO, permission sets), service-to-service identity via instance/task/execution roles, secrets-free CI patterns.
- **scope_out**: Secrets storage/rotation mechanics (Secrets Manager/Parameter Store) belong to aws-data-storage; network-level access control (security groups, VPC) belongs to aws-edge-network-cost; container/runtime packaging of the identity-bearing compute belongs to aws-compute-serverless; cross-cloud OIDC federation patterns are in multicloud-secrets-observability-cost.
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- One IAM OIDC provider per AWS account pointing at https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com with audience sts.amazonaws.com — create it once via Terraform/CDK, never by hand in console for a repeatable stack.
- One IAM role per GitHub repo + environment (e.g. my-app-gha-deploy-staging, my-app-gha-deploy-prod), each with a trust policy pinned to `token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub` for that exact repo/branch/environment — never a wildcard `repo:org/*`.
- Use `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v6` (pin to a full version, not just `@v4`) with `permissions: id-token: write` and `contents: read` at the job level, least-privilege, no `secrets: inherit`.
- Attach an IAM permission boundary to every CI-assumed role capping max privilege regardless of policy drift, in addition to a scoped inline/managed policy.
- Human console/CLI access goes through IAM Identity Center with permission sets (ReadOnly for most engineers, scoped Admin for a small break-glass group) federated from your identity provider — no IAM users with long-lived access keys, ever, including for the account root.
- Runtime AWS SDK calls (Lambda, ECS, App Runner) authenticate via the execution/task role attached to the compute resource — never inject `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` as environment variables in any Lambda, ECS task definition, or App Runner service.
- Enforce MFA and session duration limits (`MaxSessionDuration`, e.g. 1h for CI roles) on every assumable role; require external ID only for genuine third-party (non-GitHub-OIDC) cross-account trust.
- Turn on IAM Access Analyzer and CloudTrail organization trail from day one so any unused permission or unexpected `AssumeRole` is visible before it becomes an incident.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Use GitHub OIDC + IAM role assumption for all CI/CD deploys (Terraform apply, CDK deploy, container push+deploy) — this is the default and should not require deliberation.
- Use IAM Identity Center (not IAM users) whenever a human needs console or CLI access to more than one AWS account, or when >3 engineers touch the account; for a true single-developer solo account, `aws sso login` against a single Identity Center instance is still preferred over IAM user keys because it gives short-lived credentials and easy revocation.
- Use a permission boundary + SCP (if AWS Organizations is in use) rather than relying on IAM policy alone when the CI role can create other IAM resources (e.g. Terraform managing IAM) — otherwise a compromised CI run can escalate privilege.
- Skip AWS entirely for identity if the workload is 100% Supabase (Postgres + Auth + Storage) with no AWS resources — Supabase's own service-role/anon key model and its own GitHub Action apply; only bring in AWS IAM/OIDC once an AWS resource (S3, Lambda, SES, etc.) enters the stack.
- If deploying through Vercel instead of AWS compute, Vercel's own OIDC-to-AWS federation (Vercel's built-in AWS role assumption for its functions) can replace part of this pattern for read access to AWS resources from Vercel functions — evaluate before standing up a second identity path.
- Reach for cross-account roles (not a single shared account) once you have distinct staging/production AWS accounts under AWS Organizations; the GitHub Actions OIDC provider and trust policy are created per-account, and CI assumes a role in whichever account matches the deploy environment.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do scope the OIDC trust policy's `token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub` condition to `repo:my-org/my-app:environment:production` (using GitHub Environments) rather than any branch, so only an approved-environment deployment can assume the prod role.
- Do rotate/delete the IAM OIDC provider's thumbprint concern by relying on AWS's TLS-based verification (no manual thumbprint babysitting needed as of current IAM behavior), but do verify the provider URL and audience on creation.
- Do use separate CI roles per environment (staging vs production) with different trust conditions and different permission scopes.
- Do enable CloudTrail data events for `AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity` and alert on assumes from unexpected repos or outside GitHub Actions IP/agent patterns.
- Don't store `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` in GitHub repo or org secrets for any new workflow — treat any PR introducing these as a hard fail.
- Don't use a single broad `AdministratorAccess`-attached role for CI 'to keep things simple' — scope to the exact services/actions/resources the pipeline needs (e.g. `s3:PutObject` on one bucket ARN, not `s3:*` on `*`).
- Don't wildcard the trust policy's `sub` claim as `repo:my-org/*:*` — this lets any repo in the org, any branch, or any fork-triggered workflow assume the role.
- Don't create IAM users for machine identities (build servers, scripts) — every non-human caller should be a role assumed via OIDC or resource-attached role.
- Don't grant `iam:PassRole` broadly to a CI role that also has `iam:CreateRole`/`lambda:CreateFunction` — this combination is a well-known privilege-escalation path.
- Don't reuse one IAM Identity Center permission set across environments with different risk levels — separate 'prod-readonly' from 'staging-poweruser'.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- Terraform: GitHub OIDC provider + scoped deploy role (placeholders only) ---
# Create once per AWS account.
resource "aws_iam_openid_connect_provider" "github" {
  url             = "https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com"
  client_id_list  = ["sts.amazonaws.com"]
  # AWS verifies via trusted CAs; thumbprint_list can be left as AWS-managed default.
}

data "aws_iam_policy_document" "gha_trust" {
  statement {
    effect  = "Allow"
    actions = ["sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity"]
    principals {
      type        = "Federated"
      identifiers = [aws_iam_openid_connect_provider.github.arn]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud"
      values   = ["sts.amazonaws.com"]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      # Scope to one repo + one GitHub Environment (not a branch glob).
      variable = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub"
      values   = ["repo:example-org/my-app:environment:production"]
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_role" "gha_deploy_prod" {
  name                 = "my-app-gha-deploy-production"
  assume_role_policy   = data.aws_iam_policy_document.gha_trust.json
  max_session_duration = 3600
  permissions_boundary = aws_iam_policy.ci_boundary.arn
}

resource "aws_iam_role_policy" "gha_deploy_prod_scoped" {
  name = "deploy-scoped"
  role = aws_iam_role.gha_deploy_prod.id
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [
      {
        Effect   = "Allow"
        Action   = ["s3:PutObject", "s3:GetObject"]
        Resource = "arn:aws:s3:::my-app-artifacts-123456789012/*"
      },
      {
        Effect   = "Allow"
        Action   = ["lambda:UpdateFunctionCode", "lambda:PublishVersion"]
        Resource = "arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:123456789012:function:my-app-*"
      }
    ]
  })
}

# --- GitHub Actions workflow snippet ---
# .github/workflows/deploy.yml
# permissions:
#   id-token: write   # required for OIDC
#   contents: read
# jobs:
#   deploy:
#     runs-on: ubuntu-latest
#     environment: production   # gates via GitHub Environments + required reviewers
#     steps:
#       - uses: actions/checkout@v4
#       - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v6
#         with:
#           role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/my-app-gha-deploy-production
#           aws-region: us-east-1
#           role-session-name: gha-my-app-deploy
#       - run: aws sts get-caller-identity   # smoke check, no static keys anywhere

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- Wildcard trust policy (`repo:org/*` or missing `:environment:`/`:ref:` qualifier) lets any branch or fork PR in the org assume a production-capable role — classic supply-chain escalation.
- CI role granted `iam:*` or `AdministratorAccess` 'temporarily' during initial setup and never scoped down — becomes permanent over-privilege.
- Long-lived IAM user access keys checked into a secret manager or `.env` file 'until OIDC is set up', then never removed — most common finding in real audits.
- `iam:PassRole` + resource-creation permissions combined on the same CI role, enabling privilege escalation via a newly created Lambda/EC2 with a more privileged role.
- Assuming a role for too long a session (`MaxSessionDuration` at 12h) so a leaked token from a build log stays valid far longer than the job needs.
- IAM Identity Center permission sets left over-scoped (e.g. everyone gets PowerUser) because it was faster to provision during initial rollout.
- OIDC provider or role deleted/renamed during an account migration without updating GitHub's `role-to-assume` ARN, silently breaking all deploys until someone reads the run logs.
- Cross-account deploy roles missing an explicit `aws:SourceAccount`/external-ID-equivalent condition when multiple GitHub orgs or repos share one AWS account, allowing role confusion.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Generating GitHub Actions workflows that use `aws-access-key-id`/`aws-secret-access-key` inputs to `configure-aws-credentials` instead of `role-to-assume`.
- Emitting IAM policies with `"Action": "*"` or `"Resource": "*"` for a CI deploy role 'to unblock the build'.
- Creating an IAM user (`aws_iam_user` / `aws iam create-user`) for any automated or service workload.
- Trust policies with `"sub": "repo:org/*"` or no `sub` condition at all (any GitHub org's OIDC token would satisfy the federated principal).
- Embedding `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` as a plaintext env var in a Dockerfile, ECS task definition, or Lambda console/IaC config.
- Skipping `permissions: id-token: write` and then working around the resulting auth failure by falling back to static keys instead of fixing the workflow permissions block.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials (GitHub Action) — primary OIDC-to-AWS bridge for CI.
- AWS CLI v2 with `aws sso login` / `aws configure sso` — human access via IAM Identity Center, no static profile keys.
- Terraform (aws provider) or AWS CDK — declare the OIDC provider, roles, and trust policies as code; avoid console-created IAM resources for anything repeatable.
- IAM Access Analyzer — detects unused access and external/public exposure of roles and resource policies.
- AWS IAM Identity Center — workforce SSO, permission sets, integrates with an external IdP (Okta/Entra/Google) via SAML/SCIM.
- AWS CloudTrail (org trail) — audit `AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity` and `AssumeRole` events.
- Alternative/legacy: `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials` v4 (older major, still functional but lacks newer immutable-subject-claim protections) — do not pin to this for new setups.
**versions_as_of** _Version and runtime pins as of research date (labeled as_of)_:

- aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials: v6 major line (e.g. v6.2.3) is current recommended; pin an exact patch tag, not a floating `@master`. as_of 2026-08-29.
- AWS CLI v2 is current; CLI v1 is end-of-support and should not appear in new pipelines. as_of 2026-08-29.
- GitHub-hosted repos created after 2026-07-15 emit OIDC subject claims that embed org/repo IDs (not just names) to prevent subject reuse after a repo rename/delete — trust policies written against names still work but should be reviewed against the newer claim shape for repos created post-cutover. as_of 2026-08-29 [uncertain: exact claim-shape migration timeline for pre-existing repos].
**limits_quotas_pricing** _Hard limits, quotas, free-tier cliffs, and pricing units with as_of dates and primary URLs; estimates marked [uncertain]_:

- item: Max thumbprints per IAM OIDC provider | value: 5 (min 1) | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_providers_create_oidc.html
- item: Max audiences per IAM OIDC provider | value: 100 (min 1) | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_providers_create_oidc.html
- item: JWKS key cap per provider before AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity fails | value: 100 RSA keys and 100 EC keys max | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_providers_create_oidc.html
- item: IAM role session duration (assumable range) | value: 15 min to 12 hours (configurable MaxSessionDuration); recommend 1h for CI | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_providers_create_oidc.html
- item: IAM Identity Center cost | value: No additional charge for the service itself; you pay for the AWS resources accessed | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/singlesignon/latest/userguide/what-is.html
- item: AWS Free Tier structure change | value: New accounts (from 2025-07-15) get up to $200 in credits over 6 months instead of the legacy 12-month per-service free tier; account auto-closes at 6 months or when credits exhaust [uncertain: exact grandfathering rules for pre-2025-07-15 accounts] | as_of: 2026-08-29 | source: https://aws.amazon.com/free/
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- IAM user long-lived access keys — not deprecated by AWS but treated as legacy/high-risk; AWS increasingly nudges toward IAM Identity Center and OIDC federation; avoid for any new build.
- AWS CLI v1 — superseded by v2; do not install v1 in new CI images.
- configure-aws-credentials v1–v3 major lines — superseded; v6+ has the immutable-subject-claim protections for GitHub repo recycling.
- AWS SSO (old name) — fully renamed to IAM Identity Center; docs, CLI namespaces (`sso`, `identitystore`) remain for compatibility but new material should use the Identity Center name.

### security

- **identity_model**: Workload identity for CI is OIDC federation: GitHub Actions issues a short-lived JWT (issuer token.actions.githubusercontent.com, audience sts.amazonaws.com) that the runner presents to AWS STS's AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity; IAM evaluates the role's trust policy against JWT claims (chiefly `sub`, scoped to repo+environment) and issues temporary credentials (default up to the role's MaxSessionDuration) with no static secret ever created or stored. Workload identity for compute-to-AWS-service calls is the resource-attached execution/task role (Lambda execution role, ECS task role, App Runner instance role) — the SDK's default credential chain picks this up automatically via the metadata/credentials endpoint, again with no embedded keys. Human identity runs through IAM Identity Center: users/groups synced or federated from an external IdP, assigned permission sets per AWS account, authenticating interactively (browser SSO) or via `aws sso login` for CLI, always producing short-lived STS credentials cached locally. The unifying rule: every actor in the system — human, CI job, or running service — obtains AWS credentials by assuming a role for a bounded session; the account should have zero active IAM user access keys, including on the root user (root access keys should not exist at all).
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Overly broad OIDC trust policy allows any branch/fork/repo in an org (or beyond) to assume a privileged role — the single most common real-world OIDC misconfiguration.
- Compromised CI job (malicious dependency, poisoned action, PR from untrusted fork) uses the assumed role's credentials to exfiltrate data or pivot within the account during the job's session window.
- Privilege escalation via `iam:PassRole` + a resource-creation permission on the CI role, letting an attacker attach a more privileged role to a new resource they control.
- Leaked static IAM user keys (in logs, git history, or a misconfigured Lambda env var) providing indefinite access until manually rotated/revoked.
- Stale/unused IAM roles and permission sets accumulating permissions no one prunes, expanding blast radius over time.
- Root account or IAM Identity Center admin permission sets without MFA enforced.
- Cross-account role confusion when multiple GitHub orgs/repos share a single AWS account without per-repo trust-policy scoping.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Scope every OIDC trust policy `sub` condition to an exact repo + GitHub Environment (not a branch wildcard); require the deploy job to run inside a protected GitHub Environment with required reviewers for production.
- Attach a permission boundary to every CI-assumable role, independent of the inline/managed policy, capping worst-case privilege even if the policy is later widened by mistake.
- Never co-locate `iam:PassRole` with resource-creation actions on the same role unless scoped tightly to a specific resource ARN and specific target role ARN.
- Run IAM Access Analyzer continuously; alert on any newly created external-access finding or unused-access finding older than 90 days.
- Enforce MFA for all IAM Identity Center human sign-ins and for any break-glass IAM admin path; disable root access keys entirely and enable a hardware/virtual MFA device on root.
- Set short MaxSessionDuration (≤1h) on CI roles; set CloudTrail alerts on AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity calls from unexpected source repos, IP ranges outside GitHub's published runner ranges, or outside expected hours.
- Use AWS Organizations SCPs to hard-block IAM user access-key creation account-wide as a backstop against accidental reintroduction of static keys.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Must generate GitHub Actions auth via `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials` with `role-to-assume` and `permissions: id-token: write`; must never generate `aws-access-key-id`/`aws-secret-access-key` inputs.
- Must scope every generated IAM trust policy's `sub` condition to a specific `repo:org/name:environment:<env>` or `repo:org/name:ref:refs/heads/<branch>` value — never emit `repo:org/*` or an unconditioned Federated principal.
- Must never generate an `aws_iam_user` / `aws iam create-user` resource for any workload, service, or CI identity.
- Must never place `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` or `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` in Terraform variables, `.env` files, Dockerfiles, ECS task definitions, or Lambda console/IaC environment blocks.
- Must attach the narrowest IAM policy that satisfies the stated task (explicit actions and resource ARNs) rather than defaulting to managed policies like `AdministratorAccess` or `PowerUserAccess`.
- Must flag (not silently fix) any existing `iam:PassRole` + resource-creation combination found on a CI role during a review, since resolving it requires a human privilege decision.
- Should default to IAM Identity Center for any new human-access request rather than proposing an IAM user.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- `terraform plan`/`cdk diff` review required for any change touching IAM roles, policies, or the OIDC provider, with a human approval gate before apply in production.
- A policy-linting step (e.g. IAM Access Analyzer policy validation, or `checkov`/`tfsec`-style scanning) blocking merges that introduce wildcard actions/resources on CI-facing roles.
- A secret-scanning step (e.g. gitleaks/trufflehog) blocking any commit containing an `AKIA...` access-key pattern.
- A required GitHub Environment with protection rules (required reviewers, restricted branches) gating any workflow that assumes a production-scoped role.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- CloudTrail (org trail, multi-region) capturing AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity and AssumeRole calls, shipped to a central log sink for retention beyond the default 90 days.
- IAM Access Analyzer findings surfaced into a dashboard/alert channel, not just left in-console.
- Alert on: AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity from an unexpected repo/environment claim, any IAM user access-key creation event, any root-account sign-in or API call, and any policy attachment widening a CI role's permissions.
- IAM Identity Center sign-in logs and permission-set assignment changes exported to the same observability pipeline as application logs for unified audit.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- IAM, OIDC providers, and IAM Identity Center themselves are free — the cost risk is entirely indirect: over-privileged roles enabling resource sprawl (e.g. a compromised or careless CI job spinning up expensive compute) rather than direct IAM billing.
- First surprise: AWS Budgets/Cost Anomaly Detection not wired up at all in a greenfield account, so an IAM misconfiguration that enables resource creation goes unnoticed until the monthly bill.
- Second surprise: CloudTrail data-event logging (needed to actually see AssumeRole abuse) has its own per-event cost if enabled broadly across all S3/Lambda data events — scope data-event logging deliberately rather than 'log everything'.
- Third surprise: the 2025-07-15 Free Tier change to a 6-month, ~$200-credit model for new accounts means a greenfield account has a much shorter runway before real billing starts than older tutorials assume — set a Budget alert on day one, not after the first invoice.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Positive: workflow YAML containing `permissions: id-token: write` paired with `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials` and a `role-to-assume` ARN.
- Negative (flag): any `AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}`-shaped string or `aws_secret_access_key`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` literal in source, workflow files, Dockerfiles, or task definitions.
- Negative (flag): IAM policy JSON/HCL containing `"Action": "*"` or `"Resource": "*"` on a role whose name/tags indicate CI or deploy usage.
- Negative (flag): trust policy `sub` condition using a bare `repo:org/*` or missing entirely from a Federated-principal `AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity` statement.
- Negative (flag): `aws_iam_user` (Terraform) or `AWS::IAM::User` (CloudFormation/CDK) resource declarations for anything other than a documented legacy/interop exception.
- Negative (flag): `iam:PassRole` combined with `lambda:CreateFunction`, `ec2:RunInstances`, or similar resource-creation actions on the same statement/role without resource-ARN scoping.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- Default every AWS-facing GitHub Actions workflow to OIDC via `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v6` with `role-to-assume`; never static keys.
- One IAM role per repo + environment, trust policy scoped to `repo:org/name:environment:<env>`, never a wildcard.
- Human access via IAM Identity Center + permission sets; zero IAM users with access keys, including root.
- Compute identity comes from the attached execution/task role, not injected env-var credentials.
- Attach permission boundaries to CI roles as a backstop against policy drift.
- Never co-locate `iam:PassRole` with resource-creation permissions without ARN scoping.
- IAM Identity Center and OIDC providers are free; the real cost risk is unmonitored privilege enabling resource sprawl.
- 2025-07-15 Free Tier shift: new accounts get ~$200 credit over 6 months, not the old 12-month per-service free tier — budget alerts from day one.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- OIDC provider created once per account for token.actions.githubusercontent.com with audience sts.amazonaws.com.
- Every CI-assumable role's trust policy scoped to exact repo + environment/branch, no wildcards.
- Permission boundary attached to every CI role.
- No `aws_iam_user`/IAM user access keys exist anywhere in the account (including root).
- GitHub workflow has `permissions: id-token: write` and uses `role-to-assume`, pinned action version.
- IAM Identity Center set up for all human access; MFA enforced.
- IAM Access Analyzer and CloudTrail org trail enabled with alerting wired to a real channel.
- AWS Budget + anomaly alert configured before any resource is deployed, given the 6-month credit runway on new accounts.
- No `iam:PassRole` + resource-creation combination without explicit ARN scoping on any role.

### evidence

- **source_tier**: official
- **confidence**: high
- **conflicts_noted**: AWS's Free Tier model changed materially between the era most tutorials/blog posts describe (12-month per-service free tier, e.g. 1M Lambda requests, 750 RDS hours) and the current (as of 2025-07-15) credit-based model (~$200 credits over 6 months for new accounts). Most third-party 'AWS OIDC for GitHub Actions' walkthroughs predate GitHub's 2026-07-15 immutable-subject-claim change for newly created repos and IAM's TLS-based (vs thumbprint-based) OIDC provider verification, so older posts overstate thumbprint-management effort.
- **as_of**: 2026-08-29
- **review_by**: 2026-11-29
**primary_sources** _Key URLs (official docs first)_:

- https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_providers_create_oidc.html
- https://github.com/aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials
- https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/use-iam-roles-to-connect-github-actions-to-actions-in-aws/
- https://docs.aws.amazon.com/singlesignon/latest/userguide/what-is.html
- https://aws.amazon.com/free/

## Cloudflare — Workers, Pages, R2 (S3-compatible, zero egress), D1, KV, Durable Objects, Queues, Zero Trust; Wrangler; where it beats and loses to Vercel

### identity

- **name**: Cloudflare — Workers, Pages, R2 (S3-compatible, zero egress), D1, KV, Durable Objects, Queues, Zero Trust; Wrangler; where it beats and loses to Vercel
- **item_id**: cloudflare-workers-pages-storage
- **cluster**: platforms
- **short_definition**: Cloudflare's edge compute and data platform: Workers (V8-isolate compute, near-zero cold start), Pages (being superseded by Workers static assets for new projects), R2 (S3-compatible object storage with zero egress fees), D1 (serverless SQLite), KV (eventually-consistent global key-value cache), Durable Objects (strongly-consistent single-threaded stateful actors, now SQLite-backed), Queues (managed message queue), and Cloudflare One/Zero Trust (Access, Gateway, Tunnel) for securing internal apps. Deployed and configured primarily via the Wrangler CLI and wrangler.toml.
- **scope_in**: Edge-deployed TypeScript/JS compute for APIs, middleware, and Next.js (via vinext or @opennextjs/cloudflare); R2 as an S3-compatible bucket for user uploads, build artifacts, or backups where egress cost matters; D1 for small-to-medium relational workloads that want SQLite semantics at the edge; KV for read-heavy, latency-insensitive-to-staleness config/cache data; Durable Objects for coordination primitives (rate limiters, websocket rooms, per-tenant single-writer state); Queues for async job fan-out between Workers; Cloudflare Access for zero-trust gating of internal/admin surfaces sitting behind Cloudflare's edge.
- **scope_out**: Long-running stateful compute, GPU/ML training, or anything needing >5 min wall time per invocation (use a VPS, Fly.io, or a container platform instead). Not a replacement for Supabase Postgres as the system of record — D1 is for edge-local or secondary data, not the primary relational database in this stack. Not covered here in depth: Cloudflare's DNS/CDN/WAF product line, Cloudflare Images, Stream, Browser Rendering, or Workers AI (separate KB items). Zero Trust/Access is covered only briefly as a secondary concern, not as a full SASE deployment guide.
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Default to Workers with the [assets] static-assets binding for new frontend deploys, not legacy Cloudflare Pages — Cloudflare's own docs say to start new projects with Workers.
- Default to R2 for any object storage where egress volume is unpredictable or high (media, exports, backups); keep Supabase Storage only for objects tightly coupled to RLS-protected rows.
- Default to wrangler.toml (not wrangler.json) with compatibility_date pinned to a specific date, reviewed and bumped deliberately, never left to drift.
- Default to `wrangler secret put` for any runtime secret; never put secrets under [vars] in wrangler.toml, which is plaintext and often committed.
- Default to per-environment Cloudflare API tokens (staging vs production) each scoped to only the resources (Workers, R2 bucket, D1 database) that environment's CI job touches.
- Default to Durable Objects with SQLite backend (new_sqlite_classes) for new stateful actors, not the legacy KV-backed DO storage API, which Cloudflare is steering away from.
- Default to keeping Postgres (Supabase) as the source of truth; treat D1/KV/DO as edge caches or edge-local satellites unless a workload is genuinely edge-native and low-consistency-risk.
- Default to vinext for new Next.js-on-Workers projects per Cloudflare's current recommendation, but pin to @opennextjs/cloudflare instead if the app needs a feature vinext's beta doesn't yet cover — run the compatibility checker first either way.
- Default to enabling Workers Logs/observability (`[observability] enabled = true`) from day one; it is off by default in scaffolded projects.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Use Cloudflare Workers over Vercel when the workload is latency-sensitive at global edge, needs R2's zero-egress economics, or needs Durable Objects/Queues primitives Vercel doesn't offer; stay on Vercel when the team wants the most mature, battle-tested Next.js hosting (ISR, image optimization, server actions) with the least operational risk — Cloudflare's Next.js path (vinext) is explicitly in beta as of this writing.
- Use R2 over Supabase Storage when objects are large, high-egress, or need S3-API compatibility with existing tooling (backup tools, CDN pipelines); use Supabase Storage when objects need to live inside RLS policies alongside the same Postgres rows that authorize access to them.
- Use R2 over a plain VPS + self-hosted MinIO when you don't want to operate storage durability/backups yourself; a VPS wins only if data residency or air-gapped control requirements rule out any managed cloud object store.
- Use D1 when you need edge-colocated SQLite for read-mostly, per-tenant-shardable data (e.g. feature flags, small per-region caches); do not use D1 as the primary application database when Supabase Postgres already holds that data — avoid dual-writing two databases without a clear sync boundary.
- Use KV when staleness of seconds-to-a-minute is acceptable (config, feature flags, edge cache of computed values); never use KV where you need read-your-writes consistency — its 1-write/sec-per-key limit and eventual consistency will produce visible bugs under that assumption.
- Use Durable Objects when you need a single global owner for a piece of state (websocket room, in-memory rate limiter, per-resource lock) that a horizontally-scaled Worker cannot provide; do not reach for DOs as a general database — 10 GB/object and ~1,000 req/s/object are real ceilings.
- Use Queues for Worker-to-Worker async fan-out inside Cloudflare's network; prefer a hosted queue (SQS, or Supabase-adjacent tooling) or a VPS-run broker when you need cross-cloud delivery guarantees beyond Cloudflare's 100-retry/14-day-retention model.
- Use Cloudflare Access/Zero Trust to gate admin dashboards and internal tools sitting on a Cloudflare-proxied domain; for a Vercel-hosted admin panel, use Vercel's own password/SSO protection or an app-layer auth gate instead — Access only intercepts traffic that flows through Cloudflare's edge.
- Choose Vercel over Cloudflare Workers specifically for OIDC-clean CI/CD: Vercel supports scoped, short-lived deploy tokens and tighter GitHub integration; Cloudflare's CI story is a long-lived API token as a GitHub secret with no OIDC federation option — factor this into the identity-first policy decision explicitly, don't discover it during a security review.
- Stay on a plain VPS only when the workload needs persistent local disk, GPU, non-HTTP protocols, or run times far beyond Workers' 5-minute CPU ceiling — otherwise Workers' zero-ops model wins on both cost and operational load for anything HTTP-shaped.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- DO pin compatibility_date and compatibility_flags explicitly in wrangler.toml; DON'T let Wrangler infer them or leave a stale date that silently opts out of newer runtime behavior.
- DO scope each Cloudflare API token to the minimum permission set (e.g. 'Workers Scripts:Edit' + 'Account Settings:Read' for one account) and rotate on a calendar; DON'T create a Global API Key or an all-account-resources token for CI convenience.
- DO use `wrangler secret put <NAME> --env production` per environment; DON'T store secrets as GitHub Actions repo-wide secrets shared across preview and production Workers.
- DO use R2's S3-compatible API with existing S3 SDKs/tools when migrating off AWS; DON'T assume every S3 feature (e.g. some lifecycle/replication features) is at full parity — check R2's feature list before porting complex bucket policies.
- DO treat Durable Object storage transactionally (it's strongly consistent per-object); DON'T fan out writes to many DOs expecting cross-object transactions — there is no multi-object transaction primitive.
- DO set explicit `max_batch_size`/`max_batch_timeout` on Queue consumers to bound cost and latency; DON'T leave queue consumers unbounded and then be surprised by per-64KB-chunk billing on many small messages.
- DO run `npx cloudflare @opennextjs/cloudflare` or the vinext compatibility checker before migrating a Next.js app; DON'T deploy a production Next.js app cold onto vinext without checking the compatibility dashboard first — it's beta.
- DO keep D1 database IDs and R2 bucket names as CI secrets/variables, not hardcoded in wrangler.toml when the file is public; DON'T commit real account_id/database_id values in a public template repo (use placeholders and env substitution).
- DO pin `cloudflare/wrangler-action` and `actions/checkout` to a specific commit SHA in the GitHub Actions workflow; DON'T reference `@v3` or `@main` unpinned for a supply-chain-sensitive deploy job.
- DO monitor Workers CPU-ms usage against the paid plan's 30M-included budget before it becomes a billing surprise; DON'T assume 'no request limit on paid' means no cost ceiling — CPU-ms overage is metered.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
```toml
# wrangler.toml — placeholders only, no real IDs
name = "my-app-worker"
main = "src/index.ts"
compatibility_date = "2026-08-01"
compatibility_flags = ["nodejs_compat"]
account_id = "<CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID>"

[observability]
enabled = true

# Static assets (Workers-native static hosting, replaces Pages for new projects)
[assets]
directory = "./dist/client"
binding = "ASSETS"

# R2 bucket binding (S3-compatible object storage, zero egress)
[[r2_buckets]]
binding = "UPLOADS_BUCKET"
bucket_name = "<R2_BUCKET_NAME>"

# D1 database binding (serverless SQLite)
[[d1_databases]]
binding = "DB"
database_name = "<D1_DATABASE_NAME>"
database_id = "<D1_DATABASE_ID>"

# KV namespace binding (eventually-consistent global key-value)
[[kv_namespaces]]
binding = "CACHE_KV"
id = "<KV_NAMESPACE_ID>"

# Durable Object binding (strongly-consistent single-threaded actor)
[[durable_objects.bindings]]
name = "RATE_LIMITER"
class_name = "RateLimiter"

[[migrations]]
tag = "v1"
new_sqlite_classes = ["RateLimiter"]

# Queue producer/consumer bindings
[[queues.producers]]
binding = "JOB_QUEUE"
queue = "<QUEUE_NAME>"

[[queues.consumers]]
queue = "<QUEUE_NAME>"
max_batch_size = 25
max_batch_timeout = 30

# Secrets: NEVER put real values here. Use `wrangler secret put <NAME>`
# or set them per-environment in the dashboard / CI secret store.
[vars]
PUBLIC_APP_ENV = "production"
```

```yaml
# .github/workflows/deploy-worker.yml
# Auth model: Cloudflare has no GitHub OIDC federation (unlike AWS/GCP) as of
# this writing — auth is a scoped API token stored as a GitHub Actions secret.
# Mitigate the long-lived-secret risk with narrow scoping + calendar rotation,
# never with "OIDC everywhere" — that option does not exist for Cloudflare.
name: Deploy Worker

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]

permissions:
  contents: read

jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 15
    environment: production
    steps:
      - name: Checkout (pinned SHA)
        uses: actions/checkout@11bd71901bbe5b1630ceea73d27597364c9af683 # v4.2.2

      - name: Deploy via Wrangler
        uses: cloudflare/wrangler-action@f84a562284fc78278ff9052435d9526f9c718361 # v3.14.0
        with:
          apiToken: ${{ secrets.CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN }}
          accountId: ${{ secrets.CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID }}
          command: deploy
        env:
          # Any app secret needed at build time only — never Cloudflare-side
          # runtime secrets, which go through `wrangler secret put` instead.
          NODE_ENV: production
```

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- KV write-then-immediate-read from a different edge location returns stale data because KV is eventually consistent globally — code that assumes read-your-writes breaks in production only, not locally.
- Durable Object 'overloaded' errors under bursty traffic because a single object is capped near 1,000 req/s and the workload routed too much traffic to one object key instead of sharding.
- D1 query timeout (30s) or the 50-query-per-invocation free-tier cap silently truncates a batch job that worked fine in dev on the paid tier's higher 1,000-query allowance.
- R2 multipart upload or S3-API edge case fails because a client library assumes full AWS S3 feature parity that R2 doesn't implement, discovered only when a large/streamed upload runs.
- Workers CPU time limit (30s soft default on paid, 5 min hard ceiling) truncates a long synchronous computation that would have completed fine on a traditional server — this reads as an inexplicable 'silent failure' if not explicitly caught.
- A leaked long-lived Cloudflare API token (no OIDC short-lived alternative exists) sits valid until manually revoked, unlike an AWS/GCP OIDC-minted credential that expires in minutes — the blast-radius window is structurally larger.
- vinext beta gaps cause a Next.js feature (build-time image optimization, some middleware edge cases) to behave differently than on Vercel, discovered late in a migration rather than caught by the compatibility checker upfront.
- Queue message redelivery beyond the 100-retry ceiling or 14-day retention silently drops a message the consumer never successfully processed, with no automatic dead-letter unless one was explicitly configured.
- Mixing Pages and Workers static-assets deployment models on the same project mid-migration causes confusing routing/caching behavior because Cloudflare is actively converging the two products.
- wrangler.toml with an unpinned or missing compatibility_date silently picks up new runtime defaults after a Wrangler upgrade, changing Worker behavior without an explicit code change.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Treating D1 as the single source of truth alongside Supabase Postgres without a defined sync/ownership boundary — two 'primary' databases for the same entity is a recipe for drift.
- Using KV as a session store requiring strong consistency (e.g. auth token revocation) when the 1-write/sec/key and eventual-consistency model can't guarantee timely propagation.
- Granting a CI Cloudflare API token full account-level Edit scope 'to avoid future friction' instead of enumerating the specific resources it touches.
- Running a long CPU-bound job (image processing, heavy computation) synchronously inside a Worker instead of offloading to a Queue consumer or a dedicated VPS/service.
- Copy-pasting an S3 SDK integration onto R2 without checking which S3 API surface R2 actually implements, then debugging silent failures in edge-case operations.
- Deploying to Cloudflare Pages for a brand-new project in 2026 out of habit/old tutorials, when Cloudflare's own guidance now points new projects at Workers + static assets.
- Leaving `[vars]` in wrangler.toml holding what is actually a secret (API key, DB password) because it 'was easier' than wiring `wrangler secret put`.
- Sharing one Cloudflare API token across preview and production GitHub Actions environments instead of two independently-scoped, independently-revocable tokens.
- Assuming Cloudflare Access alone secures an admin panel without also enforcing app-level authorization — Access gates the edge request, not necessarily every internal API call.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- wrangler CLI (v4.x) — build, dev, deploy, secret management, tail logs
- Cloudflare dashboard — token creation, bindings inspection, R2/D1/KV browsing, Access policy config
- cloudflare/wrangler-action (GitHub Action) — CI deploy wrapper around wrangler
- @opennextjs/cloudflare — OpenNext adapter for running existing Next.js apps on Workers
- vinext — Cloudflare's newer, officially-recommended Next.js runtime adapter (beta)
- Terraform Cloudflare provider (cloudflare/cloudflare) — IaC for zones, Workers, R2 buckets, D1/KV/DO resources, Access policies
- AWS S3 SDKs (boto3, aws-sdk) pointed at R2's S3-compatible endpoint — for migration/tooling reuse
- Miniflare / `wrangler dev` — local Workers runtime emulation for KV/D1/DO/R2 during development
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Cloudflare Pages is not formally deprecated but Cloudflare's own docs now say 'Start new projects with Workers' — treat Pages as legacy/maintenance-mode for this KB's purposes and steer new work to Workers + [assets].
- Durable Objects are migrating toward SQLite-backed storage as the default/recommended backend (new_sqlite_classes); the older key-value-backed DO storage API is the legacy path — prefer SQLite-backed for new classes.
- Durable Objects SQLite storage billing begins January 2026 at D1-equivalent rates ($0.001/million rows read, $1.00/million rows written per the pricing fetch) — cost model for existing DO-heavy workloads changes at that date; re-check actual invoices after rollout.
- vinext is positioned as Cloudflare's forward-looking Next.js runtime, with @opennextjs/cloudflare explicitly relegated to a fallback for apps with a compatibility gap — expect vinext to become the sole recommended path as it exits beta; re-verify status at each review cycle.
- No evidence found of Cloudflare adding GitHub Actions OIDC federation as of this writing — re-check this specifically at each review, since it would materially change the identity_model recommendation if introduced.

### security

- **identity_model**: Cloudflare's CI/CD authentication model is a scoped API token (created via the dashboard or API, with permission groups, resource restrictions, and an optional TTL) stored as a GitHub Actions secret (CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN plus CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID) and passed to cloudflare/wrangler-action. No documentation found (across the Workers CI/CD, GitHub Actions integration, or API token creation pages) describes GitHub Actions OIDC federation to Cloudflare of the kind AWS (sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity) or GCP (Workload Identity Federation) support. As of 2026-08-29 Cloudflare API tokens are the norm and are inherently longer-lived than an OIDC-minted credential — the practical mitigation is narrow scoping (per-resource, per-environment tokens), an optional TTL/expiration on the token, and calendar-based rotation, not elimination of the long-lived-secret pattern. Treat this as a structural gap relative to the identity-first/no-long-lived-keys policy, not an oversight to code around.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- A leaked Cloudflare API token grants standing access (until manually revoked or its TTL expires) to whatever it was scoped to — R2 buckets, Workers deploy, D1/KV data — with no automatic expiry unless one was explicitly set.
- Overly broad token scope (account-level Edit instead of resource-specific) turns a single leaked CI secret into full-account compromise.
- wrangler.toml committed with real account_id/database_id/bucket names in a public or semi-public repo leaks account topology (not a secret by itself, but reconnaissance value).
- Secrets placed under [vars] instead of via `wrangler secret put` end up readable in the dashboard, in build logs, or in the repo if wrangler.toml is checked in with real values.
- R2/D1/KV data exposed via an overly permissive public binding or an r2.dev public bucket left in 'public access' mode.
- Durable Object or Worker code with an injected/unsanitized input reaching D1 SQL execution (SQL injection risk is the same as any SQLite usage) if queries are string-concatenated instead of parameterized.
- Cloudflare Access misconfiguration (overly broad email-domain or IP-range policy) exposing an internal admin app that was assumed protected.
- Unpinned GitHub Action versions (`wrangler-action@v3` instead of a pinned SHA) introduce supply-chain risk if the action's tag is ever repointed.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Scope every Cloudflare API token to the minimum permission set and specific resource (one token per environment: staging vs production), and set a TTL/expiration where the workflow tolerates scheduled re-issuance.
- Record token owner, issue date, and scope in the same credential inventory `infra-security.md` already requires for other providers; put Cloudflare tokens on the same rotation calendar as Vercel/Supabase/GitHub credentials — this is the compensating control for the missing-OIDC gap, not a workaround to skip.
- Never commit real account_id/database_id/bucket_name values; use placeholders in the repo's wrangler.toml and inject real values via CI variables or a per-environment generated config step.
- Always use `wrangler secret put` for runtime secrets; keep [vars] limited to genuinely non-secret configuration.
- Pin cloudflare/wrangler-action and actions/checkout to immutable commit SHAs, matching this repo's existing GitHub Actions pinning policy.
- Parameterize all D1 SQL (prepared statements via the D1 client API) rather than string-concatenating user input.
- Keep R2 buckets private by default; only enable public access (r2.dev or custom domain) deliberately and document why.
- Layer Cloudflare Access in front of admin/internal tools AND keep app-level authorization checks — do not rely on Access as the sole gate.
- On any suspected token leak, revoke immediately in the dashboard and reissue with the same scoping discipline — treat it identically to the 'committed then force-pushed' leak rule already in `infra-security.md`.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- MUST NOT hardcode account_id, database_id, bucket names, or namespace IDs as literal values in wrangler.toml committed to a shared/public repo — use placeholders and CI-injected or per-developer local values.
- MUST use `wrangler secret put <NAME>` for any credential/secret the Worker needs at runtime; MUST NOT write secrets under [vars] or inline in code.
- MUST pin GitHub Action versions to commit SHAs in any generated deploy workflow, not floating tags.
- MUST generate D1 queries as parameterized/prepared statements, never string-concatenated SQL.
- MUST set an explicit compatibility_date in any generated wrangler.toml, never omit it or leave a placeholder like 'latest'.
- SHOULD default new Next.js-on-Workers scaffolds to running the vinext (or @opennextjs/cloudflare) compatibility checker before assuming a feature works, and flag beta-status caveats to the human rather than silently picking the newest adapter.
- SHOULD default new static-site/frontend Worker projects to the [assets] binding pattern, not to Cloudflare Pages, unless the user's existing project is already on Pages.
- MUST NOT claim Cloudflare supports GitHub Actions OIDC federation — if asked to set up 'OIDC to Cloudflare like we do for AWS,' the agent must state this isn't available and propose scoped-token + rotation instead.
- SHOULD recommend per-environment (staging/production) API tokens and D1/R2/KV resources rather than a single shared set across environments.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- CI deploy job must fail closed if CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN or CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID secrets are missing, rather than falling back to an interactive wrangler login.
- Lint/verify wrangler.toml contains no literal account_id/database_id/bucket_name values matching a real-looking Cloudflare ID pattern before allowing a merge to a public or semi-public repo.
- Require `wrangler deploy --dry-run` (or equivalent build-only check) on PRs, with the real `wrangler deploy` gated to the merge-to-main job only.
- Pin cloudflare/wrangler-action and actions/checkout to commit SHAs; a CI policy check (matching this repo's existing Action-pinning enforcement) should reject floating tags.
- Gate production D1/R2/KV resource creation behind an explicit apply step (Terraform plan/apply or a reviewed wrangler command), never auto-applied on every push.
- Run the vinext/@opennextjs/cloudflare compatibility checker as a CI step for Next.js-on-Workers projects, failing the build on new incompatibilities rather than discovering them at runtime.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- Set a billing alert/budget on the Cloudflare account for Workers paid-plan overage (CPU-ms beyond the 30M/month included) — CPU-heavy code paths are the most common surprise line item.
- Monitor R2 Class A (write/list) operation volume specifically — it's ~12x more expensive per-operation than Class B (read) and easy to blow through with chatty list/upload patterns.
- Cap Queue consumer batch size and timeout explicitly; unbounded batching against many small (<64KB) messages multiplies billed operations.
- Avoid using Durable Objects as a high-frequency read/write store for data that KV or D1 could serve more cheaply — DO duration billing (GB-seconds) adds up under sustained active connections (e.g. long-lived websockets).
- Right-size R2 storage class: use Infrequent Access pricing tier for cold/rarely-read objects (backups, archives) rather than paying Standard rates uniformly.
- Track D1 query-per-invocation counts against the paid tier's 1,000-query ceiling before a batch job silently starts failing at scale.
- Review the free-tier boundaries (100k Workers requests/day, 100k KV reads/day, 10 D1 databases) explicitly before assuming a side project stays free indefinitely as traffic grows.
- Re-verify D1 per-row read/write pricing directly against the live pricing page before it becomes a line item in a cost model — this research pass could not confirm the exact number.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Repo contains a wrangler.toml or wrangler.jsonc — signals active Cloudflare Workers usage worth cross-referencing against this module.
- package.json depends on `wrangler`, `@opennextjs/cloudflare`, or references `vinext` — signals Next.js-on-Workers deployment, trigger the Vercel-vs-Cloudflare decision gate check.
- GitHub Actions workflow references `cloudflare/wrangler-action` — verify token scoping and SHA-pinning per this module's agent_codegen_policy.
- Environment/secrets files reference CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN or CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID — verify these are CI-secret-store values, never committed.
- Code imports an S3 SDK pointed at an `r2.cloudflarestorage.com` endpoint — signals R2 usage; check zero-egress assumptions are being exploited (not routing large downloads through a paid intermediary).
- SQL migration files or a `.wrangler/` D1 local state directory present — signals D1 usage; check query batching against invocation limits.
- Durable Object class definitions in Worker source (`extends DurableObject`) — check for SQLite-backed migration declarations (new_sqlite_classes) vs legacy KV-backed DO storage.
- References to `cloudflare-pages` build output or a `_worker.js`/`functions/` directory pattern — signals a project still on legacy Pages that could be evaluated for migration to Workers + assets.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- Cloudflare Workers: V8-isolate edge compute, 128 MB memory, CPU time capped 10ms free / up to 5 min paid — pick for latency-sensitive HTTP workloads, not long-running jobs.
- R2 is the standout: S3-compatible API, genuinely zero egress fees — default choice over Supabase Storage or raw S3 whenever egress volume is a real cost driver.
- D1 (serverless SQLite) and KV (eventually-consistent global KV) are edge-local satellites, not replacements for Supabase Postgres as the system of record.
- Durable Objects give a single-threaded, strongly-consistent actor per object — the right tool for rate limiters, websocket rooms, and per-tenant locks; not a general database (10 GB/object, ~1,000 req/s ceiling).
- Queues are Cloudflare-network-internal async messaging — fine for Worker-to-Worker fan-out, not a cross-cloud durable queue with SQS-grade guarantees.
- Cloudflare is actively steering new projects from Pages to Workers + static assets; treat Pages as legacy for new work.
- Next.js on Workers is real but not risk-free: vinext (Cloudflare's recommended path) is beta as of this writing; @opennextjs/cloudflare is the stable fallback — Vercel remains the lower-risk default for teams that want mature Next.js hosting without adapter risk.
- Critical identity gap: Cloudflare has no GitHub Actions OIDC federation — CI auth is a scoped, rotated API token, which conflicts with a strict 'OIDC everywhere, never long-lived keys' policy and must be called out explicitly rather than silently assumed solved.
- Cost model favors Cloudflare heavily for storage-egress-heavy or edge-latency-sensitive workloads; favors Vercel for teams optimizing purely for Next.js DX/maturity with lower ops risk.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- [ ] New frontend project uses Workers + [assets], not legacy Pages, unless already committed to Pages
- [ ] wrangler.toml has an explicit, reviewed compatibility_date and no real account_id/database_id/bucket_name values
- [ ] All runtime secrets set via `wrangler secret put`, none under [vars]
- [ ] Cloudflare API tokens are scoped per-resource and per-environment, with owner/date/scope recorded in the credential inventory
- [ ] GitHub Actions deploy workflow pins cloudflare/wrangler-action and actions/checkout to commit SHAs
- [ ] D1 queries are parameterized; query-per-invocation volume checked against plan limits
- [ ] R2 buckets default to private; any public access is deliberate and documented
- [ ] Durable Object classes use SQLite backend (new_sqlite_classes) for new code
- [ ] Next.js-on-Workers projects ran the vinext/@opennextjs/cloudflare compatibility checker before production deploy
- [ ] Billing alerts set for Workers CPU-ms overage and R2 Class A operation volume
- [ ] Cloudflare Access (if used) paired with app-level authorization, not relied on alone
