### Uncertain fields

## GCP compute — Cloud Run (services/jobs), Cloud Run functions, container packaging, concurrency/scale-to-zero, env/secrets, min instances

### identity

- **name**: GCP compute — Cloud Run (services/jobs), Cloud Run functions, container packaging, concurrency/scale-to-zero, env/secrets, min instances
- **item_id**: gcp-cloud-run-functions
- **cluster**: gcp
- **short_definition**: Cloud Run is Google Cloud's fully managed serverless container platform offering four workload types — Services (HTTP request/response, autoscaling, scale-to-zero), Jobs (run-to-completion parallel tasks), Worker Pools (continuous non-HTTP consumers), and Instances (long-lived singletons, preview). Cloud Run functions (formerly 2nd-gen Cloud Functions) is a source-centric, function-signature deployment mode now unified on the same Cloud Run infrastructure and control plane.
- **scope_in**: Container packaging (Dockerfile and buildpacks/source deploy), Cloud Run services and jobs configuration, concurrency and autoscaling (min/max instances, scale-to-zero, cold starts), environment variables and Secret Manager integration, IAM invoker access control, Cloud Run functions vs services tradeoffs, quotas/pricing as of 2026-08-29.
- **scope_out**: Full Workload Identity Federation setup for GitHub Actions (see gcp-identity-wif), GKE/Kubernetes, Cloud Run Worker Pools and Instances deep dive (preview features, out of scope for this P0 item), VPC networking/Serverless VPC Access internals, Cloud Build pipeline authoring details, Supabase-side configuration.
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Deploy Python services as containers via Dockerfile; use source-based buildpack deploy only for quick prototypes since it backdates file mtimes to 1980-01-01, which can break static-asset cache-busting.
- Default concurrency is 80 requests per instance; raise it for I/O-bound async Node/Python (FastAPI/Uvicorn, async Express) services once load tested, lower it toward 1 for CPU-bound or non-thread-safe workloads.
- Default CPU allocation is request-based (CPU throttled to near-zero between requests); switch to CPU-always-allocated only when running background threads/timers inside the instance.
- min-instances defaults to 0 (scale-to-zero); set min-instances=1 (or more) only for latency-sensitive user-facing services, accepting the idle billing cost.
- max-instances has a default cap even if unset; always set an explicit max-instances to bound cost and downstream DB/connection-pool load (e.g., Supabase Postgres connection limits).
- Store all secrets (Supabase service role key, DB URLs, third-party API keys) in Secret Manager and mount as env vars or volumes — never as plain --set-env-vars values.
- Do not use --allow-unauthenticated on internal/service-to-service Cloud Run services; reserve public invocation for edge-facing APIs that need it, fronted by your own auth layer.
- Use Cloud Run jobs (not services with cron-triggered endpoints) for batch/ETL work; jobs support up to 10,000 tasks with configurable parallelism and per-task retries.
- Prefer Cloud Run functions for small, single-purpose, event-triggered handlers (Pub/Sub, Storage, Eventarc); prefer Cloud Run services for multi-route APIs, custom frameworks (FastAPI/Express/Next.js), or when you need fine-grained concurrency/CPU control.
- Pin base images and buildpack builder versions explicitly in CI so builds are reproducible across GitHub Actions runs.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- DO: reference secrets via Secret Manager (--set-secrets or volume mounts) and grant the service's runtime SA roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor scoped to the specific secret.
- DON'T: put database passwords, Supabase service_role keys, or API tokens in --set-env-vars or committed YAML.
- DO: set explicit --min-instances and --max-instances on every production service; don't rely on platform defaults for cost-critical or latency-critical paths.
- DON'T: leave --allow-unauthenticated on services that only other backend services should call — use IAM invoker bindings (roles/run.invoker) scoped to specific service accounts instead.
- DO: handle SIGTERM in-app to drain in-flight requests/connections before Cloud Run kills the instance (default grace period ~10s, configurable).
- DON'T: assume container filesystem writes persist across instance restarts or across concurrent requests on the same instance — treat local disk as ephemeral scratch only.
- DO: use Cloud Run jobs (not services with `--concurrency 1` and a webhook) for long batch tasks; jobs support up to 7-day timeouts vs a service's much shorter request timeout.
- DON'T: leave concurrency at the default 80 for a Flask/sync-WSGI Python service without a production WSGI server (gunicorn with multiple workers/threads) — a single-threaded dev server under concurrency=80 will queue and time out requests.
- DO: pin the container's base image tag and buildpack builder version in CI; don't deploy `:latest` tags to production.
- DON'T: deploy secrets or config drift by hand in the console for production services — manage service config as Terraform (google_cloud_run_v2_service) or IaC, with console edits only for emergency rollback.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- Dockerfile (Python service example) ---
FROM python:3.12-slim AS base
WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
COPY . .
ENV PORT=8080
EXPOSE 8080
# Use an ASGI server with multiple workers for concurrency > 1
CMD ["uvicorn", "app.main:app", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8080", "--workers", "2"]

# --- gcloud run deploy (service) ---
gcloud run deploy my-service \
  --project=my-project \
  --region=my-region \
  --image=my-region-docker.pkg.dev/my-project/my-repo/my-service:GIT_SHA \
  --platform=managed \
  --no-allow-unauthenticated \
  --min-instances=1 \
  --max-instances=20 \
  --concurrency=40 \
  --cpu=1 --memory=512Mi \
  --timeout=300 \
  --set-secrets=SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY=supabase-service-role-key:latest,DATABASE_URL=my-service-db-url:latest \
  --service-account=my-service-runtime@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com

# --- gcloud run deploy (Cloud Run job, batch example) ---
gcloud run jobs deploy my-batch-job \
  --project=my-project \
  --region=my-region \
  --image=my-region-docker.pkg.dev/my-project/my-repo/my-batch-job:GIT_SHA \
  --tasks=50 \
  --parallelism=10 \
  --task-timeout=3600 \
  --max-retries=2 \
  --set-secrets=DATABASE_URL=my-service-db-url:latest

# --- Terraform: google_cloud_run_v2_service ---
resource "google_cloud_run_v2_service" "my_service" {
  name     = "my-service"
  location = "my-region"
  project  = "my-project"
  ingress  = "INGRESS_TRAFFIC_INTERNAL_LOAD_BALANCER" # avoid ALL unless service must be public

  template {
    service_account = "my-service-runtime@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
    scaling {
      min_instance_count = 1
      max_instance_count = 20
    }
    max_instance_request_concurrency = 40
    containers {
      image = "my-region-docker.pkg.dev/my-project/my-repo/my-service:GIT_SHA"
      resources {
        limits = {
          cpu    = "1"
          memory = "512Mi"
        }
      }
      env {
        name = "SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY"
        value_source {
          secret_key_ref {
            secret_id = "supabase-service-role-key"
            version = "latest"
          }
        }
      }
    }
    timeout = "300s"
  }
}

# Explicit invoker binding instead of allUsers:
resource "google_cloud_run_v2_service_iam_member" "invoker" {
  project  = google_cloud_run_v2_service.my_service.project
  location = google_cloud_run_v2_service.my_service.location
  name     = google_cloud_run_v2_service.my_service.name
  role     = "roles/run.invoker"
  member   = "serviceAccount:my-caller-sa@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
}

# --- GitHub Actions deploy step (assumes WIF already configured; see gcp-identity-wif) ---
- name: Authenticate to Google Cloud (WIF)
  uses: google-github-actions/auth@v2
  with:
    workload_identity_provider: projects/000000000000/locations/global/workloadIdentityPools/my-pool/providers/my-provider
    service_account: my-deployer@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com

- name: Deploy to Cloud Run
  uses: google-github-actions/deploy-cloudrun@v2
  with:
    service: my-service
    region: my-region
    image: my-region-docker.pkg.dev/my-project/my-repo/my-service:${{ github.sha }}
    flags: --min-instances=1 --max-instances=20 --concurrency=40 --no-allow-unauthenticated
**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- Concurrency left at default 80 on a synchronous single-threaded Python app (plain Flask dev server) causes request queuing and timeouts under moderate load.
- min-instances=0 on a user-facing API causes visible cold-start latency spikes (container start + app init + framework boot) on the first request after idle periods.
- max-instances unset or too high lets a traffic spike exhaust Supabase/Cloud SQL connection pool limits, causing cascading connection errors.
- Secrets passed via --set-env-vars end up in plaintext in Cloud Console revision metadata, gcloud describe output, and CI logs.
- --allow-unauthenticated applied to an internal service-to-service API exposes it to the public internet with only obscurity (unguessable URL) as protection.
- Background work started per-request (threads, timers) with default request-based CPU allocation silently stalls once the response is returned and CPU is throttled.
- Buildpack source deploys backdate all file mtimes to 1980, breaking cache-busting or ETag logic that depends on file modification time.
- Long-running batch work implemented as a Cloud Run service hitting the request timeout ceiling instead of being modeled as a Cloud Run job with its much longer task timeout.
- Container doesn't handle SIGTERM, so in-flight requests/DB transactions are hard-killed during scale-down or deploys, causing partial writes.
- Committed JSON service-account keys used for deploy instead of WIF, creating long-lived exportable credentials in CI secrets.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Using a Cloud Run service with a scheduled Cloud Scheduler HTTP trigger as a substitute for a Cloud Run job for long batch/ETL work.
- Granting roles/run.invoker to allUsers as a quick fix instead of scoping to specific caller service accounts or using an API gateway/load balancer with IAP.
- Baking Supabase or third-party API keys directly into the Docker image or source code instead of Secret Manager references.
- Running one giant Cloud Run function that fans out into unrelated business logic instead of a Cloud Run service with proper routing.
- Treating min-instances as a performance dial without accounting for its always-on billing cost across all environments (leaving min-instances=1 in every preview/staging environment).
- Ignoring adaptive concurrency behavior and manually tuning concurrency to an arbitrarily low number without load testing, leaving capacity (and cost efficiency) on the table.
- Deploying via `gcloud run deploy --source` directly to production without CI, bypassing tests, security scanning, and version pinning.
- Mixing container image tags like :latest in Terraform/CI deploy configs, making rollbacks non-deterministic.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- gcloud CLI (run, functions, secrets, iam subcommands)
- Cloud Run Terraform provider (google_cloud_run_v2_service, google_cloud_run_v2_job)
- google-github-actions/auth and google-github-actions/deploy-cloudrun GitHub Actions
- Cloud Build (used implicitly by source-based deploys and buildpacks)
- Artifact Registry (container image storage)
- Secret Manager (secret storage + versioning)
- Cloud Run buildpacks (Google Cloud buildpacks builder)
- Cloud Monitoring / Cloud Logging (Cloud Run request metrics, container logs)
- Cloud Scheduler (for triggering Cloud Run jobs on a cron)
- Eventarc (event triggers for Cloud Run functions/services)
**versions_as_of** _Version and runtime pins as of research date (labeled as_of)_:

- gcloud CLI — verify current version at deploy time; use `gcloud components update` before CI image build — as_of 2026-08-29
- google-github-actions/deploy-cloudrun@v2 — as_of 2026-08-29
- google-github-actions/auth@v2 — as_of 2026-08-29
- Cloud Run Terraform resource: google_cloud_run_v2_service / google_cloud_run_v2_job (Google provider, use latest google provider 6.x [uncertain exact minor]) — as_of 2026-08-29
- Google Cloud buildpacks builder (gcp-python312 / gcp-nodejs22 style builder images vary by language, auto-selected by source deploy) — as_of 2026-08-29
- Cloud Run functions unified onto Cloud Run infra (2nd gen model is now simply "Cloud Run functions") — as_of 2026-08-29
- python:3.12-slim / node:22-slim recommended current LTS base images for Dockerfile builds — as_of 2026-08-29
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- 1st-gen Cloud Functions is legacy; new event-driven functions should target Cloud Run functions (2nd gen, unified on Cloud Run infra), not the 1st-gen runtime.
- Cloud Run Worker Pools and Instances (long-lived singleton) are Preview features as of 2026-08-29 — do not treat their behavior/limits as GA-stable; revisit before relying on them in production.
- google-github-actions/setup-gcloud and older auth actions using exported JSON service-account keys are superseded by google-github-actions/auth with Workload Identity Federation — migrate any legacy key-based deploy workflow.
- Buildpack builder images are periodically deprecated/rotated by Google; pin and monitor the builder version used by source-based deploys rather than always tracking "latest".
- Watch for changes to default CPU allocation and concurrency tuning behavior (adaptive concurrency tuning is a relatively recent default) which can change effective per-instance throughput without a config change on your part.
- gcloud command surface for Cloud Run is still evolving (e.g., flags for Worker Pools/Instances); re-check `gcloud run --help` against this doc before the review_by date.

### security

- **identity_model**: Cloud Run services and jobs run under a per-service runtime service account (default is the Compute Engine default SA unless overridden — always override with a least-privilege, per-service SA). Deploys from GitHub Actions authenticate via Workload Identity Federation (no downloaded JSON keys); full WIF pool/provider/attribute-condition setup is documented in the sibling item gcp-identity-wif — this item only assumes a working WIF-backed deployer identity is already wired via google-github-actions/auth.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Public Cloud Run service (--allow-unauthenticated or allUsers invoker binding) exposing an internal API meant only for service-to-service calls.
- Secrets leaked via plaintext environment variables visible in Cloud Console revision details, `gcloud run services describe` output, or CI build logs.
- Over-privileged runtime service account (e.g., using the default Compute Engine SA with project-wide Editor role) letting a compromised container pivot broadly across the project.
- Long-lived exported service-account JSON keys used for CI deploys instead of WIF, creating a stealable, non-expiring credential.
- Container image supply-chain risk: unpinned base images or unscanned third-party dependencies baked into the deployed image.
- Uncapped max-instances allowing a traffic spike or malicious flood to run up compute cost or overwhelm downstream Supabase connection limits (a denial-of-wallet / denial-of-service vector).
- Buildpack/source-based deploys pulling dependencies at build time without a lockfile-pinned, reviewed dependency set.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Default every service to --no-allow-unauthenticated; grant roles/run.invoker only to specific caller service accounts or through an authenticated gateway.
- Use --set-secrets / secret volume mounts exclusively for credentials; grant roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor scoped to individual secrets, not project-wide.
- Create a dedicated, least-privilege runtime service account per service (e.g., my-service-runtime@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com) instead of using the default Compute SA.
- Use Workload Identity Federation for all CI deploy identities; disable or avoid creating exportable JSON keys for deploy service accounts (see gcp-identity-wif).
- Pin base image digests/tags and scan images (Artifact Registry vulnerability scanning) before promoting to production.
- Always set explicit --max-instances tied to downstream capacity (e.g., Supabase Postgres max connections / pooler limits).
- Enable Cloud Audit Logs for Cloud Run admin actions and IAM policy changes; alert on invoker bindings granted to allUsers.
- Review buildpack/source-deploy dependency manifests in CI (lockfiles committed, dependency scanning step) before allowing a deploy to proceed.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Never generate `--allow-unauthenticated` in code/config for a service unless the user explicitly states the endpoint must be public.
- Never write secrets as literal values in --set-env-vars, Dockerfile ENV, or Terraform env blocks — always emit Secret Manager references (--set-secrets or secret_key_ref).
- Always include explicit --min-instances and --max-instances flags/fields in generated deploy commands and Terraform, never leave them to platform defaults silently.
- When generating a GitHub Actions deploy workflow, always use google-github-actions/auth with workload_identity_provider — never emit a step that reads a `GCP_SA_KEY` JSON secret.
- When the workload described is a long-running batch/ETL task, generate a Cloud Run job, not a Cloud Run service with an HTTP trigger.
- Default generated concurrency to a conservative value (e.g., 40) with a comment recommending load-testing before raising it, rather than assuming max async throughput.
- Always scaffold a SIGTERM handler in generated service code (Python/Node) for graceful shutdown.
- Use placeholder ids (my-project, my-region, my-service) in all generated examples; never invent or reuse real-looking project ids.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- Block deploy if the Terraform/gcloud plan sets ingress to allow-all or invoker to allUsers without an explicit override flag/approval.
- Block deploy if any --set-env-vars value matches a secret-like pattern (KEY, TOKEN, PASSWORD, SECRET) instead of using --set-secrets.
- Require max-instances to be set (fail lint if absent) on any google_cloud_run_v2_service resource.
- Require the container image reference to be an immutable digest or CI-generated SHA tag, not `:latest`, before allowing a production deploy.
- Run a container vulnerability scan (Artifact Registry scanning or equivalent) and block on critical CVEs before promoting an image.
- Verify the GitHub Actions workflow authenticates via workload_identity_provider, failing the pipeline if a static key-based auth step is detected.
- Load-test concurrency settings in a staging environment before allowing a concurrency change >80 to reach production.
- Require min-instances justification (comment or PR description) for any service setting min-instances > 0, given the always-on cost.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- Cloud Monitoring: track container instance count, request latency (p50/p95/p99), and container CPU/memory utilization per revision.
- Cloud Logging: structured JSON logs from the container, correlated by trace id; enable Cloud Trace integration for request-level latency breakdown.
- Monitor cold-start frequency (instances started from zero) as a proxy for whether min-instances tuning is needed.
- Alert on 429/503 responses correlated with max-instances being hit (scaling saturation).
- Track billable instance time vs request count to catch min-instances configurations that are quietly costing more than expected.
- Monitor Secret Manager access logs for the runtime service account to detect anomalous secret access patterns.
- Dashboard Cloud Run job execution success/failure rates and per-task retry counts for batch pipelines.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- Always cap max-instances per service; multiply worst-case instance count by max CPU/memory to sanity-check monthly cost ceiling.
- Restrict min-instances>0 to production, user-facing services only; keep staging/preview environments at scale-to-zero.
- Prefer request-based CPU allocation (default) over CPU-always-allocated unless background processing genuinely requires it — always-allocated CPU is billed continuously per instance.
- Right-size CPU/memory per service based on load-test profiling rather than defaulting to the largest available instance shape.
- Batch/ETL workloads should use Cloud Run jobs with bounded task/parallelism counts rather than services with min-instances kept warm for occasional triggers.
- Review concurrency settings periodically — under-utilized concurrency means paying for more instances than necessary for the same throughput.
- Use budget alerts scoped to the Cloud Run service (via billing export + Cloud Monitoring) to catch runaway autoscaling early.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Flag `--allow-unauthenticated` (or `run.googleapis.com/invoker-iam-disabled: 'true'`, or an `allUsers` IAM binding with role `roles/run.invoker`) on any service not explicitly marked as a public-facing endpoint.
- Flag any `--set-env-vars` or Terraform `env { value = ... }` block whose key name matches secret-like patterns (SECRET, TOKEN, PASSWORD, KEY, CREDENTIAL) instead of using `--set-secrets` / `secret_key_ref`.
- Flag a `google_cloud_run_v2_service` resource or `gcloud run deploy` command missing an explicit `--max-instances` / `max_instance_count`.
- Flag a latency-sensitive service (tagged user-facing/API-gateway-fronted) with no `--min-instances` set, implying scale-to-zero cold starts on the critical path.
- Flag `--concurrency=1` left in place alongside an async framework (FastAPI/async Express/Next.js API routes) without a load-test justification comment, since it likely under-utilizes instance capacity.
- Flag container images referenced by mutable tags (`:latest`, `:main`) in production deploy configs instead of an immutable digest or commit-SHA tag.
- Flag a GitHub Actions workflow deploying to Cloud Run that references a `GCP_SA_KEY`-style secret instead of `google-github-actions/auth` with `workload_identity_provider`.
- Flag long-running (batch/ETL-shaped) logic implemented as a Cloud Run service handler instead of a Cloud Run job.
- Flag a Dockerfile or buildpack config lacking a non-root `USER` directive or running as root by default.
- Flag missing SIGTERM/graceful-shutdown handling in generated service entrypoint code.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- Cloud Run has four workload types: Services (HTTP, autoscaling), Jobs (run-to-completion, up to 10,000 tasks), Worker Pools (continuous non-HTTP), and Instances (long-lived singleton, preview) — pick based on trigger model, not habit.
- Cloud Run functions is the unified successor to 2nd-gen Cloud Functions, running on the same Cloud Run infrastructure — use it for small single-purpose event-triggered handlers, use Cloud Run services for multi-route apps/frameworks.
- Concurrency defaults to 80 with adaptive concurrency tuning (ACT) keeping per-instance CPU under ~90%; tune down for blocking/single-threaded workloads, tune up (after load testing) for async I/O-bound workloads.
- min-instances eliminates cold starts but bills continuously (reduced idle rate under request-based billing, full rate under always-allocated CPU) — reserve for latency-sensitive production services only.
- max-instances always has a default cap; always set an explicit value tied to downstream capacity (e.g., Supabase connection pool limits) to avoid cost blowouts and connection exhaustion.
- Cold starts queue for up to 3.5x average startup time or 10 seconds; idle instances persist up to 15 minutes before removal, which naturally smooths bursty traffic.
- Never store secrets in plain environment variables — always route through Secret Manager via --set-secrets or volume mounts with a scoped secretAccessor binding.
- Never leave --allow-unauthenticated on internal services; use roles/run.invoker scoped to specific caller service accounts, and route CI deploy auth through Workload Identity Federation (see gcp-identity-wif), never JSON keys.
- Cloud Run jobs support up to 7-day task timeouts (10 min default) with 0-10 configurable retries — use them for batch/ETL instead of forcing long work through a service's shorter request timeout.
- Buildpack source deploys are convenient but backdate file mtimes to 1980-01-01 (can break cache-busting) and cede build control; prefer an explicit Dockerfile for production services in a TS/Next + Python-services stack.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Container: Dockerfile present, non-root user, pinned base image tag/digest, minimal final image.
- Concurrency: value chosen and load-tested for the framework's threading/async model, not left at an unverified default.
- Autoscaling: min-instances and max-instances both explicitly set with cost/capacity justification.
- CPU allocation: request-based vs always-allocated decision made deliberately based on background-work needs.
- Secrets: all credentials mounted via Secret Manager, none present as plaintext env vars.
- IAM: runtime service account is least-privilege and per-service; invoker access scoped, not allUsers, unless the service is genuinely public.
- Identity: CI deploy uses Workload Identity Federation, no exported JSON key in repo secrets (cross-check gcp-identity-wif).
- Timeouts: request timeout (services) or task timeout (jobs) sized appropriately for the workload; long batch work modeled as a job.
- Graceful shutdown: SIGTERM handled, in-flight work drained before instance termination.
- Observability: logging/tracing wired, cold-start and scaling-saturation alerts configured.
- Cost: budget alert configured; concurrency/min-instances/max-instances reviewed against actual traffic periodically.

### Uncertain fields

## GCP data layer — GCS, Cloud SQL Postgres vs Supabase/AlloyDB, Firestore fit, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager, Cloud Tasks/Scheduler

### identity

- **name**: GCP data layer — GCS, Cloud SQL Postgres vs Supabase/AlloyDB, Firestore fit, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager, Cloud Tasks/Scheduler
- **item_id**: gcp-data-storage
- **cluster**: gcp
- **short_definition**: The GCP-native data and messaging surface — Cloud Storage (GCS) for object storage, Cloud SQL for PostgreSQL and AlloyDB for PostgreSQL-compatible relational data, Firestore for document/NoSQL, Pub/Sub for async messaging, Secret Manager for credentials, and Cloud Tasks/Scheduler for deferred and cron work. In this stack's default TS/Next + Python-services + Supabase architecture, Supabase Postgres is the primary relational database and GCS/Cloud SQL/AlloyDB/Firestore are reached for deliberately when a workload needs GCP-VPC residency, GCP-native service integration, huge-scale object storage, or document/realtime access patterns Supabase doesn't fit well.
- **scope_in**: GCS buckets and lifecycle policy for blobs/backups/exports; Cloud SQL for Postgres and AlloyDB as alternative/adjunct relational stores to Supabase; Firestore as a document/NoSQL option; Pub/Sub as the GCP-native async messaging backbone; Secret Manager as the credential store for GCP-hosted workloads; Cloud Tasks for deferred HTTP work and Cloud Scheduler for cron triggers.
- **scope_out**: Supabase-specific Postgres setup, RLS, and Auth (owned by the Supabase cluster items); GCP IAM/Workload Identity Federation mechanics (owned by gcp-identity-wif — this item only references it); Cloud Run/GKE compute hosting (owned by gcp-compute items); BigQuery and data warehousing/analytics pipelines (owned by a separate analytics item if present); Vertex AI / ML services.
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Default relational database for the stack is Supabase Postgres; do not stand up Cloud SQL or AlloyDB unless a decision gate below is triggered.
- For object storage (uploads, exports, generated artifacts, backups) reach for GCS only when the workload already lives in GCP or needs GCP-native processing (Cloud Run, Dataflow, Vertex AI); otherwise Supabase Storage is the default.
- When GCS is used, default to a single regional bucket per environment (not multi-region) unless multi-region durability is an explicit requirement, since multi-region roughly doubles storage cost.
- Default GCS bucket to uniform bucket-level access (UBLA) on, public access prevention enforced, and a lifecycle rule that transitions or deletes objects — never leave a bucket with default (fine-grained) ACLs in new projects.
- If Cloud SQL is chosen, default to Postgres engine version 16 or 17, private IP only, `require_ssl`/`ssl_mode` set to `ENCRYPTED_ONLY` or `TRUSTED_CLIENT_CERTIFICATE_REQUIRED`, and automated backups + point-in-time recovery enabled from day one.
- Default to Cloud SQL over AlloyDB unless the workload specifically needs AlloyDB's columnar engine for HTAP/analytics-on-live-data or its higher scale ceiling — AlloyDB costs more and adds operational surface.
- Default async messaging inside GCP to Pub/Sub with push subscriptions targeting Cloud Run/Cloud Functions over Workload Identity, not polling loops.
- Default all secrets consumed by GCP workloads to Secret Manager references resolved at runtime via the attached service account identity — never bake secrets into container images, Terraform state, or plain environment variables.
- Default deferred/one-off async HTTP work (webhooks, retries, rate-limited calls to third parties) to Cloud Tasks; default recurring/cron work to Cloud Scheduler hitting an authenticated HTTP endpoint (Cloud Run) or publishing to Pub/Sub.
- Treat Firestore as an intentional, narrow choice (realtime sync, mobile/offline-first clients, deeply nested document access patterns) rather than a general-purpose database — do not use it as a second source of truth alongside Supabase Postgres.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Use Supabase Postgres (default) when: the workload is standard TS/Next + Python CRUD/relational data, needs Row Level Security tied to Supabase Auth, benefits from Supabase's built-in realtime/storage/edge-functions, and has no hard GCP-residency requirement.
- Use Cloud SQL for Postgres instead of Supabase when: the workload must run inside a private GCP VPC with no public egress (e.g. regulated data that cannot traverse the public internet even via TLS), must integrate tightly with other GCP-native services via Private Service Connect, or an existing GCP-only compliance boundary (e.g. specific data-residency/sovereignty commitments already scoped to GCP) requires the database to live in the same cloud and IAM domain.
- Use AlloyDB instead of Cloud SQL/Supabase when: the workload needs HTAP — heavy analytical queries against live transactional data (AlloyDB's columnar engine) — or needs higher read-scale via read pools and vector search co-located with transactional data for GCP-native AI pipelines (Vertex AI integration), and the added cost/ops overhead is justified by that specific need.
- Use Firestore instead of Postgres (Supabase or GCP) when: the access pattern is genuinely document-shaped with shallow, denormalized reads (per-user documents, config blobs, mobile/offline-first sync), the app needs Firestore's realtime listener/offline-sync SDKs specifically (not just 'realtime updates' — Supabase Realtime covers most of that), or write patterns are naturally hierarchical collections without cross-entity joins/transactions.
- Do NOT use Firestore when the domain model requires relational joins, multi-row transactions across entities, complex aggregate queries, or strong consistency guarantees across collections — Postgres (Supabase/Cloud SQL/AlloyDB) wins.
- Do NOT split the source of truth: pick one primary relational store per bounded context. If Cloud SQL/AlloyDB is chosen for a subsystem, do not also mirror that data into Supabase 'just in case' — use clear service boundaries and sync explicitly if truly needed.
- Use GCS over Supabase Storage when: object volume/egress is large enough that GCS class tiering (Standard/Nearline/Coldline/Archive) and lifecycle rules meaningfully reduce cost, or objects are consumed directly by GCP-native compute (Cloud Run, Dataflow, BigQuery external tables, Vertex AI training data) and keeping data in-VPC avoids egress and latency.
- Use Pub/Sub over a Python-service-internal queue (e.g. Redis/Celery) when: multiple GCP-native consumers need fan-out delivery, delivery must survive consumer downtime with durable retry, or the trigger needs to originate from another GCP service (Cloud Storage notifications, Cloud Scheduler, Eventarc).
- Use Cloud Tasks over Pub/Sub when: the work is a single deferred call to a specific HTTP endpoint that needs per-task scheduling, rate limiting, and dedup — Pub/Sub is fan-out messaging, Cloud Tasks is a work queue with per-task control.
- Escalate to gcp-identity-wif whenever any of these services need cross-cloud access (e.g. a Vercel-hosted Next.js app or GitHub Actions CI calling Cloud SQL/GCS/Secret Manager) — that access must be via Workload Identity Federation, never a downloaded service-account JSON key.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- DO: enforce `publicAccessPrevention: enforced` and uniform bucket-level access on every GCS bucket; treat `allUsers`/`allAuthenticatedUsers` IAM bindings as an incident, not a config choice.
- DO: put a lifecycle rule on every GCS bucket used for logs, exports, or queue-like drop zones (transition to Nearline/Coldline, then delete) so cost and blast radius don't grow unbounded.
- DO: require SSL/TLS on every Cloud SQL instance and prefer private IP + Cloud SQL Auth Proxy or Private Service Connect over public IP.
- DO: reference secrets via `projects/*/secrets/*/versions/latest` (or a pinned version) resolved by the runtime's attached service account — never via a plaintext `.env` value in Cloud Run/GKE manifests.
- DO: make Cloud Tasks/Pub/Sub handlers idempotent — both give at-least-once delivery, so duplicate execution is expected, not exceptional.
- DO: set Pub/Sub subscription acknowledgement deadlines and dead-letter topics for every production subscription.
- DON'T: create a second Postgres (Cloud SQL/AlloyDB) 'just to be safe' when Supabase already serves the workload — this doubles the operational and identity surface for no benefit.
- DON'T: use Firestore as a cache or session store substitute for Redis, or as a relational database with client-side joins — both are anti-patterns that show up as N+1 reads and inconsistent writes.
- DON'T: grant `roles/secretmanager.admin` or broad `Editor`/`Owner` roles to a workload service account when it only needs `roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor` on specific secrets.
- DON'T: hardcode a GCS bucket name, Cloud SQL connection string, or Secret Manager resource path with a real project id in application code — inject via environment/config resolved at deploy time.
- DON'T: leave a Cloud SQL instance with a public IP and an open (0.0.0.0/0) authorized network in any environment, including staging.
- DON'T: use long-lived downloaded service-account JSON keys for CI or app-to-GCP access — use Workload Identity Federation (see gcp-identity-wif).
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
############################################
# GCS bucket: private, lifecycle rule, no public access
############################################
resource "google_storage_bucket" "my_bucket" {
  name                        = "my-bucket"
  project                     = "my-project"
  location                    = "US-CENTRAL1" # regional, not multi-region, unless required
  storage_class                = "STANDARD"
  uniform_bucket_level_access = true
  public_access_prevention    = "enforced"
  force_destroy               = false

  versioning {
    enabled = true
  }

  lifecycle_rule {
    condition {
      age = 30
    }
    action {
      type          = "SetStorageClass"
      storage_class = "NEARLINE"
    }
  }

  lifecycle_rule {
    condition {
      age = 365
    }
    action {
      type = "Delete"
    }
  }
}

# Never bind allUsers/allAuthenticatedUsers here. Grant access per-service-account:
resource "google_storage_bucket_iam_member" "my_bucket_reader" {
  bucket = google_storage_bucket.my_bucket.name
  role   = "roles/storage.objectViewer"
  member = "serviceAccount:my-workload-sa@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
}

############################################
# Cloud SQL for Postgres: private IP, SSL required
############################################
resource "google_sql_database_instance" "my_instance" {
  name             = "my-instance"
  project          = "my-project"
  database_version = "POSTGRES_16"
  region           = "us-central1"

  settings {
    tier              = "db-custom-2-8192" # 2 vCPU / 8GB, placeholder sizing
    availability_type = "REGIONAL"          # HA standby in another zone

    ip_configuration {
      ipv4_enabled    = false                       # no public IP
      private_network = "projects/my-project/global/networks/my-vpc"
      ssl_mode        = "ENCRYPTED_ONLY"             # enforce TLS on all connections
    }

    backup_configuration {
      enabled                        = true
      point_in_time_recovery_enabled = true
    }
  }

  deletion_protection = true
}

resource "google_sql_user" "app_user" {
  name     = "app_user"
  instance = google_sql_database_instance.my_instance.name
  password = "change-me-via-secret-manager" # do not hardcode in real usage; use random_password + Secret Manager
}

############################################
# Secret Manager: secret + IAM binding scoped to one service account
############################################
resource "google_secret_manager_secret" "db_password" {
  project   = "my-project"
  secret_id = "my-instance-db-password"

  replication {
    auto {}  # or user_managed { replicas { location = "us-central1" } } for residency control
  }
}

resource "google_secret_manager_secret_version" "db_password_v1" {
  secret      = google_secret_manager_secret.db_password.id
  secret_data = "CHANGE_ME" # supplied via CI secret input, never committed
}

resource "google_secret_manager_secret_iam_member" "db_password_accessor" {
  secret_id = google_secret_manager_secret.db_password.id
  role      = "roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor"
  member    = "serviceAccount:my-workload-sa@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
}

############################################
# Pub/Sub: topic + subscription with dead-letter and ack deadline
############################################
resource "google_pubsub_topic" "events" {
  project = "my-project"
  name    = "my-events-topic"
}

resource "google_pubsub_topic" "events_dlq" {
  project = "my-project"
  name    = "my-events-topic-dlq"
}

resource "google_pubsub_subscription" "events_sub" {
  project              = "my-project"
  name                 = "my-events-sub"
  topic                = google_pubsub_topic.events.id
  ack_deadline_seconds = 60

  dead_letter_policy {
    dead_letter_topic     = google_pubsub_topic.events_dlq.id
    max_delivery_attempts = 5
  }

  retry_policy {
    minimum_backoff = "10s"
    maximum_backoff = "600s"
  }
}

# Grant the subscriber service account pull rights only (no admin/edit)
resource "google_pubsub_subscription_iam_member" "subscriber" {
  subscription = google_pubsub_subscription.events_sub.id
  role         = "roles/pubsub.subscriber"
  member       = "serviceAccount:my-workload-sa@my-project.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
}

# gcloud equivalent for quick manual checks (never for production provisioning):
# gcloud storage buckets create gs://my-bucket --uniform-bucket-level-access --public-access-prevention=enforced
# gcloud sql instances create my-instance --database-version=POSTGRES_16 --no-assign-ip --require-ssl
# gcloud secrets create my-instance-db-password --replication-policy=automatic
# gcloud pubsub topics create my-events-topic

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- A GCS bucket created without `public_access_prevention=enforced` gets an accidental `allUsers` object viewer binding and leaks data — the single most common GCP data-layer incident.
- Cloud SQL instance left with a public IP and a broad authorized-network CIDR (or none at all configured, defaulting open) becomes internet-reachable; brute-force or credential-stuffing follows.
- Cloud SQL/AlloyDB hits max_connections under load because the app doesn't use a connection pooler (PgBouncer/Cloud SQL built-in pooling) — this is a frequent outage cause when many serverless (Cloud Run) instances each open direct connections.
- Pub/Sub subscriber falls behind or crashes without a dead-letter topic configured; messages retry indefinitely, silently masking a poison-message bug and inflating cost.
- Secret Manager secret rotated but the running Cloud Run/GKE workload has the old version cached in memory or pinned to a specific version number — rotation doesn't propagate until redeploy.
- GCS bucket used as an ad hoc queue (drop-file-trigger-function pattern) with no lifecycle rule accumulates unbounded objects, silently growing storage cost and eventually hitting per-bucket listing/request-rate friction.
- Firestore document exceeds the 1 MiB size limit because a team stores large blobs or growing arrays directly in a document instead of GCS + a reference — writes start failing in production.
- Cloud Tasks queue configured without rate limiting floods a downstream third-party API, tripping that API's own rate limits and causing cascading task failures/retries.
- Cross-cloud call (Vercel/GitHub Actions to GCP) implemented with a downloaded service-account JSON key checked into a repo secret manager; key leaks or is never rotated, becoming a long-lived credential risk.
- Splitting the source of truth between Supabase Postgres and a GCP-native store (Cloud SQL or Firestore) without a clear sync contract leads to data drift and inconsistent reads across the two systems.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Provisioning a second Postgres instance (Cloud SQL/AlloyDB) purely for 'GCP-native feel' when Supabase already serves the workload and no decision gate is triggered.
- Generating Terraform/gcloud that creates a GCS bucket without `uniform_bucket_level_access` or `public_access_prevention`.
- Generating a Cloud SQL instance resource with `ipv4_enabled = true` and no `authorized_networks` restriction, or without `ssl_mode`/`require_ssl` set.
- Embedding secrets as plaintext `env` blocks in Cloud Run/Kubernetes manifests instead of `--set-secrets` / `secretKeyRef` pointing at Secret Manager.
- Using Firestore as a relational join engine by fetching whole collections client-side and joining in application code.
- Writing a Pub/Sub subscriber without an ack/nack path or idempotency key, assuming exactly-once delivery.
- Issuing a downloaded JSON service-account key for CI instead of Workload Identity Federation (cross-reference gcp-identity-wif) — never generate this as a default in codegen.
- Storing a Cloud SQL/Secret Manager resource ID or connection string with a real project id, IP, or hostname in checked-in code — placeholders only until real infra exists.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- gcloud CLI (storage, sql, secrets, pubsub, tasks, scheduler command groups)
- Terraform google/google-beta provider (google_storage_bucket, google_sql_database_instance, google_secret_manager_secret, google_pubsub_topic/subscription, google_cloud_tasks_queue, google_cloud_scheduler_job)
- Google Cloud Console (Cloud Storage, Cloud SQL, Firestore, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager, Cloud Tasks, Cloud Scheduler consoles)
- Node.js client libraries: @google-cloud/storage, @google-cloud/pubsub, @google-cloud/secret-manager, @google-cloud/tasks, pg / node-postgres for Cloud SQL
- Python client libraries: google-cloud-storage, google-cloud-pubsub, google-cloud-secret-manager, google-cloud-tasks, psycopg / SQLAlchemy for Cloud SQL/AlloyDB
- Cloud SQL Auth Proxy (cloud-sql-proxy) for local/dev connections without public IP exposure
- Supabase CLI and Postgres client tooling (psql, Supabase Studio) for the default relational store
- pgBouncer or Cloud SQL's built-in connection pooling for high-connection-count serverless workloads
- Firebase CLI / Firestore emulator for local Firestore development
- Eventarc (for wiring GCS/Cloud SQL events into Pub/Sub or Cloud Run triggers)
**versions_as_of** _Version and runtime pins as of research date (labeled as_of)_:

- Cloud SQL for PostgreSQL — supports major versions through Postgres 16/17 in most regions — as_of 2026-08-29 [uncertain: exact latest supported minor/major per region not independently re-verified this session]
- AlloyDB for PostgreSQL — PostgreSQL-compatible engine, current generation with columnar engine and vector search GA — as_of 2026-08-29
- Firestore — Native mode is the recommended mode for new projects; Datastore mode retained for legacy compatibility; MongoDB API compatibility layer available — as_of 2026-08-29
- Cloud Storage — no versioned engine; storage classes Standard/Nearline/Coldline/Archive plus Autoclass and 'Rapid Bucket' (zonal, low-latency) option for AI/ML workloads — as_of 2026-08-29
- Pub/Sub — no versioned engine; supports message ordering, exactly-once delivery (subscription-level setting), and configurable retention — as_of 2026-08-29
- Secret Manager — no versioned engine; regional and automatic (multi-region) replication policies both GA — as_of 2026-08-29
- Cloud Tasks and Cloud Scheduler — stable GA services; Cloud Tasks HTTP targets support Cloud Run/GKE/Compute Engine/on-prem — as_of 2026-08-29
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Firestore Datastore mode is legacy-compatibility only — do not choose it for new greenfield work; use Firestore Native mode.
- GCS fine-grained (ACL-based) bucket access is discouraged in favor of uniform bucket-level access; new buckets should default to UBLA.
- Downloaded service-account JSON keys remain technically supported but are increasingly restricted by org policy across GCP; treat them as deprecated-in-practice for CI/cross-cloud auth in favor of Workload Identity Federation (see gcp-identity-wif).
- App Engine-targeted Cloud Tasks queues are a legacy pattern tied to App Engine; prefer HTTP targets against Cloud Run for new services.
- Cloud SQL first-generation instances and very old Postgres major versions (pre-13) are past or nearing end-of-life support paths — always provision current-generation, current-major-version instances.
- Cloud Pub/Sub 'exactly-once' guarantees are subscription-level opt-in, not default — do not assume ordering or exactly-once without explicitly enabling and testing those settings.

### security

- **identity_model**: Every service-to-service and CI interaction with GCS, Cloud SQL/AlloyDB, Firestore, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager, Cloud Tasks, and Cloud Scheduler must be authorized through IAM service accounts and short-lived credentials — never long-lived JSON key files. Workloads running on GCP compute (Cloud Run, GKE, Compute Engine) get identity automatically via attached service accounts and the metadata server; workloads outside GCP (Vercel-hosted Next.js, GitHub Actions CI, a Python service running elsewhere) must use Workload Identity Federation (WIF) to exchange an external OIDC token for short-lived GCP credentials scoped to a specific service account and role. Grant the narrowest applicable predefined or custom role per resource (e.g. `roles/storage.objectViewer` on one bucket, `roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor` on one secret, `roles/cloudsql.client` on one instance) rather than project-level Editor/Owner. Full WIF provider/pool setup, attestation conditions, and CI wiring are owned by gcp-identity-wif — this item assumes that identity plumbing exists and only specifies which role each data service needs.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Publicly readable GCS bucket (allUsers/allAuthenticatedUsers binding) exposing customer data or backups.
- Cloud SQL instance with public IP and permissive authorized networks exposed to internet-wide brute force or credential stuffing.
- Secrets stored in plaintext environment variables or checked-in config, visible in logs, process listings, or repo history.
- Long-lived service-account JSON keys leaked via CI logs, repo commits, or laptop exfiltration, granting durable unauthorized access.
- Pub/Sub topics/subscriptions with overly broad IAM (e.g. `roles/pubsub.editor` project-wide) allowing any workload to publish forged events or drain subscriptions.
- Firestore security rules misconfigured (default-allow or overly permissive) exposing documents to any authenticated or unauthenticated client.
- Cloud Tasks queue accepting unauthenticated task creation, allowing an attacker to flood a downstream handler or third-party API.
- Data residency violation from choosing multi-region/automatic replication for GCS or Secret Manager when the workload has region-pinned compliance requirements.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Enforce `public_access_prevention = enforced` and uniform bucket-level access on every GCS bucket via org policy constraint, not just per-resource config.
- Require private IP + `ssl_mode = ENCRYPTED_ONLY` (or client-cert) on all Cloud SQL/AlloyDB instances; block public IP creation via org policy where possible.
- Route every secret through Secret Manager with per-secret, per-service-account IAM bindings (`roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor`) — never a project-wide grant.
- Eliminate service-account JSON keys for CI and cross-cloud calls; use Workload Identity Federation exclusively (gcp-identity-wif) and set an org policy to disable service account key creation.
- Scope Pub/Sub IAM to per-topic/per-subscription publisher or subscriber roles, never `roles/pubsub.editor` at project level.
- Write and test Firestore security rules explicitly denying by default, allowing only the specific access patterns the app needs, validated with the Firestore emulator in CI.
- Require OIDC-authenticated invocation on Cloud Tasks HTTP targets (Cloud Run with `--no-allow-unauthenticated` and task-level auth headers).
- Pin GCS/Secret Manager replication/location policy to the region(s) required by data-residency commitments; avoid `automatic` replication when residency matters.
- Enable Cloud Audit Logs on all these services and alert on IAM policy changes (new bindings, especially `allUsers`/broad roles).
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- MUST default new relational-data code to Supabase Postgres client/connection patterns unless the task explicitly names a GCP-native requirement matching a decision gate above.
- MUST generate GCS bucket Terraform with `uniform_bucket_level_access = true` and `public_access_prevention = "enforced"` every time, with no toggle to disable public access prevention without an explicit user request and warning.
- MUST generate Cloud SQL/AlloyDB instances with private IP and SSL/TLS enforcement by default; must not set `ipv4_enabled = true` unless the user explicitly asks for a public IP and acknowledges the risk.
- MUST reference secrets via Secret Manager resource paths resolved by IAM/service-account identity, never emit plaintext secret values into generated `.env`, YAML, or Terraform variable defaults.
- MUST NOT generate code that creates or downloads a service-account JSON key for CI or app authentication; must generate Workload Identity Federation wiring instead (or flag that gcp-identity-wif should be consulted).
- MUST generate idempotent handler scaffolding (dedup key or upsert semantics) whenever writing Pub/Sub subscriber or Cloud Tasks handler code, given at-least-once delivery.
- MUST NOT scaffold Firestore as a relational replacement (e.g. generating client-side join loops across collections) — if relational semantics are needed, must redirect to Postgres.
- MUST include a GCS lifecycle rule in any generated bucket config that is described as a log/export/queue-drop-zone destination.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- Terraform plan/apply pipeline fails the build if it detects a `google_storage_bucket` without `uniform_bucket_level_access = true` or `public_access_prevention = "enforced"`.
- CI policy check (e.g. OPA/Conftest) blocks `google_sql_database_instance` resources with `ipv4_enabled = true` and no explicit override annotation.
- Secret-scanning step (e.g. gitleaks/truffleHog) runs on every PR to catch accidental plaintext secrets before they reach Secret Manager-backed config.
- IAM diff review gate flags any new `allUsers`, `allAuthenticatedUsers`, or project-level `roles/*.admin`/`Editor`/`Owner` binding introduced by a PR's Terraform plan.
- Firestore security rules are linted and tested against the local emulator in CI before deploy.
- Pub/Sub subscription configs are checked in CI to require a `dead_letter_policy` on every production subscription.
- Integration test suite verifies Cloud Tasks/Scheduler handlers return correctly on duplicate delivery (idempotency test).
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- Cloud Audit Logs (Admin Activity + Data Access) enabled and exported to a log sink for GCS, Cloud SQL, Secret Manager, and Pub/Sub IAM/config changes.
- Cloud Monitoring alerts on Cloud SQL connection count approaching max_connections, storage utilization approaching instance limit, and replication lag on HA standby.
- Cloud Monitoring alerts on Pub/Sub subscription oldest-unacked-message-age and dead-letter topic message count.
- GCS bucket-level metrics (request count, error rate, egress bytes) monitored to catch runaway consumers or lifecycle rule misconfiguration.
- Secret Manager access logs reviewed/alerted on for access from unexpected service accounts or unexpected regions.
- Cloud Tasks queue depth and dispatch error rate monitored to catch downstream endpoint failures before they cascade.
- Cost anomaly alerts (Cloud Billing budgets) set per project/service to catch runaway GCS storage growth or Cloud SQL over-provisioning early.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- Set a lifecycle rule on every GCS bucket that isn't an intentional permanent archive — unbounded growth is the most common silent GCP cost leak.
- Right-size Cloud SQL/AlloyDB instances to actual load; avoid defaulting to large dedicated-core tiers for low-traffic services — start small and scale with monitoring data.
- Prefer regional (not multi-region) GCS buckets and Standard storage class unless durability/availability requirements justify the cost premium.
- Use Cloud SQL committed-use discounts or sustained-use pricing once workload size is stable and predictable, not before.
- Avoid AlloyDB unless its HTAP/read-pool/vector features are actually used — it costs materially more than Cloud SQL for equivalent basic relational workloads.
- Set Cloud Billing budget alerts per project scoped to this data layer (GCS, Cloud SQL, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager) so a misconfigured lifecycle rule or runaway Pub/Sub retry loop is caught before month-end.
- Batch Secret Manager access where possible; excessive per-request secret fetches at high QPS can add measurable cost at scale.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Flag any GCS bucket IAM policy containing `allUsers` or `allAuthenticatedUsers` as a critical public-exposure finding.
- Flag any GCS bucket resource missing `uniform_bucket_level_access = true` or `public_access_prevention = "enforced"`.
- Flag any GCS bucket used as a queue/drop-zone pattern (inferred from naming or trigger wiring) with no lifecycle rule configured.
- Flag any Cloud SQL/AlloyDB instance with `ipv4_enabled = true` (public IP) combined with an empty or `0.0.0.0/0` authorized networks list.
- Flag any Cloud SQL instance without `ssl_mode`/`require_ssl` enforcement configured.
- Flag secrets referenced as plaintext values in environment variable blocks, Kubernetes manifests, or `.env` files instead of Secret Manager resource paths.
- Flag any service-account JSON key file present in a repo or referenced in CI config instead of Workload Identity Federation.
- Flag Pub/Sub subscriptions without a configured `dead_letter_policy` in production Terraform/config.
- Flag IAM bindings granting `roles/editor`, `roles/owner`, or `*.admin` roles to a workload service account where a narrower predefined role would suffice.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- GCP data layer is opt-in infrastructure in this stack: default to Supabase Postgres and Supabase Storage; only introduce GCS/Cloud SQL/AlloyDB/Firestore when a specific decision gate (VPC residency, GCP-native integration, HTAP/analytics, or document/realtime fit) is met.
- Every GCS bucket must ship with uniform bucket-level access, enforced public access prevention, and a lifecycle rule — no exceptions in generated code.
- Every Cloud SQL/AlloyDB instance must default to private IP + TLS enforcement + automated backups; public IP requires explicit user opt-in with an acknowledged risk note.
- All secrets for GCP-hosted workloads flow through Secret Manager referenced by IAM identity; plaintext env secrets are a scan-blocking finding.
- Cross-cloud and CI access to any of these services uses Workload Identity Federation, never a downloaded service-account key — cross-reference gcp-identity-wif for the full setup.
- Pub/Sub and Cloud Tasks both deliver at-least-once; all generated handlers must be idempotent and Pub/Sub subscriptions must have dead-letter topics.
- Firestore is a narrow, deliberate choice for document/realtime/offline-sync access patterns — never a relational-database substitute or a second source of truth alongside Supabase.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Confirm the workload actually needs GCP-native data services before provisioning anything beyond Supabase — cite the triggered decision gate.
- GCS buckets: uniform bucket-level access on, public access prevention enforced, lifecycle rule present, IAM scoped per-service-account.
- Cloud SQL/AlloyDB: private IP only, SSL/TLS enforced, automated backups + PITR enabled, connection pooling in place for serverless callers.
- Secret Manager: every secret has a scoped `secretAccessor` binding per consuming service account; no plaintext secret values anywhere in code or CI logs.
- Pub/Sub: every production subscription has an ack deadline, retry policy, and dead-letter topic; publisher/subscriber IAM scoped per-topic.
- Cloud Tasks/Scheduler: HTTP targets require authenticated invocation; handlers verified idempotent under duplicate delivery.
- Firestore (if used): security rules default-deny and tested against the emulator; document sizes verified under the 1 MiB limit.
- All cross-cloud/CI access verified to use Workload Identity Federation — no service-account JSON key files present anywhere in the repo or deploy pipeline.
- Cost guardrails in place: lifecycle rules, right-sized instances, billing budget alerts scoped to this data layer.
- Audit logging enabled and alerting configured for IAM policy changes on all provisioned data services.
