### Uncertain fields

## Infrastructure as code — Terraform/OpenTofu, Pulumi, SST v3, AWS CDK; state, drift, review gates, and when a PaaS dashboard is enough

### identity

- **name**: Infrastructure as code — Terraform/OpenTofu, Pulumi, SST v3, AWS CDK; state, drift, review gates, and when a PaaS dashboard is enough
- **item_id**: iac-and-provisioning
- **cluster**: platforms
- **short_definition**: Choosing whether and how to manage cloud resources declaratively (Terraform/OpenTofu, Pulumi, SST v3, AWS CDK) versus relying on PaaS dashboards and committed config files, with state management, drift control, and CI review gates as the operational core once IaC is adopted.
**scope_in** _What this item covers_:

- Decision criteria for PaaS-dashboard-only vs adopting a dedicated IaC tool
- Terraform and OpenTofu: license history, fork rationale, CLI workflow (init/plan/apply), remote state and locking
- Pulumi: general-purpose language IaC, state backend choices (Pulumi Cloud vs self-managed)
- SST v3 (Ion): Pulumi-based, TypeScript-native, serverless/AWS-first with growing multi-provider support, live lambda dev
- AWS CDK v2: TypeScript-first synthesis to CloudFormation
- GitHub Actions OIDC federation to AWS/GCP/Azure for short-lived plan/apply credentials
- CI review gates for IaC: plan-on-PR, apply-on-merge, plan-as-PR-comment, human approval gates
- State file risks: drift, corruption, loss, lock contention, secret leakage into state
- When this stack (Vercel + Supabase + maybe Cloudflare/Fly) needs IaC at all vs config files as the IaC
**scope_out** _Explicit non-goals and adjacent items that own them_:

- Kubernetes manifests / Helm / GitOps (ArgoCD, Flux) — separate platform-orchestration item
- Configuration management (Ansible, Chef, Puppet) for long-lived VM fleets
- Cloudflare/Fly/Railway/Render dashboard-and-token specifics — covered in sibling PaaS items, only contrasted here for the OIDC story
- Detailed AWS/GCP networking or IAM design patterns beyond what's needed to explain the OIDC trust relationship
- Full CDKTF (CDK for Terraform) evaluation — mentioned only in passing versus CDK-for-CloudFormation
- Cost optimization of the underlying cloud resources themselves (see cost-guardrails item)
- **priority**: P1

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Default to NO dedicated IaC tool for this stack: Vercel (dashboard + vercel.json), Supabase (dashboard + supabase/migrations + config.toml), and a PaaS worker host (fly.toml or wrangler.toml) committed to the repo already constitute reviewable, versioned config — that is 'IaC enough' for a 1-2 person team.
- Adopt OpenTofu (not Terraform) as the default IaC engine the moment real AWS/GCP resources appear (e.g. an S3 bucket + CloudFront for asset storage, a GCP Pub/Sub queue, an RDS instance) — OpenTofu is MPL-2.0, Linux-Foundation-governed, and a drop-in-compatible fork, removing the BUSL license-ambiguity question entirely for a small team that has no HashiCorp commercial relationship.
- Fall back to Terraform proper only if the team already has HCP Terraform / Terraform Cloud paid tooling, Sentinel policy requirements, or a vendor mandate — otherwise there is no reason to accept BUSL terms over OpenTofu's MPL-2.0 for a small greenfield team.
- Pick SST v3 (Ion) when the team is TypeScript-native and wants to go deep on AWS (or Cloudflare) serverless resources with tight DX (live lambda dev, typed resource linking) without hand-writing Pulumi or Terraform HCL.
- Pick Pulumi directly (not through SST) when the team wants general-purpose-language IaC across genuinely multiple clouds/providers beyond what SST's component library covers, or needs custom abstractions SST doesn't expose.
- Pick AWS CDK v2 only when the team is committing hard to AWS-only infrastructure and wants first-class CloudFormation drift detection, StackSets, and AWS-native change sets — accept the CloudFormation-only lock-in as the tradeoff.
- Never introduce Terraform/OpenTofu/Pulumi/CDK purely to manage Vercel or Supabase resources — their Terraform/Pulumi providers exist but add state-management overhead for what a dashboard + committed config file already does adequately at this scale.
- Always require remote state with locking (S3+DynamoDB or S3-native locking, Terraform Cloud/HCP, Pulumi Cloud, or a self-hosted equivalent) the moment more than one person or more than one machine runs plan/apply — local state is a single-environment prototyping convenience only.
- Always wire GitHub Actions OIDC role assumption for the cloud provider before the first CI apply runs — do not start with a long-lived AWS access key in repo secrets 'temporarily.'
- Re-evaluate this default at the review date or the first time a second AWS/GCP account, a compliance audit, or a third infrastructure engineer appears — those are the real triggers, not calendar time alone.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do commit Terraform/OpenTofu/CDK/SST source files to the repo under an infra/ or similar directory with clear environment separation (workspaces, directories, or stacks per environment).
- Do NOT commit .tfstate, .tfstate.backup, .pulumi/ local state, or cdk.out/ synthesized templates containing account-specific values — gitignore these explicitly.
- Do require `plan` output to be visible in the PR (as a comment or CI check summary) before any `apply` runs against a shared environment.
- Do NOT let apply run from an engineer's laptop against staging or production once CI-based apply exists — laptop applies bypass the reviewed-diff guarantee entirely.
- Do pin provider and module versions (`~>` constraints, lockfiles: .terraform.lock.hcl, Pulumi.lock, package-lock.json for CDK/SST) so plan output is reproducible across machines and CI runs.
- Do NOT use `terraform apply -auto-approve` (or the Pulumi/CDK equivalent) in any workflow that touches production without a preceding human-reviewed plan artifact from the same commit.
- Do use remote state with locking from day one of adopting IaC — treat local state as acceptable only for a single-developer, single-environment sandbox that will be destroyed.
- Do NOT hardcode account IDs, role ARNs, or region-specific resource identifiers as string literals scattered through configs — use variables/environment-specific tfvars or stack config files with placeholders reviewed before commit.
- Do scope the CI-assumed IAM role/service account to the minimum permissions the pipeline needs (least privilege on the OIDC-assumed role itself, not just on the resources it creates).
- Do NOT store the OIDC role's trust policy with an overly broad `sub` claim match (e.g. `repo:org/*:*`) — scope it to the specific repo and branch/environment that should be allowed to assume it.
- Do run `fmt`/`validate`/`lint` (tflint, checkov, or tfsec-class static analysis) as a required CI check before plan, catching syntax and known-bad-pattern issues cheaply.
- Do NOT treat a clean `plan` as a substitute for reviewing the actual diff — always read what resources are being created, changed, or destroyed, especially destroy actions on stateful resources (databases, buckets with data).
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- OpenTofu/Terraform: backend + provider with GitHub Actions OIDC role assumption (AWS) ---
# versions.tf
terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.8.0"
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "example-tfstate-bucket"      # placeholder
    key            = "envs/prod/network/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "example-tfstate-locks"        # or use S3-native locking (TF/OpenTofu 1.10+)
    encrypt        = true
  }
  required_providers {
    aws = {
      source  = "hashicorp/aws"
      version = "~> 5.0"
    }
  }
}

provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
  # No static keys. Credentials come from the OIDC-assumed role injected by
  # aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials in CI, or local `aws sso login` for humans.
}

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- State drift: real infrastructure is modified out-of-band (console click-ops, another tool) and no longer matches state, causing surprising diffs or destructive corrective plans on the next apply.
- State file corruption or loss: a failed apply, concurrent write, or backend outage corrupts or deletes the state file, leaving Terraform/Pulumi unable to reconcile what it manages — recovery requires state import/rebuild, which is slow and error-prone.
- Lock contention: two CI runs or an engineer-plus-CI race to acquire the state lock simultaneously, causing hung applies, stale locks left behind after a crashed run, and manual `force-unlock` interventions that themselves risk corruption.
- Unreviewed apply from a laptop: an engineer runs `apply` locally against staging/production outside CI, bypassing the PR review and plan-comment gate, producing infrastructure changes nobody else can see or audit.
- Secrets leaking into the state file: resource attributes (DB passwords, API keys generated by the provider) are stored in plaintext inside .tfstate/.pulumi state by design — a leaked state file or an over-broadly-permissioned state backend is a secrets leak, not just a config leak.
- Provider version drift across environments: dev was applied with an older provider version than prod, producing plans that show unexpected changes purely from provider-schema differences rather than real infra intent.
- Module/provider registry outage or a yanked module version breaking `init` mid-pipeline with no cached fallback.
- CI OIDC trust policy misconfiguration (too broad `sub` claim, or accidentally scoped to `pull_request` triggers) allowing any fork's PR workflow to assume a role with write access to real infrastructure.
- Destroy-and-recreate surprises: a seemingly innocuous attribute change (e.g. an S3 bucket name or an RDS parameter that forces replacement) triggers a destroy of a stateful resource that plan output didn't make obviously destructive-looking without careful reading.
- Long-running apply timeouts in CI causing partial applies — some resources created, pipeline killed, state now reflects a half-applied world that must be manually reconciled.
- Multiple people/tools writing to the same PaaS resource that IaC also manages (e.g. someone edits an env var in the Vercel dashboard that Terraform's Vercel provider also manages) causing perpetual drift.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Introducing Terraform/Pulumi/CDK for a single Vercel + Supabase app with one engineer and no cloud account — pure ceremony with no reviewable-diff benefit over the dashboard's own history.
- Using Terraform Cloud/HCP or Pulumi Cloud's paid tier as a forcing function to look 'enterprise-ready' before there's a team size or compliance need that justifies it.
- Managing Cloudflare, Fly, Railway, or Render resources through their community Terraform providers purely for consistency with an AWS IaC setup, when their native config files (wrangler.toml, fly.toml) already give equivalent review and reproducibility with far less state-management overhead.
- Mixing dashboard click-ops and IaC management of the same resource, producing permanent drift and a plan that never goes clean.
- Copy-pasting a monolithic single-environment Terraform/OpenTofu configuration across dev/staging/prod by hand instead of using workspaces, directories, or a proper module structure — guarantees environment drift over time.
- Storing long-lived cloud provider access keys as GitHub Actions repo secrets when the provider supports OIDC — the exact static-credential anti-pattern this stack's identity-first principle explicitly rejects.
- Running `apply` in the same CI job as `plan` with no human gate in between for any environment that serves real users.
- Treating a green `terraform plan` in CI as equivalent to a green build — nobody actually reads the diff before merge.
- Choosing AWS CDK for a team that also wants multi-cloud flexibility, then fighting CloudFormation-only lock-in later.
- Vendoring a heavyweight policy-as-code framework (Sentinel/OPA) before the team has more than a couple of IaC-touching engineers to actually author and maintain policies.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- OpenTofu — MPL-2.0, Linux Foundation-governed Terraform fork; drop-in CLI-compatible, default recommendation for this stack once IaC is needed.
- Terraform (HashiCorp) — BUSL-1.1 licensed since August 2023; still the largest ecosystem and HCP Terraform's managed offering.
- Pulumi — general-purpose-language IaC (TypeScript, Python, Go, etc.), Pulumi Cloud or self-managed state backend (S3, Azure Blob, GCS, local).
- SST v3 (Ion) — TypeScript-native framework built on Pulumi's engine, AWS- and Cloudflare-first component library, live lambda dev, resource linking.
- AWS CDK v2 — TypeScript-first, synthesizes to CloudFormation, native AWS drift detection and StackSets.
- CDKTF (CDK for Terraform) — CDK-style authoring that synthesizes to Terraform/OpenTofu JSON instead of CloudFormation; niche middle ground, not a primary recommendation here.
- Atlantis — self-hosted, open-source Terraform/OpenTofu PR automation: plan-on-PR-comment, apply-on-comment-command, locking.
- Spacelift — managed IaC orchestration (Terraform/OpenTofu/Pulumi/CDK) with policy-as-code, drift detection, and PR-comment plans.
- env0 — managed IaC orchestration similar to Spacelift, Terraform/OpenTofu/CDK focused.
- Infracost — cost-estimation diff tool that comments projected cost deltas on Terraform/OpenTofu PRs.
- tflint / checkov / tfsec / trivy config — static analysis and security-linting for HCL before plan.
- aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials, opentofu/setup-opentofu, hashicorp/setup-terraform — GitHub Actions building blocks for the CI workflow in config_skeleton.
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Terraform v1 provider protocol and older Terraform CLI versions (pre-1.x) — long since deprecated, not relevant to a greenfield project.
- AWS CDK v1 — deprecated/EOL; any new AWS CDK work must start on v2.
- SST v2 (the CDK-based generation) — superseded by SST v3 (Ion) on Pulumi; new projects should start on v3.
- Long-lived static cloud credentials in CI (AWS access keys, GCP service account JSON keys stored as repo secrets) — not formally deprecated by providers but actively discouraged industry-wide in favor of OIDC federation; treat as a legacy pattern to migrate away from proactively.
- Terraform's classic `terraform_remote_state` cross-state data source patterns are increasingly superseded by explicit outputs + `tofu`/`terraform` variables or Terragrunt-style dependency wiring in larger setups — watch if the stack grows multi-state.
- Community Terraform providers for niche PaaS platforms (Vercel, Fly, Railway) are maintained by third parties, not the vendors themselves, in most cases — watch for abandonment/staleness before depending on one for anything load-bearing.

### security

- **identity_model**: IaC CI/CD for AWS/GCP/Azure is the one area of this platform cluster where the identity-first, no-long-lived-static-credentials goal is fully achievable today: GitHub Actions issues a short-lived, workflow-scoped OIDC JWT (aud=sts.amazonaws.com or the GCP/Azure equivalent); the cloud provider's IAM trust policy validates the token's issuer, audience, and `sub` claim (which encodes the specific repo, and optionally branch/environment/tag) before minting temporary credentials (AWS STS AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity, GCP Workload Identity Federation, Azure federated credentials) scoped to exactly the permissions the plan/apply job needs, expiring automatically at the end of the run. No static access key or service-account JSON ever touches GitHub Secrets. Terraform, OpenTofu, Pulumi, and AWS CDK all consume these ambient credentials transparently via each cloud SDK's default credential chain — no IaC-tool-specific credential wiring is required beyond configuring the CI step that requests the token and assumes the role. This contrasts sharply with the sibling PaaS platforms in this cluster (Cloudflare, Fly, Railway, Render): those mostly authenticate CI via a long-lived API token stored as a CI secret, because they don't yet offer OIDC federation for their control planes — so the identity-first bar this module can clear (true short-lived, scoped, auditable credentials) is not yet reachable for the PaaS layer of this same stack, only for the AWS/GCP/Azure resources IaC manages.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Overly broad OIDC trust policy `sub` claim lets any branch or even any fork's pull_request workflow assume the CI role, escalating a low-trust PR into infrastructure write access.
- State file exposure (misconfigured S3 bucket ACL, leaked backend credentials) exposing plaintext secrets embedded in resource attributes.
- Compromised CI runner or a malicious third-party GitHub Action in the plan/apply pipeline exfiltrating the OIDC-derived temporary credentials during their short validity window.
- A dependency-confusion or typosquatted Terraform/Pulumi provider or module pulled from a public registry executing arbitrary code during `init`/`plan`.
- Insider or compromised-laptop apply bypassing CI entirely because local credentials (assumed via long-lived AWS SSO or an old access key) still have apply-level permissions.
- Lock file or state backend denial-of-service (accidental or malicious) blocking all applies by holding a lock indefinitely.
- Drift introduced by an attacker with console access making an unreviewed change that the next `apply` then either silently reverts (masking the incident) or, worse, treats as the new desired baseline if someone runs `terraform refresh`/imports it.
- A coding agent given IaC-authoring permissions inventing or hardcoding real-looking account IDs/ARNs that get committed and later assumed to be real, or worse, being granted enough CI scope to auto-apply to production.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Scope OIDC trust policy `sub` claims tightly to `repo:org/repo:ref:refs/heads/main` (and a distinct condition for PR-triggered plan-only workflows with a read-only role) rather than wildcarding branches or repos.
- Encrypt state at rest (S3 SSE, GCS default encryption) and restrict the state backend's bucket/table to only the CI role and a small break-glass admin group — never public or org-wide readable.
- Pin every third-party GitHub Action and Terraform/Pulumi module/provider to an exact version (and ideally a commit SHA for Actions), consistent with this repo's existing immutable-SHA pinning rule.
- Give the plan-only (PR) role read-only / plan-level IAM permissions and a separate, more privileged apply-only role gated behind the main-branch, environment-protected job — least privilege split between plan and apply identities.
- Require GitHub Environment protection rules (named reviewers) on the apply job for any environment serving real users, in addition to the plan-as-PR-comment review.
- Disable or tightly restrict any long-lived human AWS/GCP credentials capable of apply-level changes; route human infra work through the same PR-and-CI path or a short-lived SSO session, not a standing access key.
- Run drift-detection on a schedule (`terraform plan`/`tofu plan` as a scheduled CI job with no apply, alerting on non-empty diffs) so unreviewed console changes surface quickly instead of silently accumulating.
- Never let a coding agent run `apply` unattended against a real environment — restrict agent tool permissions to generating/editing IaC source and running `plan`/`validate`/`fmt`; require human review and human-triggered merge/apply for anything beyond a disposable sandbox.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- MUST NOT commit .tfstate, .tfstate.backup, Pulumi state exports, or cdk.out/ synthesized templates to the repository — verify .gitignore covers these before first commit in a new infra directory.
- MUST NOT hardcode real cloud account IDs, role ARNs, VPC IDs, or other environment-specific identifiers into committed source — use variables, tfvars files kept out of version control (or per-environment files clearly marked as environment-specific and reviewed), or documented placeholders.
- MUST NOT embed static cloud credentials (access keys, service account JSON) anywhere in IaC source, CI workflow files, or example configs — always route through OIDC role assumption.
- MUST always configure remote state with locking as part of the initial backend setup for any environment beyond a disposable single-developer sandbox — never leave state local as a 'temporary' default.
- MUST always run `fmt`, `validate`, and `plan` and present the plan output for human review before proposing or executing `apply` against any shared or production environment.
- MUST NOT auto-apply to production, or to any environment serving real users, without an explicit human approval step in the loop — a human-reviewed diff is a hard gate, not a suggestion.
- MUST flag, rather than silently 'fix,' any plan that shows a destroy/replace action on a stateful resource (database, storage bucket, anything holding user data) — surface it clearly for explicit human confirmation.
- SHOULD default to proposing the PaaS-dashboard-plus-config-file path first for this stack, and only introduce a dedicated IaC tool when the decision_gates in this module are actually met — do not reach for Terraform/Pulumi/CDK reflexively because the task mentions 'infrastructure.'
- SHOULD prefer OpenTofu over Terraform when proposing new IaC tooling from scratch, per this module's default, unless the user has an existing Terraform Cloud/HCP relationship.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- PR gate: `fmt -check`, `validate`, and `plan` must all pass before merge is allowed; plan output posted as a PR comment or CI check annotation for human review.
- Merge-to-main gate: `apply` runs only after merge, using the plan artifact generated from the exact merged commit (not a re-plan that could drift from what was reviewed).
- Production environment gate: GitHub Environment protection rule requiring a named human reviewer's approval before the apply job proceeds, independent of the PR review that already happened.
- Destructive-change gate: CI should highlight (via a script grepping plan JSON output for `"action": "delete"` or `"replace"`) any destroy/replace of a stateful resource and require explicit acknowledgment, not just a passing plan.
- State-lock timeout gate: CI apply jobs should have a reasonable timeout and a documented manual `force-unlock` runbook rather than indefinite hangs on lock contention.
- Drift-detection gate: a scheduled (e.g. daily) plan-only job across all managed environments that alerts (not applies) on any non-empty diff.
- Cost-estimate gate (optional, once Infracost is adopted): comment projected cost delta on the PR alongside the plan, with a manual-approval threshold for large increases.
- Static-analysis gate: tflint/checkov/tfsec-class scan on every PR touching infra source, independent of and prior to plan.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- Drift detection: scheduled plan-only CI runs (or a third-party tool like Spacelift/env0's built-in drift detection) comparing live infrastructure to state on a cadence, alerting on unexpected diffs.
- Deploy-pipeline tracing: instrument the plan/apply GitHub Actions workflow itself with basic timing/success metrics (job duration, plan-vs-apply lag, failure rate) shipped to whatever observability stack the rest of the platform uses; full OTel instrumentation of the IaC pipeline itself is likely overkill for this stack's scale and should be treated as optional, not a default.
- Cost estimation: Infracost integrated into the PR workflow to show projected monthly cost delta before merge, catching runaway-resource mistakes (e.g. an oversized instance type) at review time rather than on the next bill.
- State backend audit logging: enable access logging on the S3 state bucket (or equivalent) and on the OIDC-assumed IAM role's CloudTrail events, so every plan/apply's credential use and state read/write is independently auditable.
- Plan/apply history: HCP Terraform, Pulumi Cloud, Spacelift, or env0 all provide a built-in run history UI; the hand-rolled GitHub Actions approach relies on the Actions run log and PR comment history as the audit trail instead — sufficient at small scale but weaker for long-term searchability.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- Run Infracost (or equivalent) on every infra PR to surface projected cost delta before merge, not after the first bill.
- Tag every IaC-managed resource with an owner/environment/cost-center tag at creation time so cost allocation doesn't require after-the-fact archaeology.
- Set budget alerts (AWS Budgets, GCP Billing budgets) independent of IaC, as a backstop against a mis-sized resource slipping through review.
- Avoid provisioning HA/multi-AZ or over-provisioned instance sizes 'to be safe' in early-stage environments — default to the smallest viable size and scale up with evidence.
- Destroy ephemeral/preview environments automatically (TTL-based cleanup workflow) rather than letting per-PR infrastructure accumulate indefinitely.
- Avoid paying for HCP Terraform, Pulumi Cloud, Spacelift, or env0 team/enterprise tiers until the team size or workflow complexity in this module's decision_gates actually justifies it — free tiers or the hand-rolled GitHub Actions workflow suffice below that threshold.
- Watch for state-backend costs creeping (S3 request costs, DynamoDB read/write capacity) at scale — trivial at this stack's size but worth a periodic sanity check as the number of environments/states grows.
- Avoid over-provisioning IAM permissions on the CI role 'to avoid future friction' — broader permissions become a standing cost/risk even when unused.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Repo contains .tf/.tf.json files, a pulumi/ directory with Pulumi.yaml, an sst.config.ts, or a cdk.json/lib/*-stack.ts — signals a dedicated IaC tool is already in play and this module's CI-gate and identity-model guidance applies directly.
- Repo contains a committed .tfstate file anywhere — hard failure signal, must be removed from history and backend migrated to remote state immediately.
- GitHub Actions workflow references `aws-access-key-id`/`aws-secret-access-key` as repo secrets in an infra-touching job — signals static-credential anti-pattern that should be migrated to OIDC.
- No infra/, terraform/, pulumi/, or equivalent directory and no AWS/GCP account references anywhere in the repo or env vars — signals the PaaS-dashboard-is-enough default correctly applies, no action needed.
- A Terraform/Pulumi provider block for Vercel, Fly, Railway, Cloudflare Pages, or Render present alongside native config files for the same platform (vercel.json, fly.toml) — signals possible redundant/duplicate management worth flagging.
- CI workflow runs `terraform apply` or `pulumi up` with no preceding required PR review or environment-protection gate — signals a missing CI gate per this module's ci_gates.
- `terraform.lock.hcl`/`Pulumi.lock` missing or not committed — signals unpinned provider versions, a reproducibility risk.
- OIDC trust policy or GitHub Actions `permissions: id-token: write` present but `sub` claim condition wildcarded or missing — signals an over-broad trust relationship worth tightening.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- For this greenfield TS/Next.js + Python + Supabase stack, default to no dedicated IaC tool — PaaS dashboards plus committed config files (vercel.json, supabase config, fly.toml/wrangler.toml) are the IaC for the PaaS layer at 1-2 person scale.
- The trigger to adopt IaC is any real AWS/GCP resource, multi-environment parity needs, a compliance requirement for reviewable diffs, or a team beyond 2 people — not calendar time or aesthetic preference.
- Default IaC engine when adopted: OpenTofu, not Terraform — MPL-2.0 and Linux-Foundation-governed, drop-in CLI-compatible, removes the BUSL license-ambiguity question for a small team with no HashiCorp commercial relationship.
- For AWS-serverless-heavy work from a TypeScript-native team, SST v3 (Ion) is the highest-leverage choice: Pulumi's engine underneath, opinionated high-level components, typed resource linking, and live lambda dev.
- GitHub Actions OIDC federation to AWS/GCP/Azure is the one part of this whole platform cluster where the identity-first, no-static-credentials goal is fully achievable today — wire it from the first CI apply, never start with a stored access key.
- Remote state with locking is non-negotiable the instant more than one person or machine runs plan/apply — local state is single-developer-sandbox-only.
- The state file itself is a secrets-bearing artifact (plaintext resource attributes like DB passwords) — treat its backend access control with the same seriousness as a secrets manager.
- Plan-review-then-apply is the core CI gate: plan on every PR as a visible comment/check, apply only after merge and (for production) an explicit human-reviewer approval step distinct from the PR review.
- Contrast note for the skill's broader platform picture: this item's clean OIDC identity story does not extend to the sibling Cloudflare/Fly/Railway/Render PaaS items, which still rely on long-lived API tokens in CI secrets — don't assume identity-first parity across the whole stack.
- A coding agent must never be allowed to auto-apply to a production or shared environment; its role is generating/editing IaC source and running plan/validate, with a human gating the actual apply.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Confirm whether any AWS/GCP resources actually exist yet — if not, stop here and use dashboards + config files, do not adopt IaC preemptively.
- If adopting IaC: choose OpenTofu by default; document the specific reason if choosing Terraform, Pulumi, SST, or CDK instead.
- Set up remote state backend with locking before writing the first real resource block.
- Configure GitHub Actions OIDC trust policy scoped to the specific repo and branch/environment; verify no static cloud credentials remain in repo secrets.
- Wire the plan-on-PR / apply-on-merge GitHub Actions workflow with plan output posted as a PR comment.
- Add a GitHub Environment protection rule with named reviewer approval on the apply job for production.
- Verify .gitignore excludes all state files, and confirm none have ever been committed (check git history, not just the current tree).
- Pin all provider/module versions and third-party Action versions; commit the resulting lockfile.
- Add a static-analysis (tflint/checkov-class) CI step ahead of plan.
- Decide and document the drift-detection cadence (scheduled plan-only job) even if minimal at launch.
- Revisit this decision at review_by date or immediately on the first new AWS/GCP account, compliance ask, or third infra-touching engineer, whichever comes first.

### Uncertain fields

## Cross-provider glue — secret managers vs env vars, OIDC everywhere, OTel export targets, FinOps guardrails, egress/bandwidth traps, exit and lock-in

### identity

- **name**: Cross-provider glue — secret managers vs env vars, OIDC everywhere, OTel export targets, FinOps guardrails, egress/bandwidth traps, exit and lock-in
- **item_id**: multicloud-secrets-observability-cost
- **cluster**: platforms
- **short_definition**: The cross-cutting policy layer that ties together identity federation, secret storage, telemetry export, and cost control across every provider in a multi-provider stack (Vercel + Cloudflare/Fly/Railway/Render + Supabase + possibly AWS/GCP) — it defines the shared rules (OIDC where available, scoped tokens where not, one OTel Collector fan-out, lightweight FinOps) rather than any single provider's implementation.
**scope_in** _What this item covers_:

- Decision policy for when GitHub Actions OIDC is available vs when a static provider token is unavoidable
- How to treat and protect static API tokens for providers without OIDC support
- The OpenTelemetry Collector as the single fan-out point for traces/metrics/logs across providers
- OTLP as the vendor-neutral wire format connecting services to the Collector and the Collector to backends
- FinOps as a lightweight recurring practice: cost visibility, budget alerts, anomaly detection across providers
- Egress/bandwidth pricing traps and free-tier cliffs that appear specifically because multiple providers are combined
- Credential lifecycle policy (rotation cadence, incident rotation) applied uniformly across providers
- Cross-provider alerting taxonomy (what counts as a cost or auth anomaly worth paging on)
**scope_out** _Explicit non-goals and adjacent items that own them_:

- AWS-specific IAM/OIDC configuration mechanics (IAM OIDC provider setup, role trust policy syntax) — owned by aws-identity-iam-oidc
- GCP-specific Workload Identity Federation pool/provider configuration — owned by gcp-identity-wif
- Vercel's own env var UI, team/project token scoping mechanics, and preview/prod separation — owned by the Vercel item and deploy-ops.md
- Cloudflare's token model, Workers secrets, and R2 specifics — owned by the Cloudflare item
- Fly.io/Railway/Render specific deploy tokens, PaaS build/deploy flow — owned by the PaaS item
- Supabase service-role key handling and RLS (owned by backend-database.md / backend-auth.md)
- Container, node, and network hardening (owned by infra-security.md)
- This item owns the glue and the decision policy, not any single provider's implementation detail
- **priority**: P1

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Use GitHub Actions OIDC to every provider that supports it (AWS via IAM OIDC provider + assume-role, GCP via Workload Identity Federation, Azure via federated credentials) — zero long-lived cloud secrets for these
- For providers that only support static API tokens (Cloudflare, Fly.io, Railway, Render, Netlify, Vercel as of 2026), treat the token itself as the credential to protect: store in GitHub encrypted secrets scoped to a GitHub Environment, not a repo-wide secret
- Scope every static token to the minimum project/team/zone it needs — never an account-wide or org-wide token for a CI job
- Rotate static provider tokens on a calendar (90 days is a reasonable default) and immediately on any suspected leak or personnel change
- Use GitHub Environments with required reviewers for any deploy job that depends on a static token, as the compensating control for the missing OIDC layer
- Run a single OTel Collector as the fan-out point; instrument services with OTLP only, never wire a service directly to multiple vendor SDKs
- Pick one telemetry backend as primary (Grafana Cloud, Honeycomb, Datadog, or self-hosted) and treat additional exporters as optional secondary sinks configured at the Collector, not in application code
- Treat FinOps as a lightweight recurring practice at this stage: a monthly cost review, one budget alert per provider, and a shared dashboard/spreadsheet — not a dedicated FinOps tool or role
- Never duplicate a secret's plaintext value across two stores manually; if it must exist in two places, source one from the other (e.g., Vault -> injected as Vercel env var at deploy time) rather than pasting twice
- Default to provider-native secret stores (Vercel/Supabase/GitHub/Cloudflare encrypted secrets) until there are 3+ providers or a compliance requirement forces centralization
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Introduce a dedicated secret manager (Vault, Doppler, Infisical) only when: (a) 3+ providers each have their own secret store and rotation is manually falling behind, OR (b) a compliance/audit requirement needs a single access log across all secrets, OR (c) secrets need to be shared between CI, multiple runtime environments, and local dev consistently. Below that, native per-provider stores plus a rotation spreadsheet are sufficient and simpler to reason about
- Multi-cloud (adding AWS/GCP alongside the core Vercel+Supabase+PaaS stack) is justified only for a specific capability gap the core stack cannot fill (e.g., a GPU workload, a specific managed ML service, a data residency requirement) — never adopt a second cloud for redundancy alone at greenfield stage; that trades simplicity for a hedge against a risk that hasn't materialized
- Invest in FinOps tooling (a dedicated cost platform, chargeback automation, FOCUS-normalized billing exports) only once monthly cloud spend crosses a threshold where a human spreadsheet review takes more than an hour or misses real anomalies (commonly cited around $10k-$50k/mo spend, or 3+ billed accounts) — below that, provider-native budget alerts plus a spreadsheet cover the Inform phase of FinOps adequately
- Move from 'per-provider budget alert' to 'one FinOps dashboard' only when there are 3+ billed providers; consolidating two providers' bills is rarely worth the tooling overhead
- Adopt Vault specifically (over Doppler/Infisical) only when dynamic/leased secrets (short-lived DB credentials, PKI issuance) are actually needed — otherwise its operational overhead (unseal, HA storage backend) is not justified for a small team
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do check whether a target provider supports GitHub OIDC before writing any deploy workflow for it; don't default to pasting a long-lived key into repo secrets out of habit
- Do scope static tokens (Cloudflare API token, Fly API token, Railway token) to the single project/zone/app they deploy; don't reuse one all-access token across every CI job for convenience
- Do put static-token deploy jobs behind a GitHub Environment with protection rules; don't let any workflow triggered by a plain push to main use a production-scoped static token with no review gate
- Do route all service telemetry through one OTel Collector; don't install five separate vendor SDKs (Datadog agent, Sentry SDK, custom logger, provider-native metrics) that each need their own credentials and config
- Do set a budget alert on every provider the moment an account is created; don't wait until the first surprising invoice to configure billing alerts
- Do rotate a secret immediately when it's committed to git history, even if the commit was reverted or force-pushed away; don't treat 'removed from HEAD' as equivalent to 'not leaked'
- Do keep a single source-of-truth registry of which secret lives where (even a spreadsheet); don't let three team members each independently paste the same Stripe key into four different provider dashboards
- Do treat Cloudflare R2's zero egress-fee model as a reason to route large-object/CDN-adjacent traffic through it; don't assume all object storage or CDN egress pricing works the same way
- Do review combined free-tier usage monthly when running on 2+ free tiers simultaneously; don't assume free tiers 'just work' together with no interaction effects on usage counting
- Do document the OIDC coverage gap explicitly in onboarding material; don't let a new engineer assume 'OIDC everywhere' is already fully implemented when roughly half the stack's providers don't support it
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
# --- .github/workflows/deploy.yml ---
# Illustrates: shared OIDC permission block across jobs targeting AWS and GCP,
# plus a static-token job for a provider without OIDC support (e.g. Cloudflare/Fly/Railway).
# All values are placeholders.

name: deploy

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]

permissions:
  contents: read
  id-token: write   # required for every OIDC-federated job below

jobs:
  deploy-aws:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: production
    permissions:
      id-token: write
      contents: read
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@<pinned-sha>
      - name: Assume AWS role via OIDC
        uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@<pinned-sha>
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::<ACCOUNT_ID>:role/<GITHUB_OIDC_DEPLOY_ROLE>
          aws-region: <AWS_REGION>
          # no access-key-id / secret-access-key — token is minted per-job and expires
      - name: Deploy to AWS
        run: <deploy-command>

  deploy-gcp:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: production
    permissions:
      id-token: write
      contents: read
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@<pinned-sha>
      - name: Authenticate to GCP via Workload Identity Federation
        uses: google-github-actions/auth@<pinned-sha>
        with:
          workload_identity_provider: projects/<PROJECT_NUMBER>/locations/global/workloadIdentityPools/<POOL_ID>/providers/<PROVIDER_ID>
          service_account: <SA_NAME>@<PROJECT_ID>.iam.gserviceaccount.com
      - name: Deploy to GCP
        run: <deploy-command>

  deploy-static-token-provider:
    # No GitHub OIDC support as of 2026 (e.g. Cloudflare / Fly.io / Railway / Render / Vercel).
    # Guardrail: GitHub Environment with required reviewers + minimally scoped token + calendar rotation.
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: production   # protection rules (required reviewers) live here
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@<pinned-sha>
      - name: Deploy
        env:
          DEPLOY_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.SCOPED_PROVIDER_DEPLOY_TOKEN }}  # scoped to one project, rotated every 90d
        run: <provider-cli> deploy --token "$DEPLOY_TOKEN"

---
# --- otel-collector-config.yaml ---
# Illustrates: OTLP receiver, fan-out to two exporters (placeholder endpoints).

receivers:
  otlp:
    protocols:
      grpc:
        endpoint: 0.0.0.0:4317
      http:
        endpoint: 0.0.0.0:4318

processors:
  batch: {}
  attributes/redact:
    actions:
      - key: http.request.header.authorization
        action: delete

exporters:
  otlphttp/primary_backend:
    endpoint: https://<PRIMARY_BACKEND_OTLP_ENDPOINT>
    headers:
      Authorization: Bearer ${env:PRIMARY_BACKEND_API_KEY}
  otlphttp/secondary_backend:
    endpoint: https://<SECONDARY_BACKEND_OTLP_ENDPOINT>
    headers:
      Authorization: Bearer ${env:SECONDARY_BACKEND_API_KEY}

service:
  pipelines:
    traces:
      receivers: [otlp]
      processors: [attributes/redact, batch]
      exporters: [otlphttp/primary_backend, otlphttp/secondary_backend]
    metrics:
      receivers: [otlp]
      processors: [batch]
      exporters: [otlphttp/primary_backend]
    logs:
      receivers: [otlp]
      processors: [attributes/redact, batch]
      exporters: [otlphttp/primary_backend]

---
# --- budget-alert-policy.pseudo ---
# Not tied to one provider; apply the equivalent construct in each provider's billing console.

budget_alert:
  name: "monthly-cloud-spend-guardrail"
  scope: <provider-account-or-project>
  amount_monthly_usd: <THRESHOLD>
  thresholds:
    - percent: 50
      notify: ["#eng-alerts"]
    - percent: 90
      notify: ["#eng-alerts", "oncall-email"]
    - percent: 100
      notify: ["#eng-alerts", "oncall-email", "founder-sms"]
  anomaly_detection: true   # where the provider supports it (e.g. AWS Cost Anomaly Detection)
  review_cadence: monthly   # roll up all provider alerts into one shared sheet/dashboard

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- A workflow is written for a provider assuming OIDC support that doesn't actually exist, discovered only when the deploy fails in CI
- A static provider token is scoped account-wide because scoping felt like extra setup, and it later leaks via a misconfigured log line, exposing the entire account
- Secrets are pasted into three different providers' dashboards by three different engineers, drift silently, and a rotation only updates one of the three copies
- Every service ships its own vendor SDK (Sentry + Datadog + a custom logger) with its own API key, quadrupling the credential surface with no single point of rotation or revocation
- Free tier usage across two providers (e.g. a CDN free tier and a compute free tier) combines in a way neither provider's dashboard shows together, and the bill spikes only when both are exceeded simultaneously
- NAT gateway or CDN egress costs accumulate silently for weeks because no budget alert was configured on that specific provider account
- A GitHub Environment protection rule is configured but the required reviewers list is stale (departed engineer), silently degrading the review gate to a rubber stamp
- Telemetry data with PII/secrets in it (e.g. an Authorization header) is exported unredacted to a third-party backend because the OTel Collector has no redaction processor configured
- Cost anomalies go unnoticed because each provider's bill is reviewed independently and no one looks at the combined trend
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- One org-wide Cloudflare/Vercel/Fly API token shared across every CI workflow because separate tokens felt like busywork
- Wiring every microservice directly to a vendor's proprietary tracing SDK instead of emitting OTLP to a shared Collector
- Treating FinOps as a one-time setup project rather than a recurring monthly practice
- Using a dedicated secret manager (Vault) from day one for a 2-provider stack when native secret stores would suffice, adding operational overhead with no corresponding benefit
- No budget alert on any provider until the first bill shock forces one
- Assuming 'OIDC everywhere' is fully achieved and never documenting which providers are still on static tokens
- Copy-pasting the same API key into local .env, CI secrets, and a teammate's Slack DM as 'the fast way to share it'
- Letting a secret manager itself become a single point of failure with no break-glass access plan if it goes down or the account is locked

### tooling

- **versions_as_of**: 2026-08-29
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Whether any of Cloudflare, Fly.io, Railway, Render, Netlify, or Vercel adds native GitHub Actions OIDC support during 2026 — this would close part of the documented OIDC coverage gap and should trigger an update to this item and the sibling provider items
- FinOps Foundation FOCUS spec maturity and adoption — check whether more providers export FOCUS-normalized billing data, which would make cross-provider cost dashboards materially easier
- OpenTelemetry Collector contrib vs core distribution changes — exporter/receiver components sometimes move between distributions or get deprecated
- GitHub OIDC subject claim format changes (e.g. immutable repository ID requirements) — GitHub has iterated on recommended `sub` claim patterns; trust policies written against an older pattern may need updates

### security

- **identity_model**: This is the core split the item exists to document. (1) Providers with real OIDC/workload-identity federation from GitHub Actions: AWS (IAM OIDC identity provider + role trust policy + sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity), GCP (Workload Identity Federation pool + provider + service-account impersonation), and Azure (federated credentials on an app registration). For these, GitHub mints a short-lived, job-scoped JWT; the cloud provider validates the `sub`/`aud` claims against a trust policy and issues a temporary credential that expires when the job ends. Zero long-lived cloud secrets are stored in GitHub for these providers — this is a strict security upgrade over static keys and should be the default for every job targeting AWS or GCP. (2) Providers WITHOUT GitHub OIDC support as of 2026: Cloudflare, Fly.io, Railway, Render, Netlify, and Vercel all require a static API token or personal/deploy token stored as a GitHub encrypted secret to deploy from CI. There is no federation handshake available — the token itself IS the long-lived credential. For these, the guardrail shifts from 'eliminate the secret' to 'contain the blast radius of the secret': scope the token to the single project/zone/team it needs (never account-wide), store it in a GitHub Environment (not a bare repo secret) so required-reviewer protection rules apply to the job that uses it, and rotate it on a fixed calendar (90 days is a reasonable default) plus immediately on any suspected leak. Reconciling with infra-security.md's Cloud IAM least-privilege section: that section's language ('No long-lived personal tokens in CI. Prefer OIDC federation... where a static secret is unavoidable, store it in the provider's secret store, scope it minimally, and rotate it') is fully consistent with this item — infra-security.md already anticipates the static-secret case as the accepted fallback, not a violation. The important clarification this item adds is that a scoped MACHINE/deploy token (created specifically for CI, minimally scoped, calendar-rotated) is categorically different from a PERSONAL token (an individual engineer's own credential reused for automation) — infra-security.md's anti-pattern list specifically flags 'personal PAT with org-wide scope' as the violation, not 'scoped machine token where OIDC is unavailable.' Roughly half of a typical greenfield stack's providers (Cloudflare/Fly/Railway/Render/Netlify/Vercel) fall into bucket (2). This means the outline's aspirational framing of 'OIDC everywhere' cannot currently be fully realized in this stack — that is a documented, expected gap given the current state of these providers' platforms as of 2026, not an oversight or a sign of incomplete implementation.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Credential sprawl: the same or related secrets exist in GitHub secrets, a provider dashboard, a teammate's local .env, and possibly a secret manager, with no single source of truth for which is current
- A leaked long-lived token on ANY provider that doesn't support OIDC (Cloudflare, Fly, Railway, Render, Netlify, Vercel) is the single highest-value target in the stack, precisely because it cannot be replaced by a short-lived federated credential
- Secrets duplicated in multiple places going stale: a rotation updates the secret manager but not the copy pasted directly into a provider's dashboard months earlier, leaving a stale, unrevoked credential live
- OTel Collector exporting sensitive header/attribute data (auth tokens, PII) to a third-party observability backend because no redaction processor was configured
- Cost-based reconnaissance/abuse: an attacker triggers expensive operations (large egress, compute scale-out) specifically to run up a bill or mask other activity inside a spend spike
- A departed engineer's personal access token still working because rotation is aspirational rather than scheduled
- Static-token CI jobs with no GitHub Environment protection rules, meaning a compromised PR-triggered workflow can exfiltrate the token or use it directly
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Maintain one canonical registry (even a simple spreadsheet or a secret manager's own listing) of every secret, which provider issued it, where it's stored, and its last-rotated date
- For every non-OIDC provider, put the deploy job behind a GitHub Environment with required reviewers and restrict which branches can trigger it
- Scope every static token to the single project/zone/team it needs; verify this at issuance time, not after an incident
- Rotate on a calendar (90 days default) and treat 'committed then removed' the same as 'leaked' per infra-security.md
- Add a redaction/attribute-filtering processor to the OTel Collector pipeline before any exporter, stripping auth headers and known PII fields
- Configure a budget alert with anomaly detection on every provider account at creation time, not after the first surprising invoice
- Use OIDC for every provider that supports it, eliminating the largest share of long-lived secrets outright rather than trying to mitigate their risk after the fact
- Require gitleaks/push-protection in CI (already a hard gate per infra-security.md) so a secret committed to any repo is caught before merge
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Before generating any deployment workflow, check whether the target provider supports GitHub Actions OIDC (AWS, GCP, Azure do; Cloudflare, Fly.io, Railway, Render, Netlify, Vercel do not as of 2026) and default to OIDC when supported
- Never generate a workflow that stores a static long-lived credential as a repo-level secret for a provider that supports OIDC — use OIDC unless the user explicitly overrides with a stated reason
- For any provider requiring a static token, generate the deploy job to run under a GitHub Environment with protection rules, not a bare workflow trigger
- Never generate a workflow step that echoes, prints, or logs a secret value (including via `echo $TOKEN`, debug-mode CLI flags, or accidental inclusion in error output)
- Default to the most narrowly scoped token/role the target action needs (single project/zone, minimal permission set) rather than a broad or default-admin credential
- When generating an OTel instrumentation snippet, point it at the local Collector endpoint (OTLP over localhost:4317/4318) rather than hardcoding a vendor SDK and API key directly into application code
- When generating a budget/cost-alert config, always include at least one threshold below 100% (e.g. 50%, 90%) so the alert is a warning, not a fire alarm after the fact
- Flag to the user, rather than silently working around it, any case where a requested provider genuinely has no OIDC path and a static secret is the only option

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- Every workflow job that assumes a cloud-provider role via OIDC must declare `permissions: id-token: write` explicitly and scope it to the minimum job, never at the workflow root, so unrelated jobs can't mint credentials.
- Workflows that use a static provider token (Cloudflare, Fly, Railway, Render, Netlify, Vercel) must read it from a GitHub Environment with required reviewers on protected branches — this is the review gate substituting for the OIDC step-up these providers lack.
- Run a secret-scan (gitleaks-class) on every PR touching CI workflow files or IaC before merge — a workflow edit is the highest-leverage place to accidentally hardcode a token.
- Require `terraform plan` / `tofu plan` (or the relevant provider's dry-run) output posted as a PR comment for any change touching cross-provider glue (OTel collector config, secret-manager policy) before apply.
- Validate the OTel Collector config (`otelcol validate --config`) in CI before deploying a new collector version so a bad exporter config doesn't silently drop telemetry in production.
- Cost-anomaly check: run a budget/cost-diff step against the prior week's spend baseline on a schedule (not per-PR) and fail loudly (Slack/GitHub issue) rather than silently emailing a bill.
- Rotate-and-verify gate: a scheduled CI job that checks the age of every static provider token against its documented rotation window and opens an issue when a token is overdue.
- Cross-provider drift check: a scheduled job comparing declared IaC state (Terraform/OpenTofu) against live cloud state for the providers that support it, failing loudly on unexpected drift.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:

- Default pattern: every service emits OTLP (traces, metrics, logs) to a local/sidecar OpenTelemetry Collector; the Collector is the only component that holds backend credentials and fans out to one or more real destinations (Grafana Cloud, Honeycomb, Datadog, self-hosted)
- OTLP is the vendor-neutral wire format — instrumentation libraries should target OTLP, not a specific vendor SDK, so the backend can be swapped by changing Collector config alone, with zero application code changes
- Cross-provider alerts worth wiring: 5xx/error-rate spikes on any provider's compute/edge layer, authentication/authorization failure clusters on any provider (a spike often indicates credential compromise attempts), and cost anomalies (sudden spend deviation) flagged per-provider and, once mature, on a consolidated view
- The Collector pipeline should include a redaction/attribute-filtering processor before export, since telemetry is a common accidental leak path for secrets and PII (headers, query strings, log bodies)
- Batch and retry-with-backoff-and-jitter are handled by the Collector per the OTLP spec, so instrumented services don't need bespoke retry logic for telemetry export
- Until 3+ providers are in play, per-provider native dashboards plus the Collector's own telemetry is sufficient; don't build a consolidated cross-provider dashboard prematurely
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- AWS NAT Gateway + data transfer charges are a classic silent cost trap: per-GB processing fees plus data transfer charges compound quickly for any workload routing meaningful traffic through a NAT gateway; consider VPC endpoints or architecture that avoids NAT for high-volume paths
- GCP egress (data leaving a GCP region/network to the internet or another cloud) is billed per GB and easy to miss until a workload's traffic pattern changes (e.g. a new cross-region replica or a spike in outbound API calls)
- Generic CDN bandwidth overage: most CDN/edge platforms have a bandwidth allowance with a per-GB overage rate that only becomes visible after a traffic spike (viral content, DDoS-adjacent scraping) blows past it
- Cloudflare R2 is the notable counter-example: it advertises zero egress fees, which is a specific reason to route large-object or CDN-adjacent storage traffic there when bandwidth cost is the concern, rather than a same-class-but-cheaper object store
- Free-tier cliffs when multiple providers' free tiers are combined: each provider's free tier resets and is tracked independently, so a workload that stays under two separate providers' individual free-tier limits can still produce a surprising combined bill if one tier is exceeded and the overage rate is materially higher than expected — review actual usage against each free tier monthly, don't assume dashboards surface this proactively
- Multi-provider bill sprawl makes anomalies hard to see: a 3x cost spike on one small provider account can be invisible next to a much larger primary bill unless each provider has its own anomaly-aware alert
- A budget alert per provider is table stakes: configure this at account creation, with at least a 50%/90%/100%-of-expected-spend threshold ladder, not just a single end-of-range alert
- Where possible, consolidate into a single dashboard or monthly report (even a manually assembled spreadsheet pulling each provider's billing export) once there are 3+ billed providers — the goal is catching a spike that no single provider's own dashboard would flag as unusual
- Treat egress-heavy architecture decisions (e.g. serving large media/files, cross-region replication, multi-cloud data movement) as a cost-review checkpoint before shipping, not an after-the-fact bill surprise
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- A GitHub Actions workflow references a static access-key/secret-key pair for AWS or a service-account JSON key for GCP where an OIDC role/provider could be used instead
- A repo secret or provider dashboard token has no recorded rotation date or owner
- Multiple services each import a distinct vendor observability SDK (Sentry + Datadog + New Relic) with no shared Collector layer
- No budget/billing alert configured on a provider account that has an active production workload
- The same-looking secret value (or a fragment of it) appears in more than one place in the repo, CI config, or documented runbooks — signals manual copy-paste duplication
- A CI workflow deploying to a static-token provider has no GitHub Environment or protection rule attached
- OTel/telemetry config exports raw headers or full request/response bodies without a redaction step
- A stack description mentions 3+ cloud/PaaS providers with no mention of a consolidated cost review process

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- Default: GitHub Actions OIDC to AWS/GCP/Azure; scoped, rotated static tokens (behind GitHub Environments) for Cloudflare/Fly/Railway/Render/Netlify/Vercel, which don't support OIDC as of 2026
- One OpenTelemetry Collector as the fan-out point; instrument with OTLP, never wire services directly to multiple vendor SDKs
- FinOps is a lightweight monthly practice at this stage (budget alerts + spreadsheet review), not a dedicated tool, until spend or provider count crosses a real threshold
- The 'OIDC everywhere' aspiration has a real, documented gap: roughly half of a typical greenfield stack's providers still require static tokens — this is expected, not a build failure
- Cost guardrails must span providers: NAT gateway/data-transfer fees (AWS), egress (GCP), CDN bandwidth overage (generic), and R2's zero-egress model as the counter-example worth routing large objects through
- Introduce a dedicated secret manager (Vault/Doppler/Infisical) only past 3+ providers or a real compliance driver — not by default at greenfield stage
- Treat a scoped, calendar-rotated machine/deploy token as distinct from a personal access token; infra-security.md's ban is on the latter, not the former
- Never let secrets exist in more than one place without one being derived/sourced from the other; maintain a single registry of what's stored where
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- For every provider in the stack, confirmed: does it support GitHub OIDC? (yes -> federated role/SA; no -> scoped static token + GitHub Environment)
- Every static provider token scoped to its minimum project/zone/team, with owner and issue date recorded
- Rotation calendar set (90-day default) for every static token; immediate-rotation trigger defined for suspected leaks
- GitHub Environments with required reviewers configured on every deploy job that uses a static token
- One OTel Collector deployed; all services instrumented via OTLP pointing at it, not at vendor SDKs directly
- OTel Collector pipeline includes a redaction processor for auth headers/PII before export
- Budget alert (with anomaly detection where available) configured on every provider account, with at least a 50/90/100 threshold ladder
- Combined free-tier usage across providers reviewed monthly, not assumed safe
- Single secret registry maintained (spreadsheet or secret-manager listing) covering what's stored where and last-rotated date
- Decision documented: is a dedicated secret manager or FinOps tool justified yet, per the decision gates, or is native-per-provider still sufficient
