### Uncertain fields

## PaaS selection — Fly.io, Railway, Render, Netlify (plus Hetzner/VPS baseline) for Python services, workers, and Postgres; deploy, secrets, scaling, pricing

### identity

- **name**: PaaS selection — Fly.io, Railway, Render, Netlify (plus Hetzner/VPS baseline) for Python services, workers, and Postgres; deploy, secrets, scaling, pricing
- **item_id**: paas-selection-fly-railway-render-netlify
- **cluster**: platforms
- **short_definition**: Decision framework for where to run Python backend services, background workers, Postgres, and static frontends when Vercel isn't the right fit, comparing Fly.io Machines, Railway, Render, Netlify, and a bare Hetzner VPS baseline on deploy model, secrets, scaling, and pricing.
**scope_in** _What this item covers_:

- Hosting stateless Python API services (FastAPI/Django/Flask-style long-running processes)
- Hosting background workers / queue consumers (Celery, arq, RQ, custom loops)
- Managed or self-hosted Postgres options outside of Supabase
- Scheduled/cron job execution
- WebSocket and other long-lived connection workloads
- Static/JAMstack frontend hosting and edge/serverless functions (Netlify) as a comparator to Vercel
- Bare VPS (Hetzner) as a cost/control baseline for all of the above
- CI/CD deploy mechanics and secrets/identity model for GitHub Actions to each platform
- Pricing structure, free-tier behavior, and cost traps per platform
**scope_out** _Explicit non-goals and adjacent items that own them_:

- Vercel-specific deployment (covered in a separate cluster item)
- Supabase itself as a platform (covered elsewhere; only referenced here as the primary DB)
- AWS/GCP/Azure managed container or serverless services (ECS, Cloud Run, Azure Container Apps) — separate cluster item
- Kubernetes cluster operation (Fly Kubernetes mentioned only as a pricing data point, not a recommended pattern here)
- GPU training workloads (only inference placement is touched in the decision matrix)
- Detailed Dockerfile/build optimization guidance (belongs in deploy-ops reference)
- **priority**: P0

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:

- Default Python API/worker target: Fly.io Machines (per-second billing, scale-to-zero, full Docker control, good for FastAPI + background workers in the same org).
- Default when the team wants near-zero ops and predictable dashboard UX over lowest cost: Render (web service + worker + cron in one render.yaml blueprint).
- Default when the team wants fastest iteration / best DX for small teams and is prepared for usage-based billing risk: Railway.
- Default for the frontend (Next.js) and any purely static/edge-function surface: Netlify or Vercel — not Fly/Railway/Render.
- Default Postgres when not using Supabase and need full control/cost predictability: self-hosted Postgres on a Hetzner VPS, or Fly Managed Postgres for less ops burden.
- Default for cost-sensitive high-bandwidth (video/file serving) workloads: Hetzner VPS or object storage + CDN, not Netlify/Render bandwidth billing.
- Default for scheduled cron jobs tied to the same codebase as the API: Render Cron Jobs or Fly Machines scheduled via flyctl/GitHub Actions cron trigger.
- Treat all platform CLI/API tokens as long-lived secrets requiring the same rotation discipline as cloud IAM keys, since none support OIDC federation.
**decision_gates** _When to use / when not / which alternative (incl. the Supabase, Vercel, and VPS alternatives)_:

- Selection matrix — map each workload class to the best-fit platform:
- {'matrix': [{'workload_class': '1. Stateless Python API service (FastAPI-style)', 'platform': 'Fly.io Machines', 'why': 'Per-second billing, scale-to-zero via min_machines_running=0, full Dockerfile control, multi-region placement near Supabase; Render is a close second for teams wanting less ops (managed TLS, dashboard-first, but always-on billing per instance-hour, not per-second).'}, {'workload_class': '2. Background worker / queue consumer', 'platform': 'Fly.io Machines or Railway service (no public port)', 'why': "Both support a second process/service in the same app/project with no HTTP listener; Fly bills per-second so idle-but-waiting workers between queue pulls are cheaper than Render's always-on worker instance pricing."}, {'workload_class': '3. Postgres hosting when not using Supabase', 'platform': 'Fly Managed Postgres, or self-hosted Postgres on Hetzner VPS', 'why': 'Fly deprecated support/guidance for its old unmanaged Postgres pattern and now pushes Managed Postgres as the supported path; Hetzner is the cost-floor option for teams comfortable running their own backups/HA. Railway and Render both offer managed Postgres add-ons that are fine for small/medium workloads but cost more per GB than Hetzner at scale.'}, {'workload_class': '4. Static/JAMstack frontend', 'platform': 'Netlify (or Vercel)', 'why': "Purpose-built for static builds, previews, and edge functions; none of Fly/Railway/Render/Hetzner match Netlify's DX for this narrow case."}, {'workload_class': '5. Scheduled cron jobs', 'platform': 'Render Cron Jobs, or Fly Machine triggered via GitHub Actions schedule + flyctl', 'why': 'Render has a first-class Cron Job resource type in render.yaml; Fly has no native cron primitive, so cron must be driven externally (GitHub Actions `schedule:` trigger, or a Machine with an in-process scheduler).'}, {'workload_class': '6. WebSocket / long-lived connections', 'platform': 'Fly.io Machines', 'why': 'Machines are long-lived VMs, not request-scoped functions, so WebSockets work natively; avoid Netlify Functions entirely (execution-time-bounded, not designed for persistent connections) and be cautious with Render free tier (spin-down kills idle connections).'}, {'workload_class': '7. GPU/ML inference', 'platform': 'Fly.io GPU Machines (where available/region-limited) or a dedicated GPU provider outside this set', 'why': 'Railway/Render/Netlify do not offer GPU instances; Fly has limited GPU Machine availability by region — for heavy/production ML inference, treat this whole set as insufficient and research a dedicated GPU/inference platform separately [uncertain: Fly GPU region and pricing details not verified in this pass].'}, {'workload_class': '8. Cost-sensitive high-bandwidth workload (video/file serving)', 'platform': 'Hetzner VPS + object storage/CDN', 'why': 'Hetzner advertises high inclusive traffic allowances and is materially cheaper per-GB egress than Fly ($0.02-0.12/GB), Render, or Netlify bandwidth billing; for pure static asset serving, pair with a CDN rather than routing bytes through any of the four PaaS platforms.'}]}
- Gate: if the workload needs true scale-to-zero with per-second billing and Docker-level control, choose Fly over Render/Railway.
- Gate: if the team's top priority is minimizing ops/on-call surface and small pricing overhead is acceptable, choose Render's blueprint model over Fly's more manual Machines model.
- Gate: if the workload is bursty/spiky in resource usage and the team can tolerate usage-based billing volatility for faster iteration, Railway is acceptable; set hard spending limits/alerts before trusting it in production.
- Gate: never place a production long-running Python worker on Netlify Functions — function execution-time and runtime model are not designed for that (JAMstack/edge use case only).
- Gate: if projected monthly compute+bandwidth exceeds roughly one mid-tier Hetzner VPS cost (~€5-20/mo range) 3-5x over, seriously evaluate moving that specific service to a Hetzner VPS + your own orchestration, accepting the added ops burden.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do set min_machines_running=0 and auto_stop_machines on Fly for low-traffic internal APIs to actually get per-second billing benefit; don't leave min_machines_running>=1 by default and expect Fly to be cheap.
- Do scope Fly deploy tokens with `flyctl tokens create deploy -x <TTL>` per app; don't reuse a full personal/org flyctl token in CI.
- Do put all real secrets in `flyctl secrets set` / Railway sealed variables / Render 'sync: false' env vars; don't commit them into fly.toml, railway.json, or render.yaml.
- Do use Render Blueprints (render.yaml) to keep web/worker/cron/db definitions in version control; don't hand-configure services only through the dashboard for anything beyond a quick prototype.
- Do set Railway spending limits/usage alerts before going to production; don't assume Hobby/$5 credit behavior generalizes to real traffic.
- Don't run a stateful Postgres primary as an 'unmanaged Fly Postgres' app for new projects — Fly explicitly says it can't support or guide unmanaged Postgres; use Managed Postgres or move it off Fly.
- Do treat Render's free-tier spin-down (15 min idle, ~1 min cold start) as unacceptable for any user-facing API with a latency SLA; don't put customer-facing endpoints on the free plan.
- Don't route large file/video downloads through Netlify Functions or Render/Railway compute; use object storage + CDN and only orchestrate signed URLs from these platforms.
- Do pin exact Python/runtime versions in nixpacks.toml (Railway), the Render runtime env var, and the Fly Dockerfile base image; don't rely on platform-default 'latest' Python versions across three different platforms and expect parity.
- Do keep worker and web processes as separate services/machines/dynos rather than cramming a background loop into the same process as the HTTP server, on every platform in this set.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:

# ---- fly.toml (Fly.io Machines app: Python FastAPI service) ----
app = "REPLACE_APP_NAME"
primary_region = "iad"

[build]
  dockerfile = "Dockerfile"

[env]
  PORT = "8080"
  ENVIRONMENT = "production"
  # Non-secret config only. Secrets go via `flyctl secrets set`, never in [env].

[http_service]
  internal_port = 8080
  force_https = true
  auto_stop_machines = "suspend"   # or "stop" — controls scale-to-zero behavior
  auto_start_machines = true
  min_machines_running = 0          # 0 = scale to zero; >=1 for latency-sensitive APIs

[[vm]]
  size = "shared-cpu-1x"
  memory = "512mb"

# Secrets (NOT in this file):
#   flyctl secrets set DATABASE_URL=postgres://... SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY=...
#   flyctl secrets set --stage KEY=value   # stage without redeploying machines yet

# ---- railway.json (Railway service config; also see nixpacks.toml) ----
{
  "$schema": "https://railway.com/railway.schema.json",
  "build": {
    "builder": "NIXPACKS"
  },
  "deploy": {
    "startCommand": "uvicorn app.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port $PORT",
    "restartPolicyType": "ON_FAILURE",
    "restartPolicyMaxRetries": 5,
    "numReplicas": 1
  }
}
# Notes:
# - nixpacks.toml can pin Python version and install steps if not using a Dockerfile.
# - Background worker = a second Railway "service" in the same project with a
#   different startCommand (e.g. `python worker.py`) and no public networking/domain.
# - Env vars/secrets are set in the Railway dashboard or via `railway variables set KEY=VALUE`;
#   sealed variables hide values from the UI after creation but cannot be un-sealed or read via CLI.

# ---- render.yaml (Render Blueprint: web service + worker + cron + Postgres) ----
databases:
  - name: REPLACE_DB_NAME
    plan: starter
    postgresMajorVersion: "16"

services:
  - type: web
    name: REPLACE_API_NAME
    runtime: python
    plan: starter
    buildCommand: "pip install -r requirements.txt"
    startCommand: "uvicorn app.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port $PORT"
    envVars:
      - key: DATABASE_URL
        fromDatabase:
          name: REPLACE_DB_NAME
          property: connectionString
      - key: SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY
        sync: false   # marks it a secret set manually in the dashboard, not committed

  - type: worker
    name: REPLACE_WORKER_NAME
    runtime: python
    plan: starter
    buildCommand: "pip install -r requirements.txt"
    startCommand: "python worker.py"

  - type: cron
    name: REPLACE_CRON_NAME
    runtime: python
    schedule: "0 * * * *"
    buildCommand: "pip install -r requirements.txt"
    startCommand: "python run_hourly_job.py"

# ---- GitHub Actions: deploy to Fly.io (static API token, NOT OIDC) ----
name: Fly Deploy
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    concurrency: deploy-group
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: superfly/flyctl-actions/setup-flyctl@master
      - run: flyctl deploy --remote-only
        env:
          FLY_API_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.FLY_API_TOKEN }}
          # FLY_API_TOKEN should be a scoped deploy token:
          #   flyctl tokens create deploy -x 999999h -a REPLACE_APP_NAME
          # NOT a full personal/org token. Rotate periodically; no OIDC alternative exists.

# ---- GitHub Actions: trigger Render deploy (deploy hook URL as secret) ----
name: Render Deploy
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: curl -X POST "${{ secrets.RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL }}"
      # RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL is a secret bearer-style URL, not a scoped OIDC token.

# ---- GitHub Actions: Railway deploy (CLI + project token) ----
name: Railway Deploy
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: npm i -g @railway/cli
      - run: railway up --service REPLACE_SERVICE_NAME
        env:
          RAILWAY_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.RAILWAY_TOKEN }}
          # RAILWAY_TOKEN is a project or account API token, static, GitHub-secret-based.

# ---- OIDC support summary ----
# None of Fly.io, Railway, Render, or Netlify support GitHub Actions OIDC federation
# (no "assume role via OIDC" equivalent to AWS/GCP/Azure). All four require a static
# API token or deploy-hook secret stored as a GitHub Actions secret. This conflicts
# with an "identity-first, no long-lived static keys" policy — the practical mitigation
# is short-lived *scoped* deploy tokens (Fly's `flyctl tokens create deploy -x <TTL>`),
# least-privilege project/service scoping (Railway/Render), secret rotation on a schedule,
# and treating these tokens with the same handling rigor as cloud provider long-lived keys.

**failure_modes** _Top production failure modes and outage classes_:

- Fly Machine configured with min_machines_running=0 for a WebSocket service, causing dropped connections mid-session when the platform decides to scale a machine down.
- Railway usage-based billing surprise: a runaway worker loop or memory leak silently consumes CPU/RAM credits far beyond the Hobby/Pro included credit, producing an unexpectedly large bill.
- Render free-tier web service used for a demo/staging API that customers hit directly; first request after idle takes ~1 minute and looks like an outage.
- Team deploys an 'unmanaged Fly Postgres' cluster for production data with no one owning backups/failover, discovers Fly does not support it operationally when something breaks.
- Long-running Python job or WebSocket handler deployed as a Netlify Function; it gets killed at the function timeout boundary mid-request under real load, unlike in local testing.
- GitHub Actions FLY_API_TOKEN / RAILWAY_TOKEN / RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL leaked via a misconfigured workflow (e.g., printed in logs, or exposed to a fork PR's workflow_run context) because there is no OIDC short-lived-credential fallback.
- Bandwidth-heavy service (file/video serving) hosted directly on Render or Netlify racks up unexpected egress charges because bandwidth was budgeted like a CDN instead of PaaS metered billing.
- Cron job implemented as a Fly Machine with an in-process sleep loop stays billed 24/7 instead of running only for the job duration, erasing the cost advantage over Render's native Cron Job type.
- Sealed variable set on Railway needs to be read back or copied to a new environment; team discovers sealed variables cannot be un-sealed or copied, causing a redeploy scramble.
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Using Netlify Functions as a general-purpose Python backend because 'it's already there for the frontend' — wrong tool for long-running/stateful workloads.
- Running the Postgres primary as an unmanaged Fly Postgres app in 2026 for a new project instead of Managed Postgres or Supabase.
- Treating Railway's included credit ($5/$20) as a real production budget ceiling rather than a small buffer against the per-resource usage rates.
- Putting all secrets in plain (non-sealed, non-`sync: false`, non-flyctl-secrets) environment variables visible in a platform dashboard UI accessible to the whole team.
- Assuming any of Fly/Railway/Render/Netlify support GitHub OIDC role assumption the way AWS/GCP do, and designing a CI pipeline around that assumption.
- Serving large static media (video, big downloads) directly from a Render/Railway/Fly compute instance instead of object storage + CDN, paying compute-tier bandwidth rates for CDN-shaped traffic.
- Deploying background workers to the free tier of any platform and expecting them to run continuously — free tiers are designed around idle-spin-down or credit exhaustion, not always-on workers.
- Hardcoding a single-region Fly Machine for a globally distributed user base and calling it 'edge deployment' without actually placing Machines in multiple regions.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- flyctl (Fly.io CLI) — deploy, secrets, machine management
- superfly/flyctl-actions (GitHub Action) — CI deploy wrapper for Fly
- Railway CLI (@railway/cli) — deploy, variables, shell
- Railway GitHub integration (auto-deploy on push, no CLI required for simple cases)
- render.yaml Blueprints — Render's IaC config format
- Render Deploy Hooks — secret URL-triggered redeploys from any CI
- netlify-cli / Netlify GitHub integration — for the frontend/edge-function side only
- Hetzner Cloud Console / hcloud CLI / Terraform hcloud provider — VPS provisioning
- Terraform or Pulumi providers exist for Fly (community), Render, and Hetzner for IaC-managed infra
- OpenTelemetry SDK / Prometheus exporters — for shipping metrics off-platform since native cross-platform observability is limited
- **versions_as_of**: 2026-08-29
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:

- Fly's unmanaged Postgres pattern is in a support-limbo state: still present in docs but explicitly called out as unsupported for guidance/help — treat as effectively deprecated for new production use in favor of Fly Managed Postgres.
- Railway's pricing/billing terms have shifted (post-paid card requirement, mid-cycle partial charges) — re-check before assuming older Railway pricing blog posts or comparisons are current.
- Netlify's legacy request-count-based Functions billing exists alongside newer credit/GB-Hour billing — confirm which billing model an account is on before estimating costs, as they are not equivalent.
- Watch for further consolidation/renaming of Render's 'compute plans' naming (Starter/Standard/Pro family) which has changed tier names over time.
- Fly GPU Machines availability and pricing is region-limited and evolving quickly; do not treat any GPU figure here as stable.

### security

- **identity_model**: None of Fly.io, Railway, Render, or Netlify support GitHub Actions OIDC federation (no OpenID Connect 'assume role' pattern equivalent to AWS/GCP/Azure) as verified against their current CI docs. Fly.io: GitHub Actions authenticates via a static FLY_API_TOKEN GitHub secret, ideally a scoped deploy token created with `flyctl tokens create deploy -x <TTL>` rather than a full personal/org token; flyctl's own secrets model (`flyctl secrets set`) is separate from CI auth and only manages app runtime env vars via an encrypted vault that injects them into Machines at boot. Railway: CI/CD auth uses a RAILWAY_TOKEN (project or account API token) as a GitHub secret consumed by the Railway CLI, or the built-in GitHub App integration which auto-deploys on push (no explicit token in the user's workflow file, but still a static installed-app credential model, not OIDC); app secrets use env vars, shared variables, and 'sealed' variables that hide values in the UI once set. Render: CI triggers deploys via a Deploy Hook — a secret bearer URL stored as a GitHub secret and hit with a plain curl request; app secrets are plain environment variables or `sync: false` variables set manually per-environment outside the committed render.yaml. Netlify: primarily deploys via its own build system (Git integration) or netlify-cli with a personal/site API token as a GitHub secret; no OIDC federation. Net effect: this whole platform tier is a structural gap against an 'identity-first, no long-lived static keys' policy — the best available mitigation is scoped, short-TTL tokens plus rotation discipline, not true workload identity.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:

- Long-lived FLY_API_TOKEN / RAILWAY_TOKEN / RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL / Netlify token stored as a GitHub Actions secret is a single static credential that, if leaked, can trigger arbitrary deploys or read secrets.
- Workflow misconfiguration exposing secrets to `pull_request_target` or fork-triggered workflows, letting an external contributor exfiltrate deploy tokens.
- Deploy hook URLs (Render) function as bearer credentials with no scoping beyond 'can trigger this one service's deploy' — leakage means anyone can trigger unwanted deploys.
- Overly broad flyctl/Railway/Render account-level tokens used in CI instead of app/project/service-scoped tokens, widening blast radius of a leak.
- Free-tier spin-down (Render) or scale-to-zero (Fly) can be abused for a denial-of-wallet style attack if a public endpoint is hammered to force repeated cold starts / machine starts, spiking cost or degrading availability.
- Bandwidth-based billing (Fly, Railway, Netlify) creates a cost-based DoS vector: an attacker driving high egress traffic can produce large unexpected bills, not just downtime.
- Secrets injected as plain environment variables (Render default, Fly runtime env) are visible to anything with process/dashboard access — insider risk if platform team access isn't scoped.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:

- Use the narrowest-scoped token type each platform offers (Fly deploy token with TTL and app scope; Railway project token; Render deploy hook per-service) — never an account/org-wide token in CI.
- Set an explicit expiry/TTL on Fly deploy tokens and rotate on a schedule (e.g., quarterly) rather than issuing 999999h tokens and forgetting them.
- Restrict which workflows/branches can access deploy secrets (protected branches + required reviewers on workflow file changes; avoid exposing secrets to `pull_request` from forks).
- Store deploy hook URLs and API tokens only as GitHub Actions encrypted secrets, never in repo files, and treat render.yaml `sync: false` / Railway sealed variables / flyctl secrets as the only path for real secrets.
- Set Railway usage/spending alerts and Fly billing alerts to catch cost-based DoS or runaway resource usage early.
- Put a CDN or WAF (e.g., Cloudflare) in front of publicly reachable Fly/Render/Railway/Netlify endpoints to absorb abusive traffic before it reaches metered compute/bandwidth.
- Audit who has dashboard access to each platform (Fly org members, Railway project members, Render team members) with the same rigor as cloud IAM, since dashboard access can read/change env vars.
- Periodically diff and re-verify each platform's current CI-auth documentation for OIDC support, since this is an active area of platform development and could change.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:

- Never hardcode API tokens, deploy hook URLs, or database credentials into fly.toml, railway.json, render.yaml, netlify.toml, or any committed workflow file — always reference `${{ secrets.NAME }}` or platform secret managers.
- When generating a Fly config, default to `auto_stop_machines`/`auto_start_machines` with `min_machines_running = 0` for low-traffic services, and explicitly call out when to raise it for latency-sensitive or WebSocket workloads.
- When generating a GitHub Actions workflow for Fly, use `flyctl tokens create deploy -x <TTL>` guidance in comments and scope the token to a single app; do not suggest using a personal flyctl token.
- Never generate a Netlify Function intended to run a long-lived Python process, WebSocket server, or background worker — flag this as an architecture mismatch and suggest Fly/Railway/Render instead.
- When asked to set up Postgres on Fly, default to recommending Fly Managed Postgres (or Supabase) over the legacy unmanaged Fly Postgres pattern, and note the support gap if the user insists on unmanaged.
- When generating render.yaml, mark any credential-bearing env var with `sync: false` rather than inlining a plausible-looking placeholder value that could be mistaken for a real secret.
- Always state explicitly in generated docs/comments that none of these four platforms support GitHub OIDC — do not imply OIDC is available or silently omit the caveat.
- When cost is a stated constraint and the workload is bandwidth-heavy (file/video serving), recommend Hetzner + object storage/CDN over routing traffic through Fly/Render/Railway/Netlify compute billing.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:

- Require `flyctl deploy --build-only` (or equivalent dry-run/build check) in a PR workflow before merge so build failures surface pre-merge, not on push to main.
- Gate production deploys on GitHub Environments with required reviewers for any workflow that reads a Fly/Railway/Render/Netlify API token or deploy hook secret — these platforms lack OIDC, so the Environment protection rule is the substitute review gate.
- Run `render.yaml` / `railway.json` / `fly.toml` schema validation (or `flyctl config validate`) in CI so a malformed config fails fast instead of failing mid-deploy.
- Block merges on a health-check smoke step post-deploy (curl the service's `/health` endpoint with retries) before marking the deploy job green.
- Pin the deploy CLI version in the workflow (`flyctl`/`railway`/`render-cli`) rather than `@latest`, and bump it deliberately via a reviewed PR.
- Require a passing `pytest`/build step for Python services before the deploy job runs — never let CI deploy on a red test suite.
- For Postgres-hosting choices (Fly Postgres, Render Postgres, Railway Postgres), require a migration-plan step (e.g. `alembic upgrade --sql` dry run) in CI before any migration job runs against production.
- Alert-on-deploy: wire the deploy job's success/failure to the same channel as the cost/budget alerts so a bad deploy and a cost spike are visible in the same place.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:

- Render free tier: cold-spin-down after 15 min idle with ~1 min cold start — never point production or demo-to-customer traffic at a free web service.
- Render free Postgres: 1GB cap and 30-day hard expiry — never store data you can't afford to lose or re-provision.
- Railway usage-based billing: RAM/CPU/network charges accrue beyond the plan's included credit with no hard cap by default — set spending limits/alerts before production traffic.
- Fly per-second billing is cheap only if machines actually stop; `min_machines_running >= 1` on many machines silently reintroduces always-on costs.
- Fly/Railway/Netlify bandwidth is billed per-GB and is not competitive with CDN pricing for high-bandwidth (video/large file) workloads — route that traffic through object storage + CDN instead.
- Netlify Functions credit/GB-Hour billing on newer plans differs fundamentally from legacy per-request billing — verify which billing model an account uses before estimating costs from older blog posts.
- Watch Railway's shift to mandatory post-paid billing and mid-cycle partial charges — budget forecasting based on older prepaid-model assumptions will be wrong.
- Hetzner has no metered-compute cost surprises (flat monthly VPS price) but shifts 100% of scaling/failover/ops cost to the team — factor in engineering time, not just the dollar price, when comparing to managed PaaS.
- Any workload with unauthenticated public endpoints on usage-billed platforms (Fly, Railway, Netlify) is a cost-based DoS surface — put rate limiting or a CDN/WAF in front before going live.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:

- Repo contains a fly.toml, railway.json/railway.toml, render.yaml, or netlify.toml — signals which PaaS(s) are already in use.
- GitHub Actions workflow references FLY_API_TOKEN, RAILWAY_TOKEN, or RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL secrets — signals static-token CI deploy pattern in place.
- Dockerfile present alongside fly.toml — signals Fly Machines Docker-based deploy path.
- Presence of `nixpacks.toml` — signals Railway (or other Nixpacks-based) buildpack deploy rather than Dockerfile.
- requirements.txt or pyproject.toml combined with a `worker.py`/`celery` entrypoint — signals a background worker workload that needs a non-web service definition on whichever platform is chosen.
- Presence of scheduled/cron-style scripts (e.g., `run_hourly_job.py`) without a corresponding render.yaml cron service or GitHub Actions schedule trigger — signals a cron gap to flag.
- Any large media/file assets or video-serving routes implemented as API endpoints rather than pointing at object storage/CDN — signals a bandwidth cost-guardrail violation to flag.
- `.env` files or hardcoded connection strings committed to the repo instead of using platform secrets mechanisms — signals a secrets-hygiene violation regardless of which PaaS is used.

### actionability

**skill_module_bullets** _Bullets that must land in the target references module_:

- For Python API services and background workers outside Vercel, default to Fly.io Machines for cost/control, or Render for lowest ops overhead; never Netlify Functions for long-running or stateful work.
- None of Fly/Railway/Render/Netlify support GitHub OIDC — CI auth is always a static scoped API token or deploy-hook secret; apply key-rotation discipline as if it were a cloud IAM key.
- Fly's unmanaged Postgres is effectively unsupported for new production use — recommend Fly Managed Postgres or Supabase instead.
- Use the selection matrix (workload class -> platform) as the first filter before any deeper platform-specific config generation.
- Treat Render/Railway free tiers and usage-based billing as demo/staging-only unless spending limits and monitoring are explicitly configured.
- Route high-bandwidth static/media traffic through object storage + CDN, and treat Hetzner as the cost-floor baseline for any workload where PaaS pricing looks disproportionate.
- Cron jobs: prefer Render's native Cron Job resource; on Fly, drive cron externally via GitHub Actions schedule or an explicit scheduler, since Fly has no native cron primitive.
- Cross-platform observability requires self-instrumenting with OpenTelemetry and exporting to a third-party backend — no platform here provides a first-party OTel collector.
**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Workload classified against the 8-class selection matrix and a specific platform chosen with a documented reason.
- CI deploy workflow uses a scoped, TTL-limited token (Fly deploy token, Railway project token, Render deploy hook, Netlify site token) stored only as a GitHub Actions secret.
- No secrets committed in fly.toml / railway.json / render.yaml / netlify.toml / workflow files — verified with a secret-scan pass.
- Fly Machines configured with explicit `auto_stop_machines`/`min_machines_running` decision matching the workload's latency/statefulness needs.
- Postgres hosting decision made explicitly (Supabase vs Fly Managed Postgres vs Render/Railway managed vs self-hosted Hetzner) with backup/HA ownership assigned.
- Spending limits or budget alerts configured on Railway/Fly/Render/Netlify before production traffic is routed.
- Free-tier usage (if any) confirmed to be staging/demo-only, never customer-facing.
- High-bandwidth static/media traffic confirmed to be served via object storage + CDN, not through PaaS compute billing.
- Cron/scheduled jobs have a concrete execution mechanism (native Render Cron, or externally triggered Fly job) rather than an always-on sleep loop.
- Observability plan in place (OTel SDK + external backend) since no platform here provides first-party OTel ingestion.

### Uncertain fields

## Vercel plans, pricing units, spend management, hard limits, and the Hobby→Pro→Enterprise cliffs; when to move a workload off Vercel

### identity

- **name**: Vercel plans, pricing units, spend management, hard limits, and the Hobby→Pro→Enterprise cliffs; when to move a workload off Vercel
- **item_id**: vercel-cost-limits
- **cluster**: vercel
- **short_definition**: Vercel bills primarily on consumption of managed-infrastructure resources (Fast Data Transfer, Edge Requests, Function Invocations, Fluid Compute Active CPU + Provisioned Memory, ISR reads/writes, Image Optimization transforms) layered on top of a $0 Hobby / $20-per-seat Pro / custom Enterprise account tier. In a greenfield Next.js 16 + Python services + Supabase stack, Vercel typically hosts the Next.js frontend and light API routes/edge middleware; the cost-guardrail question this item owns is which plan tier to run on, how to budget and cap spend (Spend Management), which usage-based line items cause the most billing surprises, and at what point a workload's shape (long-running batch, high-concurrency background work, heavy egress) makes AWS/GCP/Fly/Railway cheaper and more predictable than Vercel Functions.
- **scope_in**: Hobby vs Pro vs Enterprise plan differences and hard cliffs; Vercel's consumption-based pricing units (Fast Data Transfer, Edge Requests, Function Invocations, Fluid Compute Active CPU, Provisioned Memory GB-hours, ISR reads/writes, Image Optimization transforms); Spend Management (budgets, alert thresholds, auto-pause, webhooks); build minutes/build machine limits; seats and RBAC/billing-role access control; commercial-use restriction on Hobby; decision criteria for moving compute-heavy or long-running workloads off Vercel to AWS/GCP/Fly/Railway.
- **scope_out**: OIDC/WIF federation from Vercel to AWS/GCP for runtime cloud access (see aws-compute-serverless / gcp-cloud-run-functions identity sections); Next.js framework-level deployment config, ISR/ SSR mechanics themselves (see deploy-ops.md); Supabase pricing/limits (separate cluster item); general AWS Fargate/Lambda or GCP Cloud Run pricing (covered in their own items) — this item only supplies the decision gate for when to leave Vercel, not the target platform's own cost model; Vercel Sandbox / v0 pricing (adjacent product, not covered here).
- **priority**: P1

### practice

**defaults** _Opinionated greenfield defaults for 2026 (what to pick without deliberation)_:
- Start every greenfield project on Hobby only for prototyping/personal use; move to Pro before any real user traffic or any commercial use, since Hobby's terms of service prohibit commercial use outright.
- On Pro, enable Spend Management on day one with a conservative budget (e.g. $50-100/mo for an early-stage app) and turn on "pause production deployments" plus email + SMS alerts at 50/75/100% — do not leave it in notify-only mode once real spend is possible.
- Default Fluid Compute (Active CPU billing) is on by default for new functions since 2025; do not opt back into legacy "wall-clock GB-Hours" billing unless a specific workload needs the old always-on concurrency model.
- Set function `maxDuration` explicitly and as low as the workload allows (default 300s cap on Pro without extension) — never leave functions at the platform max in an LLM-streaming or webhook-processing route.
- Serve all raster images through `next/image` with sane `deviceSizes`/`imageSizes` and long cache TTLs to bound Image Optimization transform counts; do not let user-uploaded-image galleries hit the default transform pipeline unbounded.
- Keep the team's Billing/Owner role assignment small (1-2 people) and put everyone else on Member/Viewer so plan and spend-cap changes require a specific role, not general team membership.
- Treat Vercel as the home for the Next.js frontend, edge middleware, and short (<60s) API routes only; route long-running Python jobs, queue consumers, and cron-heavy background work to a dedicated compute platform from day one rather than shoehorning them into Vercel Functions.
**do_dont** _Concrete do and don't lists_:

- Do enable Spend Management with both a budget threshold and auto-pause before you have paying/production traffic on Pro.
- Do restrict the Billing and Owner RBAC roles to a minimal set of people; do not let general Members change plan tier or spend caps.
- Do set explicit `maxDuration` and memory per function in `vercel.json` rather than relying on platform defaults.
- Do route large/unoptimized images through `next/image` with capped `deviceSizes` and long `minimumCacheTTL` to bound Image Optimization costs.
- Do budget for the Spend Management enforcement delay (checks run every few minutes, not instantly) — set your cap meaningfully below your true pain threshold.
- Don't assume Hobby quotas are safe for any project with real or commercial traffic — Hobby is non-commercial only and pauses hard on overage with no purchase path other than upgrading.
- Don't run long-lived background workers, queue consumers, or multi-minute batch/ETL jobs as Vercel Functions — they will either hit duration limits or become the single largest Active CPU + Provisioned Memory line item on the invoice.
- Don't leave webhook signature verification out of a Spend Management pause/notification webhook consumer — validate `x-vercel-signature` before acting on the payload.
- Don't rely on increasing a Spend Management budget mid-cycle to un-pause projects — paused projects must be resumed individually; raising the cap does not auto-resume them.
- Don't stream large media or serve high-bandwidth downloads directly from Vercel Functions/CDN without checking Fast Data Transfer economics against an object-storage + CDN alternative.
**config_skeleton** _Minimal correct config or IaC skeleton with placeholder identifiers only (English comments ok)_:
```jsonc
// vercel.json — cap function region/memory/duration to control cost blast radius
{
  "functions": {
    "api/webhook.ts": {
      "memory": 512,
      "maxDuration": 15,
      "regions": ["iad1"]
    },
    "api/llm-stream.ts": {
      "memory": 1024,
      "maxDuration": 60,
      "regions": ["iad1"]
    }
  }
}
```

```ts
// next.config.ts — bound Image Optimization transform volume/cost
import type { NextConfig } from 'next'

const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  images: {
    // fewer breakpoints = fewer distinct transforms billed per source image
    deviceSizes: [640, 828, 1200, 1920],
    imageSizes: [64, 128, 256],
    minimumCacheTTL: 60 * 60 * 24 * 30, // 30 days, reduces re-transform churn
    remotePatterns: [
      { protocol: 'https', hostname: 'my-app.vercel.app' }
    ]
  }
}

export default nextConfig
```

```text
# Spend Management setup — dashboard steps (not expressible in vercel.json)
# Team: team_example, Project(s): prj_example
# 1. Dashboard -> team_example -> Settings -> Billing -> Spend Management -> Enable
# 2. Set spend amount (USD) below the absolute max you're willing to accept,
#    accounting for the several-minute check-interval enforcement lag.
# 3. Enable "Pause production deployment" toggle; confirm by typing team name.
# 4. Settings -> My Notifications -> Team -> Spend Management ->
#    enable Web + Email at 50/75/100%, enable SMS at 100% (verify phone number).
# 5. Configure webhook URL (must be public HTTPS) to receive 50/75/100% and
#    endOfBillingCycle POST events; verify x-vercel-signature header before acting.
```

```yaml
# GitHub Actions step — flag bundle-size growth and stale preview deployments
# (cost-adjacent: large bundles increase build minutes/time; stale previews
# accumulate against Projects/Deployments-per-day limits)
- name: Check Next.js bundle size budget
  run: |
    pnpm build
    npx next-bundle-analyzer-cli --budget 250kb --entry app/page.tsx || \
      (echo "::warning::bundle exceeds size budget, review before merge" )

- name: Clean up stale preview deployments (weekly cron job, separate workflow)
  run: |
    vercel remove --safe --yes $(vercel list --meta git-branch!=main --scope team_example | awk '{print $1}')
```
**anti_patterns** _Patterns agents must not generate (static keys in CI, public buckets, open security groups, etc.)_:

- Running a long-lived WebSocket server, job queue consumer, or multi-minute ETL/batch process as a Vercel Function instead of a dedicated compute platform.
- Leaving Spend Management disabled (or notify-only, no auto-pause) on a Pro team with production traffic and no other spend cap.
- Granting Owner or Billing RBAC role broadly to all team members instead of restricting who can change plan tier or spend limits.
- Serving large media/downloads directly through Vercel's CDN/Functions instead of object storage + a CDN, driving unnecessary Fast Data Transfer spend.
- Setting `maxDuration` to the platform maximum by default on every function instead of sizing it to the actual workload.
- Assuming a raised Spend Management budget automatically resumes paused projects (it does not — each must be resumed manually).
- Prototyping a project on Hobby with real customer traffic or monetization already live, in violation of the non-commercial fair-use terms.

### tooling

**tools** _Primary CLIs/SDKs/consoles and alternatives_:

- Vercel Dashboard (Usage tab, Billing/Spend Management settings) — primary surface for cost visibility and budget config
- Vercel CLI (`vercel`, `vercel list`, `vercel remove`) — deployment cleanup and inspection scripting
- Vercel REST API (`/v9/projects/{id}/pause`, `/unpause`) — programmatic project pause/resume, useful from a Spend Management webhook handler
- Vercel log drains / Observability Plus — usage and runtime log export for cost attribution
- next/image + next.config.ts image config — primary lever for Image Optimization transform cost control
- Alternative platforms for workloads moved off Vercel: AWS Fargate/Lambda, GCP Cloud Run, Fly.io, Railway
**deprecation_watch** _Services, runtimes, or plans to avoid for greenfield (sunset dates)_:
- Legacy non-Fluid Compute billing (fixed wall-clock GB-Hours regardless of I/O wait) only applies to projects deployed before April 23, 2025 that have not opted into Fluid Compute; do not build new greenfield projects against this old model — Fluid/Active CPU billing is the forward path and generally cheaper for I/O-bound workloads.
- Vercel has changed its pricing/billing model multiple times in recent years (bandwidth to Fast Data Transfer rename plus mechanics change, GB-Hours to Active CPU/Provisioned Memory split, invocation billing moving to per-unit) — do not hardcode any per-unit price into infra code or docs without a review_by date; treat all unit prices in this file as needing quarterly re-verification.
- Avoid architecting around Hobby plan quotas for anything expected to grow — the 30-day quota-exceeded lockout has no self-serve pay-to-unlock path short of a full team upgrade.

### security

- **identity_model**: This item's identity concern is billing/spend role-based access control on Vercel, not cloud OIDC federation (that topic is covered by the AWS/GCP items). Vercel gates plan changes and Spend Management configuration behind the Owner and Billing RBAC roles specifically — Member and Viewer roles cannot alter spend caps, change plan tier, or manage payment details. Keep the Owner role limited to 1-2 accountable people per team; assign a distinct Billing role (rather than Owner) to whoever needs to manage invoices/spend caps without full account control, and audit team membership periodically via the Activity Log, which records spend-amount creation/updates and project pause/unpause events. Do not let CI/CD service tokens or shared team logins carry Owner/Billing privileges — scope automation tokens to the minimum project-level permission needed (e.g. deployment tokens), never account-billing scope.
**threats** _Security threats for this surface_:
- Uncontrolled team membership growth into Owner/Billing roles, letting any team member raise spend caps or disable Spend Management protections.
- A compromised or overly-broad Vercel API token being used to disable Spend Management, remove pause protections, or exfiltrate billing/usage data.
- Bot/scraper traffic driving Edge Requests, Fast Data Transfer, and Function Invocations far past budget before Spend Management's multi-minute check interval reacts.
- A public webhook endpoint configured for Spend Management events being spoofed (fake 100%-threshold payloads) if `x-vercel-signature` verification is skipped, causing false pause/unpause actions or masking a real overage.
- Hobby-tier commercial-use violations exposing a team to account suspension without warning, disrupting production traffic that should never have been on Hobby.
**mitigations** _Required mitigations and guardrails_:
- Restrict Owner and Billing RBAC roles to a small, named set of accountable people; use Member/Viewer for everyone else.
- Store any Vercel API tokens used by automation with least-privilege scopes and rotate them; never embed them in client-side code or public repos.
- Enable Spend Management with both alert notifications and auto-pause from the start of any Pro-tier production usage; set the cap conservatively below the real pain threshold to absorb the multi-minute enforcement lag.
- Verify `x-vercel-signature` on every Spend Management webhook payload before taking any pause/unpause or notification action downstream.
- Put a WAF/bot-protection layer (Vercel Firewall managed rulesets on Enterprise, or a third-party bot-mitigation service on Pro) in front of high-traffic public endpoints to reduce Edge Request/Invocation abuse.
- Move any project with real users off Hobby to Pro proactively rather than waiting for a fair-use violation flag.
**agent_codegen_policy** _Must-generate / must-not for coding agents touching this surface_:
- Must generate `vercel.json` function configs with explicit, workload-appropriate `maxDuration` and `memory` rather than platform defaults, and must default new functions to short durations (<=60s) unless the task specifically requires longer.
- Must not generate code that runs long-lived background loops, queue polling, or multi-minute batch processing inside a Vercel Function — must instead flag this as a candidate for a separate compute platform and scaffold accordingly.
- Must not hardcode any Vercel per-unit price (Active CPU rate, Fast Data Transfer rate, etc.) into application code, comments implying guaranteed pricing, or cost-estimation logic without clearly labeling it as an estimate subject to change.
- Must generate `next/image` config with bounded `deviceSizes`/`imageSizes` arrays for any new project rather than leaving unlimited/default transform surface area.
- Must not grant Owner or Billing team roles to automation-generated invite flows or seed scripts; must use project-scoped tokens for any CI/CD Vercel API usage.
- Should recommend (not silently skip) enabling Spend Management as part of any "go live" or "deploy to production" checklist it generates for a Vercel project.

### quality

**ci_gates** _CI checks and deploy gates (plan/apply review, policy, drift, smoke)_:
- Bundle-size budget check on every PR touching the Next.js app (fails the build or warns above a set threshold) since larger bundles slow builds and, at extremes, affect build-minute consumption.
- Preview deployment cleanup job (scheduled, e.g. weekly) to remove stale branch preview deployments and keep Deployments-per-day and Projects limits from being consumed by dead branches.
- A manual or automated gate before promoting a Hobby project to production traffic, confirming the team has upgraded to Pro (commercial-use compliance).
- A pre-deploy check confirming Spend Management is enabled with auto-pause for any team about to receive a production domain/DNS cutover.
- Lint/config check that no `vercel.json` function entry is missing an explicit `maxDuration`.
**observability** _Logs, metrics, traces, alerts; OTel export path; what to alert on_:
- Vercel Usage dashboard (per-project and per-team) as the primary source of truth for Fast Data Transfer, Edge Requests, Invocations, Active CPU, Provisioned Memory, ISR, and Image Optimization consumption trends.
- Activity Log for Spend Management events (budget creation/update, project pause/unpause) as an audit trail.
- Log drains / Observability Plus exported to an external sink (e.g. Datadog) for cross-referencing spend spikes with specific routes or deploys.
- Alert on: Spend Management 50/75/100% thresholds (web/email/SMS), any unexpected project pause (503 DEPLOYMENT_PAUSED), and week-over-week Fast Data Transfer or Active CPU growth exceeding a set percentage, which usually signals an unoptimized asset, a bot-traffic event, or a regression in caching/ISR settings.
- Correlate Image Optimization transform counts with deploys that touch `next/image` config or add new image sources — this is one of the most common silent-cost-creep vectors.
**cost_guardrails** _Budgets, alerts, egress/NAT/bandwidth traps, spend caps, and the first three cost surprises teams hit_:
- Enable Spend Management on every Pro team from day one; treat auto-pause as the primary hard stop, not just notifications, since notifications alone do not stop spend.
- Set the spend cap meaningfully below your absolute pain threshold to absorb the several-minute enforcement check delay.
- Restrict who can change the spend cap or disable Spend Management to Owner/Billing roles only.
- Track the three most common first-bill surprises: (1) Image Optimization transform volume from unbounded `deviceSizes`/user-uploaded images, (2) ISR read amplification from low `revalidate` windows plus bot/crawler traffic, (3) Fast Data Transfer overage from serving large static/media assets directly instead of via object storage/CDN.
- Watch Fluid Compute's split billing carefully for AI/LLM streaming routes: Provisioned Memory bills for the full instance lifetime (including I/O wait), so a slow-to-stream LLM response can cost more in memory-hours than its Active CPU time suggests.
- Build a monthly reconciliation habit: compare the Usage dashboard's projected on-demand charges against the Spend Management cap and the Pro plan's included usage credit before the billing cycle closes, since Vercel does not auto-throttle Pro/Enterprise usage at 100% by design (only alerts, unless auto-pause is explicitly enabled).
- Mirror the budget/alert pattern used for AWS/GCP items in this same research set (explicit numeric budget + multi-threshold alerting + an automated stop action) as a cross-reference — Vercel's Spend Management is the platform-native equivalent of an AWS Budgets action or a GCP budget alert plus Cloud Function auto-disable, so engineer's cost-guardrail conventions for those clouds should read consistently with this section.
- For workloads that are inherently expensive on Vercel (long-running Python jobs, high-concurrency queue consumers, heavy egress), the cheapest guardrail is architectural: move them off-platform rather than trying to cap their Vercel spend after the fact.
**skill_scan_signals** _Positive/negative signals engineer-scan and check-oss-leaks should flag for this surface (identifier shapes, config smells)_:
- Positive: `vercel.json` present with explicit `functions.*.maxDuration` and `memory` fields set per-route rather than left to defaults.
- Positive: `next.config.ts`/`next.config.js` with a bounded `images.deviceSizes`/`imageSizes` array and a non-trivial `minimumCacheTTL`.
- Positive: presence of a documented Spend Management budget (e.g. referenced in README/runbook) with auto-pause noted as enabled.
- Negative/flag: a `vercel.json` function config with `maxDuration` at or near the account's maximum (800s/1800s) without clear justification in comments — likely a long-running job that doesn't belong on Vercel Functions.
- Negative/flag: cron-triggered Vercel Functions performing what looks like ETL/batch processing (large loops, external DB bulk writes, file processing) — candidate for migration to a dedicated worker.
- Negative/flag: hardcoded Vercel per-unit prices embedded in application cost-calculation code (a sign the price will silently drift out of date).
- Negative/flag: team invite/IaC scripts assigning Owner or Billing role broadly instead of Member/Viewer by default.
- Negative/flag: Python service directories deployed as Vercel Functions containing worker/queue-consumer patterns (long polling loops, `while True`) rather than request/response handlers.

### actionability

**checklist** _Pre-ship checklist for this surface_:

- Confirm the project is on Pro (not Hobby) before any commercial use or real user traffic goes live.
- Enable Spend Management with a defined budget, 50/75/100% alerts, and auto-pause on production deployments.
- Verify Owner/Billing RBAC roles are restricted to the intended accountable team members only.
- Set explicit `maxDuration`/`memory` in `vercel.json` for every function; flag any function near the account's max duration for review.
- Confirm `next.config.ts` bounds `images.deviceSizes`/`imageSizes` and sets a meaningful `minimumCacheTTL`.
- Identify any long-running, high-concurrency, or heavy-egress workloads in the codebase and confirm they are NOT deployed as Vercel Functions.
- If a Spend Management webhook is configured, confirm `x-vercel-signature` verification is implemented before the payload is acted on.
- Review the Usage dashboard against the Spend Management cap and Pro included-usage credit at least monthly.
